The Bell That Counts to Nine

The Bell That Counts to Nine

Prologue The winter bread

The sack broke against a nail in the landing, and Runa put her foot over the hole.

Grain ran into her shoe. She could feel it slipping between her toes, hard and cold through the worn stocking. The man who carried the other end of the sack shouted something in a language she did not know. When she tried to lift her end he shouted again.

“Keep still,” her mother said. “He means keep still.”

“I am keeping still.”

“You are telling me about it. Just do it.”

Svanhild got her cloak under the split. There was a little barley on the wet planks already. A boy bent to gather it and the carrier caught his collar as a swell lifted the boat beneath the landing. The plank between boat and shore slewed sideways. Runa watched a row of pale fingers close on its edge, then disappear as the man below pulled himself upright.

“Not there,” her mother told the boy. “Beside me. Give me the corners.”

They tipped the damaged sack into the cloak. The man from the boat made a low, apologetic sound, quite unlike his shouting. His beard was crusted with salt. One of his eyes would not open properly.

Svanhild answered him in the southern speech. Runa knew the word for thanks because the man had been saying it all morning to people who took sacks away from him. It seemed an odd arrangement. Ingrith’s steward thanked nobody, although he was receiving more than anyone else.

The boat had come in after daylight, too late for the tide the pilots wanted. People on the higher path had seen it waiting beyond the skerries: a dark hull rising out of grey water, then water over the place it had been. Hervi’s father had gone out in the smaller boat with two of his sons. From shore they seemed to spend a long time going the wrong way. Runa’s mother had taken her wrist when she asked about that, and held it until the boats came between the stones.

Now the grain ship lay at the outer end of the landing. Its master had a bandage round his chin and wanted to leave before the wind changed. Men carried sacks without quite straightening their knees. When someone offered them hot ale, they drank where they stood.

Runa and the boy gathered the cloak’s corners. Her mother lifted the heaviest part and they brought their load to the shed. Inside, the smell of dry grain was so strong that Runa swallowed.

A priest sat on an upturned basket with a board across his knees. He had put a cloth over his book except for the few lines on which he was writing. Water dripped from the shed roof onto his left shoulder. Each time he dipped his pen he moved the inkpot away from the drip; each time someone passed behind him he moved it back.

“Another sack,” said Svanhild.

He looked at the cloak, then at the split cloth trailing from it. “Part of another sack.”

“There was a little lost.”

“How much?”

“You may go outside and count it.”

He raised his face. Runa expected anger. Instead he rubbed his eyes with the wrist that held the pen, leaving a black mark against his temple.

“Put it on the scale first.”

They weighed it, cloak and all, and then weighed Svanhild’s spare cloak to allow for the cloth. The two cloaks were not the same weight. Runa said so. Her mother said she knew. The priest asked which was heavier, and Runa brought him the lighter one so he could feel how thin it was.

He made another mark.

Svanhild stood before him while men edged past with their sacks. She was forty that winter, broad through the shoulders, with a dark braid which she had cut shorter after it caught in a door hinge. It still annoyed her when anyone remembered. She usually made people move round her; now she kept stepping aside.

“There is a woman up by the old drying racks,” she said. “Her husband was on the boat that went down in the summer. She has two small children and his mother.”

“The steward has the household distribution.”

“He does.”

The priest waited.

“He has her under her husband’s father’s house. That house is empty. The old man died before his son.”

“What is her name?”

Svanhild told him. He asked her to say it again. She did, more slowly, and he tried the sounds under his breath before writing. Then he pointed at an entry farther up the page.

“Same woman?”

“No. That is his sister.”

He drew his pen back from the paper. Runa watched him wait for the ink on its tip to settle.

“Her name again, please.”

This time Svanhild leaned down and said it close to him. She did not make a joke about his hearing. Outside, a man began calling for a rope.

The priest wrote the name and a number beside it. Then he tore a narrow piece from the edge of a spoiled sheet and copied something onto that.

“Give this to the man at the scale.”

Svanhild took the scrap. “And the family with the twins.”

“Which family?”

“Their house burned. They sleep in three places now. No one has counted them at all.”

The priest laid his pen down.

Runa looked at the dampness spreading on his shoulder. He was younger than she had first thought. There were sore-looking places where the wind had split his lip.

Her mother began giving him the names.

They carried one sack uphill between them and returned for the other. Runa’s shoe made a grinding sound. When they came to the widow’s door, her mother told her to stamp her feet outside. She did, and a little barley fell from the loose stitching onto the step.

The widow crouched and picked it up.

Runa wished her mother would say something, but Svanhild was helping the old woman move a chest so they could set the sack against the inside wall. She asked where the roof leaked. The widow showed her two places. One child came to the sack and put both hands on it, as if feeling whether anything inside was alive.

On the way home Runa finally took off her shoe. She sat on a stone with her foot drawn up under her skirt while Svanhild shook the grain into her own palm.

“You might have said.”

“I thought it would get less.”

“How?”

“I don’t know.”

Her mother tipped the grain into the pocket where she kept scraps of cord. She knelt to pull Runa’s stocking straight. The heel had worn through. Her thumb touched the exposed skin, and Runa pulled away because it tickled.

“Hold still, then.”

“You keep saying that.”

“It keeps being useful.”

At the hall that evening the men from the ship sat close to the fire. The one with the bad eye had fallen asleep over a bowl. Nobody woke him when the benches were moved.

Svanhild stood in the space cleared beside the hearth. She had washed her face and put on the heavy silver pin that always pulled her neckband crooked. Runa sat where she could see both her mother and the visitors. She liked knowing what came before the first word: the settling of her mother’s weight, the breath taken while everyone else still talked, the little glance towards a person who would understand the first joke.

She began with Hervi’s father going out past the stones.

The pilots disagreed at once about which stone she meant. Svanhild allowed them a moment, then gave the stone a name so disgraceful that one of the men from the ship laughed before anyone translated. The hall quietened to hear where she would take it.

Runa forgot her cold feet. She watched the southern master understand that the bent sail in her mother’s hands was his sail, and the hand pressed to the chin was his bandage. He began shaking his head, smiling. When she reached the place where the grain came ashore, people called out the names of the men who had carried it. Ingrith’s welcome received its proper place. So did the pilot boat, and the broken oar, and the boy who had nearly gone between landing and ship.

Svanhild did not tell about the widow’s step.

Runa waited through the applause, which was prolonged by a request for the disgraceful stone again. Her mother obliged, giving the stone a wife. By the time she came to sit down she was hot and thirsty. Runa passed her a cup.

“You left her out,” she said.

“Who?”

“The woman we took the barley to.”

Svanhild drank. “We took barley to two women.”

“The one with the old mother.”

“She has her barley.”

“I know.”

“Then why are you looking at me like that?”

Runa looked down. Her mother put the cup between her palms and turned it slowly, warming one hand and then the other.

“There was no place for everyone in that telling,” she said. “I would still be standing.”

“You put the stone’s wife in.”

Svanhild laughed, then saw that Runa was not joking. She touched the girl’s knee beneath the table.

“Next time you may stand beside me and remind me of what I have left out.”

Runa would once have wanted that more than anything. She had imagined knowing all the beginnings and never having to ask which dead man belonged to which house. Tonight she could still see the priest bending over the book. He had needed her mother to give him the woman’s name three times. When they came back for the second sack, the first name was still there.

“Will you let me go to their school?” she asked.

Her mother glanced towards the visitors.

“To learn the letters?”

“And the reckoning.”

“You can reckon.”

“More of it.”

Svanhild set down the cup. Across the room someone was asking her to settle how many sons a long-dead skipper had possessed. She held up a finger without looking at him.

“You will have to get up early.”

“I do.”

“You get up when I have called you three times.”

“I’ll get up.”

“And you’ll still have work at home.”

“Yes.”

Her mother examined her face. Runa kept it still, afraid that an expression might make the answer change.

“Then we had better find you something to carry a slate in.”

Later, at home, Svanhild emptied a small bag onto the bed. Out came a bone comb, two river stones, a broken clasp and a piece of red cloth Runa had been forbidden to cut. Her mother shook the bag hard. Something remained caught in a seam.

Runa reached in and brought out a fishhook.

“Not that one,” Svanhild said. “It’ll catch your papers.”

They searched again. The second bag had a sound lining but a short strap. Svanhild measured it across Runa’s body, made her turn, and found a length of old belt in the chest.

Runa held the lamp while her mother sewed. When her arm began to shake, Svanhild drew up a stool and put the lamp on it.

“Go and bring me the good needle. No, the other good needle. The one I told you not to use.”

Runa brought it.

Part One The people in the valley

Chapter 1 The landing she remembered

The pilot was late, and Runa had already told her daughter twice that lateness was not a judgment on their return.

“I didn’t say it was.” Eydis stood with one hand hooked through the lashings of a wool bale. Her travelling hood had fallen back. The wind pushed her hair into her mouth as fast as she pulled it out. “I said I could see a boat.”

“Where?”

“There. Behind the low thing.”

“The skerry.”

“I know what a skerry is.”

Runa followed the pointing hand. Something small appeared in a hollow between waves. It was too far away for her to distinguish an oar from a strip of foam. She wished she had not said skerry in that correcting voice. The last two days aboard had left her with a headache and a tenderness behind her teeth. Every question sounded repeated.

Brother Petros lowered himself onto the bale beside Eydis.

“If that is him,” he said, “I hope he knows we are waiting.”

“Everyone here knows,” said the passenger in the fur cap.

He had been telling people what everyone here knew since they passed the headland. Runa had first mistaken him for a neighbour grown broad with age, then realized he belonged to a house farther along the coast and had only visited Varnvik once. He was bringing iron cooking pots. Whenever the hull struck a wave, they clanged beneath the deck.

The master stood in the bow, watching the gap. Runa had looked at his route book that morning. She could read the southern abbreviations more easily than he could, and had helped him distinguish a water depth from a marginal note about timber dues. In gratitude he had shown her the old approach, traced in brown ink between two islands that looked much farther apart on paper.

Now he would not take it.

“The summer storm moved the bed,” he had said.

“Your draught is under the marked depth.”

“Yes.”

She had waited for him to explain further. He had shut the book, carefully, with a scrap of wool marking the page.

There was another boat now. Or the same boat emerging farther to the left. Eydis climbed onto the bale and Runa caught the back of her coat.

“Stay down.”

“I can see better.”

“So can I when you are not in front of me.”

Eydis got down, offended. Petros moved his feet to give her room.

They had come north with the last ordinary cargoes before the hard weather. The ship carried meal and iron, lamp oil in stoppered jars, three bundles of worked leather, and a cockerel whose owner had assured everyone it would sleep under the cloth. It had not. At dawn it had begun calling from beneath Runa’s berth, with the peculiar satisfaction of a creature that knew exactly where it belonged.

Her daughter had laughed until she cried. Runa, who had been awake most of the night, had nearly done the other thing.

The fur-capped passenger wanted to know why they could not go to the lee cove.

“Because this is a cargo ship,” said the master, without turning.

“It has water in it, hasn’t it?”

Nobody answered. The pots knocked together below them.

At last the pilot boat came close enough for Runa to recognize Hervi by the way he rowed: short strokes, elbows out, an apparent reluctance to extend himself which had annoyed his father when they were young. His hair was grey now. He looked up at the rail, saw her, and grinned.

“Runa. You grew a child.”

“She did most of it herself.”

“How old?”

“Twelve,” Eydis said.

“Hers, I meant.” He nodded towards Runa.

Eydis laughed with surprise. Runa smiled despite herself.

Hervi came aboard over the side. He spoke first to the master, quietly, pointing twice towards the opening beyond the nearest skerry. Then he spat over the rail, rubbed his hands and told them to shift the wool aft.

Petros rose to help. Hervi stopped him.

“Not you, long man. You’ll move more than the wool.”

The engineer looked down at himself. He was tall and thin, and had spent the voyage trying to make his knees fit in places meant for other people.

“I can carry.”

“Stay where the master puts you. You can carry ashore.”

They entered slowly. Runa stood beside her daughter while Hervi called instructions in a voice she had never heard from him as a boy. He waited through a small wave, then used the next one. A patch of pale water slid past within a boat’s width. The fur-capped man opened his mouth. Hervi lifted a hand without looking at him and the man closed it.

The shore came apart into familiar pieces. The black roof of the drying shed. Three stones at the turn of the footpath, only two of which she could have named. Above them the hall, longer than she remembered because an extension had been built against its western end. The chapel tower stood on the knoll to the east. It had once seemed absurdly high to her. Now the bell opening looked small against the hill beyond it.

She searched for her mother’s roof. A new shed obscured the lower part of the house. There was smoke from the chimney, and something pale spread over the fence.

Sheets, she thought. Her mother was drying sheets for them.

A boy on the landing shouted their arrival uphill. Two dogs ran down before any people did. One had three legs and reached the edge first.

Svanhild came at a walk.

Runa had written twice to say when they might arrive, and once to say the first date was no longer possible. She had pictured her mother waiting indoors, determined to make her come all the way to the door. Instead Svanhild walked past the dogs and stopped where the landing widened, her boots planted apart. Her braid lay over the shoulder of a red-brown coat Runa had never seen.

Eydis was already waving. Svanhild waved back with her whole arm.

The boat touched. Men brought lines across. Runa took her work bag and turned to find Petros holding out her other bundle.

“You have a family to meet,” he said.

“So have you. For the next several months.”

“That sounds like a warning.”

“Keep your papers dry.”

Her mother embraced Eydis first because Eydis got to her first. The girl disappeared into the coat, then came out with her hood straightened and her hair pulled from her mouth. Svanhild held her shoulders at arm’s length.

“Let me see. Turn round.”

“Mother,” Runa said.

“I am looking at my granddaughter.”

“She is not a boat.”

“Too narrow in the beam for a boat.”

Eydis turned willingly. She liked being looked at when the looking came with affection. Runa remembered that from visits farther south, when Svanhild had arrived with presents that would not fit and refused to admit she had bought them for a younger child.

Then her mother took her.

The embrace was hard enough to press the buckle of the work bag against Runa’s ribs. She had thought about what she would say first. Something about the crossing, perhaps, or the house. She found herself holding the back of her mother’s coat and saying nothing at all.

“You are thin,” Svanhild said into her hair.

“I am exactly the weight I was when you saw me.”

“Then you were thin then.”

They separated. Svanhild kept a hand on her sleeve.

“What have you paid for this?”

“The coat?”

“No, the child. Of course the coat.”

“It was part of my allowance.”

“A fine allowance.” She rubbed the cloth between finger and thumb. “Does it turn rain?”

“Most rain.”

“We have the rest.”

Petros introduced himself. His Linn speech was deliberate and carefully placed, each word arriving before he seemed wholly certain of the next. Svanhild listened without finishing anything for him. When he named the office that had sent him, she replied in the southern language.

He smiled with relief.

Runa disliked the relief. She had been translating his conversations for a week. Her mother had managed in one sentence to turn that effort into something he was glad to be spared.

“You’ll eat with us,” Svanhild told him.

“I must see the chapel first.”

“Afterward, then.”

“I may be late.”

“I have fed people late before.”

A woman carrying a basket stopped to ask Runa whether Eydis ate fish. Before Runa could answer, the woman bent towards the girl and asked her directly.

“Yes,” Eydis said.

“Good. Your mother used to make work of the bones.”

“I was little.”

“We all were, at some point.”

The woman moved on. Someone else wanted to know whether Runa had remarried. Someone wanted to know whether the school in the south was as strict as people said. The fur-capped passenger passed them with one pot under each arm, furious that he could not find a porter. Nobody asked where the road would go.

Runa tried to help with the baggage, but Hervi had already engaged two boys. Svanhild gave them instructions about which door to use. Eydis followed the dogs up the path until Runa called her back from a patch of dark mud.

“She knows how to walk,” her mother said.

“She doesn’t know how to get mud out of the only decent boots she brought.”

“I have soap.”

At the first rise Runa stopped. The landing lay below them, its new boards pale against older wood. Torve’s yard stood beyond, under a shed roof supported by crooked posts. A half-built boat sat upright on trestles. Someone inside it was striking a tool with a wooden mallet, a quick double tap and a pause.

The gap between yard and chapel had narrowed. A row of small sheds occupied ground where people once spread nets. Runa pointed.

“When did those go up?”

“Which?”

“By the chapel path.”

“Oh. Years.”

“They weren’t here last time.”

“Then fewer years.”

Runa looked at her. Svanhild smiled, taking the question as affectionate complaint, and perhaps it had been. Runa was no longer sure. She had visited for short stays, summer days and one hurried burial, but had not lived in the house since she went south. She was thirty-four. Her mother still knew where she ought to put her feet.

The bell began while they were crossing the yard.

Runa reached for the clasp on her work bag. Petros had asked her to meet the local clerk at the afternoon hour, and she had said she would. Her papers were inside, wrapped in oilcloth, with the letter naming her authority placed on top. She was halfway through the buckle when Svanhild opened the door.

“Take your things off first.”

“I said I’d be at the chapel.”

“They can see the boat from there. They know you’ve arrived.”

The room smelled of onions cooking in fish fat, and warm wool, and the faint sharpness of ash dampened too soon. Runa’s old chest stood beneath the window. A new cover lay over it, red along the edge. Her mother had set a cup on the table in front of the place where Runa used to sit.

Eydis went straight to the hearth. She crouched, held out her hands, and began telling Svanhild about the cockerel.

Runa let the bag hang from her shoulder until the ninth stroke had faded. Then she unfastened the coat.

The hook beside the door was still loose. She lifted it to make sure it would bear the weight, exactly as she had done when she was sixteen.

Chapter 2 The room above the smoke

Her mother had kept the bed and changed everything around it.

Runa discovered this gradually. The shelf where she expected to put her comb held a box of hooks. The chest she had shared with her mother’s spare clothes contained Eydis’s bedding, folded in a way that suggested Svanhild had done it several times. Her old sleeping place was now a store for things that could not bear damp. They were to use the little room above the kitchen, which had a low roof and a stair so steep that Runa stopped halfway up to pass her bag ahead.

“It was finished last summer,” Svanhild said below her. “Mind your head.”

Runa struck it.

“I said mind.”

“I heard.”

Eydis took the bag and backed away, grinning. The room had a shuttered opening, two narrow beds and a strip of worn sailcloth on the floor. A small shelf held a jug and basin. Through a gap beside the chimney came the smell of dinner.

Runa put a hand on the wall. It was warm.

“You’ll have to open the shutter at night,” her mother said. “Otherwise the smoke settles.”

“The shutter doesn’t close properly.”

“Then you’ll be saved the trouble.”

Svanhild came up with the second bundle and set it on the bed by the chimney. She looked round, seeing the room through them now. Runa knew the look and wished she had praised something before inspecting the shutter.

“It is good and warm,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Thank you.”

Her mother pulled at the corner of a blanket. “That one is for Eydis. You’ll want the wider bed.”

“I wanted the chimney side,” Eydis said.

“You can have it.” Runa put her bag on the other bed.

“I’ve put the softer mattress there.”

“Mother, we can exchange the mattresses.”

Svanhild stopped pulling the blanket. For a moment Runa thought she had offended her. Then her mother snorted.

“You may also sleep upside down, if it pleases you. Dinner soon.”

She went down. Eydis opened the shutter and peered out.

“I can see the tower.”

“You could see it from the road.”

“From bed, I mean.”

Runa unwrapped the papers, checked that no water had reached the inner fold, and put them in a row. She was tired enough to want to put everything away at once. Knowing where a thing was made her less tired. Eydis undid her own bundle, took out a clean neckcloth, a book and a wooden bird with one wing missing, and arranged them on the shelf as if she had been living there for months.

“Don’t put the book under the jug.”

“It isn’t under it.”

“It will be when someone moves the jug.”

Eydis moved the book to the foot of her bed. Runa watched the girl take off her wet boots. One stocking had slipped down round her heel. Her feet were larger than they had been at midsummer; soon the boots would no longer fit at all. Runa had meant to ask the leather worker about another pair before they sailed.

“Tomorrow we’ll see about your boots.”

“What’s wrong with them?”

“Nothing yet.”

“Then why—”

“Your grandmother is waiting.”

They went down in their stockings. Runa found her travelling coat spread close to the fire, too close, and moved it back.

“Leave it,” Svanhild said.

“The lining draws if it gets hot.”

“It won’t get hot there.”

“It has before.”

Her mother turned a fish in the pan. “Then you have bought a foolish coat.”

“The office bought it.”

“An educated foolish coat.”

Eydis laughed. Runa hung the coat over a chair and did not reply.

There were two other people at the table. The older woman was Oddrun, whom Runa recognized by her eyes before the rest of her face; she had always looked as though she was allowing someone enough time to make a fool of himself. The boy beside her was new. He was tall for his age and had a scratch along one cheek. He stood when Runa came in, then sat too quickly after Oddrun tugged his sleeve.

“This is Solli,” Svanhild said. “He eats here on workdays.”

“And other days,” said Oddrun.

“If someone has been working,” the boy said.

“He’ll tell you what counts as work if you give him an opening.” Svanhild put the pan down. “Sit.”

The meal was fish, onions and barley mashed with broth. There was pickled cabbage in a cracked bowl and bread that resisted Runa’s knife. Eydis took more fish than she could finish. Solli waited to see whether she would leave any, then looked away when Runa noticed.

“Have another,” Svanhild told him.

“I’m well.”

“You can be well with another fish.”

He took it.

Oddrun asked about Runa’s work. She listened to the first explanation, which concerned surveying and the preparation of estimates, and then asked what Runa would actually do tomorrow.

“Visit the landing, probably. Speak to the households along the line. Find out what they need moved and when.”

“That sounds more like it.”

“The estimate has to come from somewhere.”

“So does the fish.” Oddrun lifted a bone from her mouth. “Does the long priest know where?”

“He’s an engineer.”

“Can engineers eat fish?”

Svanhild laughed, and Runa had to laugh with her. The barley was better than anything they had eaten aboard. When she went to serve herself again, her mother pushed the bowl closer without interrupting the conversation.

Eydis asked Solli about the three-legged dog. He said it belonged to nobody. Oddrun said it belonged to everyone who claimed it had eaten at someone else’s house. Svanhild told a story about the dog’s theft of a whole smoked fish from a wedding table. In her version the theft occurred at the wedding of a woman who had married six summers earlier.

“Five,” Runa said.

Her mother looked at her.

“She married the summer before Eydis began school. That was five.”

“Did the fish taste different?”

“No.”

“Then let the dog have it.”

Everyone laughed except Eydis, who was watching her mother. Runa ate another spoonful of barley. She did not know why she had corrected the date. It was an ordinary thing to know, and she had known it. Svanhild resumed with the dog going under the bride’s skirt, and after a moment the room was easy again.

After Oddrun took Solli out to fetch the last basket, Eydis carried her bowl to the shelf and asked where she should put the fish bones. Svanhild showed her. The girl went back to the table to collect everybody else’s, doing it in the brisk manner she used when she had decided to be useful without being asked.

Runa let her. She sat a moment longer with the last warmth of the bowl between her hands.

Her mother had added a shelf by the hearth. On it stood a little stone lamp Runa did not recognize, a box with a broken clasp, and the cup she remembered her mother refusing to throw away after it cracked. The crack was darker now. Something had been rubbed into it to keep it from leaking.

“Who made the shelf?”

“Solli helped me.”

“Is it straight?”

“Enough for cups.”

Runa almost said she had only asked. Her mother had only answered. They looked at the shelf together, and the small difference between its ends grew so obvious that both began to smile.

“I wanted it higher,” Svanhild said. “He said I would complain every time I reached.”

“He was right.”

“He is often right about things that are none of his business.”

Eydis came back with the bones. Svanhild directed her outside with the covered vessel, warning her about the dog. The door closed. Runa listened to her daughter’s steps and to the immediate coaxing voice with which she greeted the animal she had been warned against feeding.

“She is happy here,” Svanhild said.

“She arrived an hour ago.”

“That doesn’t prevent happiness.”

“No.”

Runa set down the bowl. She was tired enough to tell the truth without arranging it first.

“I was afraid she would hate the journey and blame the place.”

“She can hate the journey. So can you.”

“I wanted to come.”

“I know.”

Outside Eydis said no to the dog, then said it more firmly. Svanhild listened with visible pleasure. Runa was glad to sit where somebody else might get up first if the girl needed help.

“Your letters made the room sound larger,” she said.

“It seemed larger before I put two beds in it.”

“You could have told me.”

“And have you take a room at the chapel?”

Runa looked at her. Svanhild reached for the washing cloth and began wiping the table.

“I wanted you in the house.”

“We’re in it.”

“Yes.”

There was nothing useful to add. Runa took the cloth from her and wiped the other end. Beneath the table a familiar groove caught the pad of her thumb, a mark she had made with a knife when she was much younger. She had expected her mother to have sanded it out years ago. She touched it once, then went on wiping.

Eydis returned with the vessel and a triumphant account of the dog having obeyed her. A smear on its lid suggested a less complete victory. Svanhild inspected it, raised an eyebrow and said the dog must be improving. The girl understood enough to laugh without surrendering her account.

The three of them cleared the table. Runa carried the basin; Svanhild poured; Eydis dried the first cup so eagerly that it slipped and struck the edge of the board. Nobody spoke until they had checked for a new crack. There was none. The girl set it safely on the crooked shelf and breathed out.

Petros arrived while they were washing the bowls. He apologized, first for lateness and then for his boots. Svanhild made him sit at the place nearest the fire. The portion she had put aside was bigger than any she had served to the rest of them.

“The chapel roof leaks,” he told Runa.

“Which part?”

“The part above the table.”

“Move the table.”

“There is a second leak above the other place.”

Oddrun, putting on her cloak, advised him to move the roof. Petros took a moment to understand, then laughed. His laugh was unexpectedly loud in the room. Eydis looked up, pleased.

Runa offered to walk back with him. Her mother glanced towards the dark window.

“You’ve only just arrived.”

“I said I would go.”

“Then go.”

The words were neutral. Runa put on her coat, which was still damp inside, and went.

Outside, the cold had tightened. Small puddles along the path had begun to take a skin. Petros carried a lantern low so they could see the stones. He was quiet until they passed the boatyard.

“I am grateful for the dinner,” he said.

“She likes feeding people.”

“I noticed.”

Runa could not tell whether he meant the size of the portion or something else. He stopped to adjust the lantern handle.

“Would you rather work tomorrow?”

“No. I want to see the room.”

He nodded and led on.

The school occupied the lower part of the chapel’s newer wing. There were pegs for cloaks, a bench with little hollows worn in it, and a table on which someone had left a bowl full of pebbles sorted by size. A pair of child’s mittens dried on a rail. Runa stopped beside them. The smaller mitten had been patched with cloth from a blue adult sleeve.

A woman came from the inner room carrying a stack of slates. She had a firm, square face and a thin silver chain disappearing into the neck of her dress. Runa knew her at once, although she had not thought of her for years.

“Astri.”

The woman put the slates down before embracing her. She smelled faintly of chalk dust and wool warmed by a fire.

“You’ve come with the road.”

“So people keep telling me.”

“And a daughter.”

“People tell me that too.”

Astri stepped back. “Does she answer as fast as you did?”

“Faster.”

“Poor child.”

Runa smiled. They had sat on the same bench during the chapel’s first winter of lessons. Astri had been slow with numbers and quick with reading. Runa remembered helping her with a subtraction. She was about to say so when Astri added, “Do you remember turning my slate over before I had finished?”

Runa did.

“You said I could do it without the counters.”

“I thought you could.”

“Yes. I particularly remember that part.”

Petros had moved to the back of the room to inspect something by the wall, giving them privacy or pretending to. Runa rubbed the seam of her glove.

“I was a tiresome girl.”

“You were a girl. I bit someone the following summer.”

“Who?”

“My brother. He continues to deserve it.”

They laughed. Astri showed her the school cupboard and the survey room beyond it. The inner space had been cleared for a table, two stools and a rack for drawings. A bucket stood exactly where Petros’s elbow would rest if he tried to write. He had placed his instruments along the driest wall in a careful order: measuring rods, a level in its case, a reel of cord and a small brass tool Runa had only seen him use once.

Astri warned them that the children needed the room twice a week until a bench could be mended.

“Of course,” Runa said.

“I mean they need it with the floor clear. Not just a space for their feet.”

“We can put the rods up.”

“And the sharp thing.”

Petros looked at the brass tool. “It isn’t sharp.”

“They’ll find a way.”

Astri left them a little oil for the lamp. Runa and Petros moved the table, discovered a place where the roof’s second drip narrowly missed it, and arranged the stools so neither person would sit directly beneath a wet beam. Petros opened the box of papers he had carried from the ship. Its wooden lid had swollen; he worked at the fastening patiently until it came free.

Runa spread her appointment letter before him. They checked the limits of the emergency allowance, the promised local labour and the dates on which the regional office expected reports. None of the dates allowed much time for weather. Petros drew a line through the first one.

“We can tell them why,” he said.

“The messenger will already have gone.”

“Then we can tell the next one.”

She liked him better for that.

By the time she got home, the lower window was dark. She opened the door carefully, not wanting to wake anyone, and heard her mother’s voice from the hearth.

“Again. Don’t hurry after the turn. You’ve brought the ship round; let us see it.”

Eydis repeated a line. Runa recognized the tune before the words. It was one Svanhild had used when Runa was small, beating the rhythm on the side of the bread trough while she waited for the dough. Eydis’s version was thin and uncertain, the pitch rising where she feared she might forget.

Her grandmother answered with the next line, low enough that the girl had to listen.

Runa stood just inside the doorway. Her mother sat with a stocking drawn over one hand. Eydis knelt beside her, chin on the chair’s arm. The lamp lit the side of the girl’s face and one bright thread of Svanhild’s needle. Neither looked up.

Eydis tried again. This time she waited in the right place.

“Yes,” Svanhild said. “There.”

Runa took off her coat and hung it farther from the fire.

Chapter 3 Mouths and households

Petros needed to know how many people the road would feed. By midday he had three answers, each given with assurance, and Runa had begun a fourth.

They sat at the end of the hall’s long table, beside a shutter which admitted a narrow blade of light. Ingrith was elsewhere along the coast. Her steward, Dagrin, had brought the household list he used for winter dues and laid it flat with a knife at one end. He was a neat man whose beard grew in two colours. Runa remembered the darker one.

“Those are the houses owing labour,” he said.

“And these?” Runa pointed to the margin.

“Widows exempted from cart work.”

“People, not houses.”

“Houses under widows. You asked what they were.”

She copied the heading. Beside her, Petros was making small marks on a clean sheet. He had learned enough during the morning to stop saying families.

Oddrun stood across the table with a net bag hanging from one wrist. She had been on her way somewhere when Runa asked a question. Now she appeared to have abandoned the journey for the pleasure of watching.

“How many at your house?” Runa asked her.

“Tonight?”

“This winter.”

“Am I feeding them or naming them?”

“For provisions.”

“Then tonight.”

Dagrin exhaled. “Her house is six.”

“Which six?”

He looked down at the list.

“My sons are on another coast,” Oddrun said. “One of them has taken his wife. If you send me their barley, I’ll eat it, but you’ll have complaints when they come back.”

“Are they coming back?”

“Yes.”

“When?”

“When the boat does.”

Petros put down his pen.

“Let us say the people here now,” he said. “Then another number for people expected. We can allow for both.”

Runa drew a second column. Dagrin watched the ruled lines cross the space in which the office form expected a household head’s birthplace.

“That is not how the district rolls are kept.”

“This is the engineer’s copy.”

“Will you send both?”

“I’ll make the proper one from it.”

Oddrun shifted the bag on her wrist. Runa noticed the movement but was busy fitting a heading above the new column.

“Solli eats with my mother,” she said. “Should he go under her house?”

“He eats with everyone,” Oddrun said.

“But where does he live?”

“With me this week.”

“Usually.”

“With me most weeks. When Torve keeps him late he sleeps in the loft. When his foster father comes back he goes there.”

Dagrin tapped the list. “He owes service with his foster father’s boat.”

“He also owes me a morning with the lines,” said Oddrun. “And he owes Svanhild two hens he let into the cabbage.”

Petros smiled. Runa did not. She had ruled the page neatly, and there were now three sorts of obligation which could not be put in the same column. She remembered showing a junior clerk how to keep a first interview from sprawling. Ask the question clearly; do not let the answer drift into anecdote.

“How many meals does he eat at your house in a week?” she asked.

Oddrun considered her. “How hungry is he?”

Runa put down the ruler.

At the other end of the hall a woman was sorting dried peas, tipping them from a bowl onto a cloth. The little clicks became distinct in the silence. Dagrin began to say something, then stopped when Petros leaned forward.

“We are not fixing his share for all winter,” the engineer told Oddrun. “We need to know what stores must cross the gut if water interrupts the boats. If he sleeps here and eats there, I need to avoid counting his hunger twice.”

“Then don’t.”

“How?”

“Ask who has bought for him.”

Runa turned a fresh sheet sideways.

They worked another hour. It was awkward work, with no single number emerging from the questions as cleanly as Petros had hoped. Some households had bought meal together. Several exchanged fish for grain without bringing the grain through the hall. One woman kept stores for an absent sister’s children, who were staying beyond the gut; Dagrin counted the children at the sister’s empty house. Two men in the hall’s service ate at home except during Ingrith’s visits. The outer settlements expected winter guests whose boats had not yet arrived.

None of these things was difficult to describe. Each became difficult when Runa tried to fit it into the place allowed.

At last Petros gathered the rough sheets and asked permission to borrow the hall list. Dagrin refused, politely. It could be consulted here.

“Then I’ll return,” Runa said.

“You may copy it.”

“I will.”

“Accurately.”

She looked at him. He looked back, without malice and without yielding. She rolled the clean form and put it in its case.

Outside, Oddrun was waiting.

“I’ll take you to three doors,” she said. “Then I have work.”

“You don’t have to.”

“I know.”

The first house stood beside the drying racks. Its occupant, a woman named Selka, had been born far south of the Linn coast and married into the valley nineteen years earlier. She wore a local wool dress with a bright woven belt whose pattern Runa did not recognize. A boy sat on the floor beside her, sorting small wooden pegs.

Selka was delighted to see the writing case.

“Can I have a copy?”

“When it’s finished.”

“Of mine, I mean.”

“Yes.”

“With the house in it?”

“Yes.”

“And how long I’ve been here.”

Runa opened the case. “The exact year?”

“I have the year.” Selka went to a small chest and brought out a folded strip of paper, soft at the edges. It recorded payment for passage, with her name in a different hand from the master’s. She smoothed it on the table. “This was mine. He said I didn’t need to keep it after we married. I kept it.”

The boy continued to sort pegs. Selka gave Runa the names of those eating at the house, the meal already stored and the fish owed by a neighbour. Her husband’s elder brother called the house his when taxes were asked and hers when the roof needed work. She said this without lowering her voice. The boy snorted.

“Write the roof if there’s a place,” she said.

“There isn’t.”

“Pity.”

When Runa finished, Selka made her read the entry aloud. She liked hearing the date. She asked for it again.

At the second house nobody wanted to give a date.

The man at the door listened to Runa’s explanation and said he had food. His wife, standing behind him, said they had enough until the next ordinary delivery. He turned his head slightly, and she stopped.

“We are asking in case that delivery can’t come,” Runa said.

“Then we’ll fish.”

“If the boats can’t go?”

“We’ve lived here through winters.”

Oddrun moved past Runa and held out a twist of twine.

“Found this in the net you lent me. Yours?”

The man took it. His shoulders lowered a little.

“Thought I’d lost that.”

“You nearly did.”

They spoke about a repair while Runa waited. She looked at the door frame. It had been rebuilt on one side with sound new wood and left weathered on the other. Inside, a child’s shoe stood on the hearth stone, stuffed with dry grass. The wife watched Runa look and moved so she could no longer see it.

Runa closed the case.

“I can come another day.”

The man nodded immediately.

Farther along the path Oddrun said, “He’ll answer about grain.”

“I asked about grain.”

“You had the whole book out.”

Runa nearly replied that it was only a folder. The distinction would have been exact and useless. She put the folder deeper in her bag.

At the third house they found Solli eating.

He sat on a low bench with a wooden bowl in his lap. A woman at the hearth was asking him whether he had carried a message to Torve. He said yes. She asked what Torve had said. Solli swallowed and admitted he had not seen Torve himself, but had given the message to his daughter.

“Which message?” Oddrun asked.

The woman turned. “The brace.”

“Then he’ll want it by dusk.”

Solli looked at his bowl, judging the time needed to finish. Runa could see him deciding whether to claim he had already arranged it.

“Eat,” she said. “We won’t keep you.”

The woman gave her name as Ivara. Runa knew the house by Ivara’s father, although he had been dead long enough for the carving on the lintel to split. Ivara had taken in a sister’s daughter and a cousin recovering from a broken leg. The cousin was expected to leave after the first snow, if someone could fetch him. Solli had been helping with the heavy work and eating there in return.

“Have you bought stores for him?” Runa asked.

“I’ve fed him what we have.”

“For the winter?”

“He won’t be here all winter.”

“Suppose he is.”

Ivara set a pot back on its hook. “Then I’ll be glad to have him.”

Runa made a note about shared work and variable meals. It was the first note that day she knew would require a sentence when transferred. Solli finished his food, thanked Ivara, and went out with the brace on his shoulder. A moment later he came back for his mittens.

“He’s growing,” Ivara said, as if apologizing for the appetite. “Some days you can almost see it.”

Oddrun stayed to inspect the cousin’s leg. Runa stepped outside to give them room. The light had changed while they were indoors. Smoke lay flat beneath the hill, and the air smelled of snow though there was none yet. She could see the path to the two outer settlements running along the shore, broken where the gut opened. On this tide a person might cross by the lower stones. By evening those stones would be under water.

Petros came up from the landing, carrying his level. He stopped when he saw her.

“Any better?”

“More useful.”

He took the rough sheet she offered. She had separated stores already in the settlement from purchases expected, people present from those who might return. He read slowly, asking about two abbreviations.

“This I can use.”

“It won’t satisfy the office.”

“I’ll write that it satisfies me.”

“That won’t make the missing headings go away.”

“No.” He rolled the sheet carefully. “Tomorrow, perhaps.”

She laughed at his tone. He smiled, then looked past her towards the gut.

“I want to see the far houses before the weather turns. Would you come?”

“Yes.”

Ivara appeared at the door with a small bundle for Oddrun. She saw the rolled sheet in Petros’s hand.

“Is that the new list?”

“One of the working lists,” Runa said.

“And the finished one?”

“That will be copied.”

“Where will it be kept?”

“At the chapel. The regional office will have a copy for the works.”

“Will Dagrin?”

Runa hesitated. The steward had supplied information. He would expect access to what she produced from it. Her appointment did not say otherwise.

“Some of it.”

Ivara tightened the knot in Oddrun’s bundle.

“Come back before you give him mine,” she said.

Runa wrote that down on the outside of her folder, where she would see it first.

Chapter 4 A place beside the singer

Her grandmother could make a room wait without appearing to stop.

Eydis had been trying to learn how. If she paused in the middle of a verse, people thought she had forgotten it. Svanhild could turn towards the fire, pick up a spoon, examine something on its handle, and still hold the moment before a ship struck shore. Eydis found herself leaning forward even when she knew how the ship came in.

“Don’t copy my hands,” Svanhild said.

Eydis lowered them.

“I wasn’t.”

“You were picking up a spoon that isn’t there.”

“I thought it might help.”

“It will when you’re eating.”

Her grandmother pulled a length of yarn through the stocking over her hand. The needle emerged just below a thin place at the heel. She tugged, examined it and made another stitch. Eydis had imagined lessons taking place with both of them upright, like the school recitations. Instead she was holding yarn while Svanhild darned stockings for a man who had paid in dried fish.

“Start at the rain,” Svanhild said.

“From the beginning?”

“Does it rain at the beginning?”

“Yes.”

“Then that will do.”

Eydis began. She knew the first lines well enough now to watch her grandmother’s face. It made her go wrong. Svanhild let her finish the mistaken line, which was worse than being corrected halfway through.

“Where is the sail?”

“Wet.”

“Where?”

“Over the ship.”

“Over it how?”

Eydis drew the shape in the air.

“There. You pulled it in. You can’t have it spread in the next line without setting it again.”

“Could they set it?”

“In that wind? They could. Briefly.”

Eydis laughed. Svanhild waited for her to recover, then made her begin again.

The account concerned a boat that had returned from the southern islands with a split mast, one dead man and a cargo of glazed bowls. Eydis liked the bowls. They seemed stranger than treasure: all that danger for something a person might drop after dinner. Her grandmother had a blue piece from one of them, or from a bowl like them, which she kept above the door. She would not say which it was until Eydis asked the question properly.

“How would I know?” Svanhild said. “I wasn’t alive.”

“Then why do you keep it?”

“Because I like blue.”

That was the sort of answer Eydis distrusted. Her grandmother often meant it and something else at the same time. At home her mother generally told her the important part first, then became impatient if she asked about another part. Eydis had learned to save the other questions until someone else was present.

She tried the rain again. This time she remembered where the sail was, and the man bringing the bowls up from the hold could get past it. Svanhild gave a small nod without looking up.

Someone knocked. Oddrun entered before an answer, carrying a wooden shuttle.

“Yours?”

“No.”

“You had it.”

“I can have things that aren’t mine.”

“That’s why I’m asking.”

Svanhild took the shuttle and turned it over. Eydis remained poised at the next line. She could feel the words pressing behind her teeth, afraid they would go if she waited too long.

“It was Tova’s mother’s,” Svanhild said. “She lent it to me when the good one broke.”

“Then Tova can have it.”

“I suppose.”

Oddrun glanced at Eydis. “Which boat?”

“The blue bowls,” Eydis said.

“Mind the man with the pole. Your grandmother forgets him when she gets interested in the mast.”

“I do not.”

“You did at the summer gathering.”

“There were children falling asleep.”

“And a man with a pole holding the boat off the rock.”

“He’ll have his turn.”

Oddrun left with the shuttle. Svanhild made a face at the closed door, a small face Eydis was delighted to be allowed to see.

“Where were we?”

“At the bowls.”

“Then go on.”

Eydis had lost the line.

Her grandmother waited. Outside, Oddrun spoke to someone on the path. A crow landed on the fence and shook water from its wings. Eydis tried to think of the sound of the next word instead of its meaning. Nothing came.

Svanhild hummed the turn. Eydis caught it and continued, too quickly at first, then more slowly. The man with the pole appeared. This time she felt the bowl boat swing under him. When she reached the line in which the cargo slid across the hold, Svanhild’s needle stopped.

“Yes,” she said. “That will carry.”

Eydis grinned. She could not prevent it. Her grandmother smiled back, and for several lines Eydis no longer cared where to put her hands.

Afterward she asked for a scrap of paper.

“What for?”

“The line about the pole.”

“You’ve just said it.”

“I want to remember it tomorrow.”

“Then say it tomorrow.”

“Please.”

Svanhild considered, then nodded towards the chest. “There are spoiled letters under the cloth. Find a clean back.”

Eydis found one and used the little pen her mother had cut for her. The words looked too small on the page. She added a mark where her grandmother had hummed the turn. Svanhild leaned over to see.

“What’s that?”

“So I wait.”

“Will it make you?”

“I’ll know.”

Her grandmother touched the mark with the end of the needle. “Don’t put it there. Wait before he pushes. Once he’s pushing, you’d better let him finish.”

Eydis scratched out the mark and moved it. It was the first thing she had written in Varnvik that she wanted someone else to read.

They took dinner to the warning keeper’s house after midday.

Svanhild wrapped the pot in a cloth and tied its handles together. Eydis carried bread. The path went past the chapel and along the gut, where the water was low enough to expose a broad green seam on the rocks. A man crossed below them with his boots over his shoulder. He stepped slowly from stone to stone, lifted his knees where the water deepened, and swore at something Eydis could not see.

“Could we go that way?”

“We could get our feet wet that way.”

“He’s doing it.”

“He has someone to dry his stockings.”

They took the narrow timber footbridge farther inland. It was sound at one end and patched at the other, with a handline that sagged when Eydis tried to use it. She stopped in the middle to look down. The current moved both ways around a stone, one strip smooth, the other breaking into little white points.

“Keep coming,” Svanhild said.

Eydis crossed. On the far side her grandmother put down the pot and rearranged the cloth where it had slipped.

“When it runs over that stone, don’t come this way alone.”

“Which one?”

“The flat one. Find it.”

Eydis pointed.

“No. Behind.”

She found the right stone and named a pale streak along its top to show she had it. Her grandmother nodded.

They climbed above the shore. The warning house stood near a low rise, with a little roofed bell frame beyond it. The bell was dark and much smaller than the chapel bell. Its rope had been drawn up and looped out of reach. A shutter on the house hung open against the wall. No smoke came from the chimney.

“Is no one here?” Eydis asked.

“She’ll be back.”

“Who?”

“Tova.”

“Why does she need dinner?”

“Because she hasn’t had it.”

Svanhild led her round the house to a sheltered seat made from a thick plank on two stones. Eydis sat, glad to put the bread down. From here the shore looked folded. The gut was partly hidden behind its bank, but she could see a tall pale rock opposite the chapel and, beyond it, the darker channel to the landing.

Her grandmother followed her gaze.

“You see the white shoulder?”

Eydis nodded.

“What is below it?”

“A crack.”

“And below that?”

“A black line.”

“Brown in summer. When the water takes that line while the outer water is falling, you tell someone.”

“Why?”

“Because it shouldn’t.”

Eydis leaned forward to see better. Her grandmother shifted the pot out of the way of her foot.

“It happened when your mother was little. The people at the lower nets said the tide was going out and they had plenty of time. Your great-aunt had to fetch them uphill by their collars. One left his trousers on a peg and went home without them.”

“Why had he taken them off?”

“Because he expected wet legs and not an audience.”

Eydis laughed. Svanhild pointed again, making her find the mark from where they sat. Then she stood and took the pot to the door.

They waited a while. Eydis ate a crumb from the breadcloth and was told to leave the rest. She walked towards the bell frame. Beneath it the ground was bare and hard, except for a little tuft growing where a foot would usually stand. The rope ended in a worn loop. She wondered whether she could jump high enough to reach it.

“Don’t,” Svanhild said, without turning.

“I wasn’t.”

“You were measuring.”

Eydis put her hands behind her back.

Tova came by the upper path with a basket on her hip. She was younger than Runa, perhaps, with a face reddened by wind and a long lock of hair that had come loose from its pin. When she saw them she stopped, looked at the pot, and let her shoulders fall.

“I forgot.”

“I thought you might.”

“I meant to leave the key.”

“We have not starved on your step.”

Tova set down the basket and took a key from under her neckband. The lock resisted. She braced her hip against the door and worked the key, easing it back a little before turning it again.

Inside, the room was cold enough to make Eydis keep her cloak fastened. A pair of boots stood by the hearth, much too large for Tova. There were two cups on the shelf and only one place set at the table.

Tova put the dinner pot on a trivet and began making up the fire. Svanhild took the kindling from her.

“Sit.”

“I should heat it.”

“It was hot when we left.”

“That was a while ago.”

“Then you should have hurried.”

Tova smiled a little. She sat and ate from the pot while Svanhild coaxed the fire. Eydis watched steam begin to rise from the damp ends of the sticks.

“How was the clinic?” her grandmother asked.

“Busy.”

“That is a clerk’s answer.”

Tova glanced at Eydis and then back to her food. “Busy in ways I am not telling you over dinner.”

“Fair.”

A moment later Tova asked about the boat, and Svanhild told her Eydis had come with an engineer and a rooster, only one of which was expected to stay all winter. Eydis explained the rooster properly. Tova laughed with food still in her mouth and had to cover it with her hand.

When they left, her grandmother took the empty pot. Tova stood in the doorway with the basket against her leg. Inside it Eydis could see folded cloths and a little jar wrapped so it would not break.

“Will you be there tomorrow too?” Svanhild asked.

“At the clinic? Yes.”

“And the day after?”

“Yes.”

Her grandmother looked towards the bell frame. Tova did not follow her eyes.

“Tell me when you hear from the hall,” Svanhild said.

“You’ll hear before I do.”

They said good night while there was still light enough to walk home.

On the return path Svanhild stopped below a birch and asked Eydis to find the mark again. From here the white rock was narrower. The crack looked like a shadow, and for a moment she could not distinguish the dark line from the wetness beneath it. She moved two steps to the left.

“There.”

“Good.”

“Can they see it from the chapel?”

“From the upper window.”

“Why don’t they watch it there?”

“They ring hours there.”

Eydis waited for more. Her grandmother continued down the path, carrying the empty pot against her side. A faint clink came from the lid at each step.

At home Runa asked what she had learned. Eydis told her the pole man’s line and the story of the trousers. She did not mention the cold house until later, when they were going to bed.

“Tova’s dinner had a skin on it.”

Runa pulled the blanket up over her.

“That happens.”

“She still ate it.”

“Was she hungry?”

“I think so.”

“Then I’m glad you took it.”

Her mother kissed her forehead. Eydis wanted to explain that taking it had not made the house warm when they arrived, or put anyone there before Tova came home. But Runa was already moving the jug away from her book, and Eydis could not find a way to begin that did not sound like another complaint.

She touched the scrap of copied verse beneath her pillow. In the morning she would see whether the mark made her wait.

Chapter 5 The seam beneath the boat

Torve’s daughter was inside the hull when Runa arrived, and would not get out until her father stopped telling her where to look.

“I can see it.”

“You’re looking too high.”

“Because that’s where it’s coming from.”

“Feel below.”

“I have felt below.”

Runa stood at the open end of the shed with her folder tucked beneath her arm. Rain tapped the roof, ran along a bent strip of bark and dropped into a bucket whose placement suggested considerable experience. The hull rested above her waist on trestles. Only the girl’s head and shoulders showed over its side.

Torve crouched beneath the bow, holding a lamp. He had become heavier since Runa last saw him, the flesh settling low on his face. His hands were still quick.

“Good morning,” she said.

Neither answered immediately. The girl reached down, felt along something inside, and gave a short sound of vindication.

“Here.”

“Show me.”

“I can’t show you while you’re under it.”

Torve came out, straightened with an effort and climbed onto the bench beside the hull. The girl moved over just enough. They bent their heads together. Runa waited while the rain filled another small measure of the bucket.

Finally Torve said, “That’s it.”

His daughter looked at Runa and rolled her eyes with such economy that her father did not see.

“Runa,” he said, coming down. “You’ll have to wait.”

“I need the estimate for the ferry repairs.”

“I have to know how much is rotten first.”

“That’s why I’m here.”

“Then you’ve come at the right time.”

He handed her a brace of wood, pointed to a place under the hull and told her to hold it there. She crouched. The ground was damp. A cold drop travelled from the roof down the back of her neck.

“Hard up,” he said.

She pushed.

“Like that.”

For a while the work consisted of pressure. She could hear the girl scraping inside, stopping, and scraping again. Torve moved along the outside with a narrow blade. The smell released from the seam was sourer than the general smell of tar and old water.

Runa’s forearm began to tremble.

“How long?” she asked.

“Until I get this out.”

She shifted her feet, which weakened the pressure. Torve told her to keep it steady. She wanted to say that he should have warned her how long she would need to hold it. Instead she gripped the brace with both hands.

The girl looked over the side.

“Solli!”

The boy appeared from behind a pile of planks, chewing something.

“Take that.”

He slipped into Runa’s place. She stood, flexing her fingers. He held the brace with one hand and continued chewing.

“You could have called him earlier,” she said.

“He was eating,” the girl answered.

Runa laughed, surprised. Torve glanced up, pleased at the sound without knowing its cause.

The seam came free in a long, dark strip. He laid it on the bench and used the blade to expose softer wood beneath. His daughter climbed out. She was sixteen or seventeen, broad through the wrists, with a scrap of sailcloth wrapped round one thumb. Her name was Vigdis. Runa remembered carrying her once, a solemn toddler who had kept trying to pull the clasp off her coat.

“You won’t remember me,” Runa said.

“You came when my mother was buried.”

“I did.”

Vigdis nodded and turned to fetch another tool. Runa looked at the workbench. There were cups along its far edge, one holding pegs, another hardened pitch. She remembered Torve’s wife bringing those same cups indoors to wash after he had used them for things that were not drink.

“Will the ferry take the loads?” she asked.

“It will when I’ve finished with it.” Torve pressed the blade into the exposed wood. “What loads?”

“Petros has written them here.”

He wiped his hand before taking the sheet. Runa appreciated the care. Too many people either feared touching a paper or used it as if the material had been put into the world to absorb grease.

“On a smooth crossing?” he asked.

“For ordinary use.”

“What does he call ordinary?”

“I can ask.”

“Ask him to come down. I’ll show him.”

They discussed the width of the cargo, the places where men would stand and whether people would insist on bringing an animal across with the sacks. Torve said people always did things with animals that no one put into estimates. Vigdis said the animal was usually better behaved than the man holding it.

Runa wrote that the engineer should inspect the boat before finalizing capacity. Then she put her pen away and helped Vigdis lift a sound plank down from the rack. This was work she could do without shaking. The plank smelled fresh where the end had been cut. Together they turned it until the grain lay the way Torve wanted.

Dagrin arrived with a man Runa did not know.

The stranger wore a good leather apron over his travelling clothes. He looked at the hull first and the people second. Torve saw him do it.

“This is Kolfin,” the steward said. “From the jarl’s western yard.”

“We’ve met.”

Kolfin nodded. He kept his hands beside him, as though unwilling to be seen reaching for anything.

Dagrin asked Torve to step outside. Torve said whatever concerned his yard could be said in it. Solli quietly set down the brace. Runa closed the flap of her folder.

“The winter boat work is to be divided differently,” Dagrin said. “The western repairs have fallen behind. Kolfin can bring men here, and the landing is better placed for the supply route.”

“Which work?”

“The estate’s work.”

“Which boats?”

Dagrin named them. Torve stood without moving while the names went past. Runa knew two of the boats, both of them old customers of this yard, although customer was not exactly the word for a hull supplied in return for occupation and dues.

“And where am I to do mine?” Torve asked.

“You retain the small shed until the end of the present undertaking.”

Vigdis made a sharp movement behind Runa. Torve lifted his hand towards her without looking.

“The small shed has no launching way.”

“Arrangements can be made.”

“By whom?”

“Torve.” Dagrin lowered his voice. “The landing was held for service. The jarl is entitled to arrange that service.”

“My father held it. His father held it.”

“And both did work for the hall.”

“So have I.”

“Your work isn’t in question.”

“Then what is?”

Rain fell into the bucket. Kolfin glanced towards it. He looked uncomfortable, but not sufficiently uncomfortable to leave.

The steward took a folded notice from inside his coat and offered it. Torve did not take it. Dagrin waited, then laid it on a clean part of the bench.

“There will be a hearing tomorrow, if you want it. Before the winter sitting is arranged.”

“I want my yard.”

“You will be heard.”

When the two men had gone, the bucket overflowed. Solli moved it and put another in its place. Nobody had told him to.

Runa stood with her folder under her arm. Her presence now seemed intrusive, though it had begun as ordinary work. She wanted to say that the notice did not itself settle anything. Torve knew that. She wanted to ask whether there was an old agreement. He might take the question as an offer she had no authority to make.

Vigdis solved the problem by asking her.

“Can they?”

“I need to know the terms under which—”

“Can they put us out?”

“I don’t know.”

The girl turned back to the hull.

Torve picked up the notice at last. He read slowly, his lips moving once over a phrase. Then he folded it along the existing crease and put it under the cup of pegs.

“I’ll have your price after the hearing,” he told Runa.

She left him to his work.

The gathering next morning took place in the lower hall, not on the exposed Thing ground. People came because the boatyard mattered to them, because they had business with Dagrin, or because something was happening and they were near enough to listen. A woman brought a child who slept through the first half. A man argued about whether this was a hearing or merely an opportunity to state what would go before the Thing. The distinction consumed more time than Runa expected.

Torve stood beside Vigdis. His daughter wore a clean dress under her work jacket. There was still a dark rim beneath one fingernail which she kept rubbing with her thumb.

Dagrin described the redistribution of work. Torve described the yard his father had repaired after a winter collapse, the slips he had maintained and the boats he had delivered. Two men spoke to the quality of those boats. Nobody disputed it.

Then someone asked Svanhild how the landing had first been given.

Runa had known her mother would be present. She had not known that the room would turn to her with such relief.

Svanhild sat with her hands on her knees. She named the older jarl, the storm in which Torve’s grandfather had saved boats belonging to the hall, and the work promised afterward. Her account was clear enough that Runa could have copied it without asking for a word to be repeated. The landing and its benefits had been held while the house supplied boat service. The hall retained the right to arrange its work.

Torve interrupted.

“My grandfather was given more than work.”

“He was given a place to do it,” Svanhild said.

“For his children.”

“Who continued the service.”

“And if the hall gives the work away?”

No one answered quickly. Runa waited for her mother to distinguish something, as she so often could. Svanhild looked at Torve, then at the men who had spoken for his boats.

“The work and the holding have always stood together,” she said.

The steward nodded. A man near the hearth began explaining that the jarl could not maintain two yards for one set of obligations. Torve tried to answer him and was told to let him finish. When he was allowed to speak, the point he needed to answer had changed.

Runa felt the frustration in her own body. She had sat through southern hearings conducted in more orderly fashion which produced much the same experience. She could see Torve gathering too many objections at once. He began with his grandfather, moved to the launching way, then demanded to know why Kolfin should have the benefit of timber his own household had bought.

“That can be valued,” Dagrin said.

“It is already in the yard.”

“And can be valued.”

The hearing ended with a recommendation that he accept compensation for improvements which could not be moved. Nothing was burned or seized. There was no dramatic refusal to hear him. People spoke kindly afterward about the difficulty of losing a place where one’s family had worked for so long. Runa found that almost worse.

Outside, her mother fastened her coat.

“You could have told me,” Runa said.

“Told you what?”

“That you were giving the account.”

“I often give accounts.”

“Of my work’s boatwright being put out of his yard.”

“He is the valley’s boatwright before he is your work’s.”

Runa looked away. She could not decide whether the correction was deserved, and hated having to decide while angry.

“Does the account mean they can dismiss him for doing nothing wrong?”

“It means what was agreed.”

“Which was?”

“I have just said it.”

Svanhild drew her scarf tight and went to speak to an older woman waiting by the gate. Runa remained under the eaves until the cold drove her to move.

She went to the yard for the estimate. Torve was not there. Vigdis had chalked marks on two benches and was measuring the door of the small shed with a knotted cord.

“That one won’t go through,” she said, seeing where Runa looked. “We can take the top off. The legs have to come separately.”

“Where is your father?”

“Walking.”

Runa held the far end of the cord while Vigdis checked the width again. This time she was useful from the beginning. The girl marked the measure with a knot, pulled it tight, and stood holding it without knowing where to go next.

“Will he come back soon?” Runa asked.

“Yes.”

Vigdis looked down the path. After a while she began untying the measuring knot, which had tightened too far to be easily undone.

Chapter 6 Where the water goes

Petros brought more cord than food.

Runa noticed when they stopped above the lee cove. He opened his bag to find a second reel, a wrapped instrument, two pegs and the heel of yesterday’s bread. Hervi watched this inventory with increasing satisfaction. At last he took a piece of dried fish from his own pocket and held it out.

“For when you finish eating the rope.”

Petros accepted. “I thought we would be back by midday.”

“So did your mother, probably.”

“My mother has not expected me before dark for many years.”

Hervi liked that. He moved over on the rock and made room for him.

They had come round the headland by the raised path, examining the existing track as they went. In two places water had cut across it. Elsewhere the surface remained sound beneath a skin of mud. Petros drove a peg where a drain might be cleared, then stood back and asked Hervi where the material could be put without interfering with the boats.

Runa wrote while the pilot answered. Dagrin had sent a labourer named Gerd to show them the estate’s boundaries. He was a narrow man with large hands and a way of examining every patch of bare ground as if deciding how much effort it would take to dig. When Petros asked about timber, Gerd could name trees he had seen three valleys away. When asked who owned those trees, he became less helpful.

The lee cove lay beneath them, a pale strip of shingle between dark shoulders of rock. Runa had visited it as a child and thought it farther from home than it was. The path took less than an hour without loads. She wrote that down and added in fair light. Below, small waves collapsed against the shingle. Beyond the cove mouth the water broke white where the deeper swell reached the headland.

“A grain ship couldn’t come in,” Petros said.

“No.” Hervi pointed with the dried fish. “She’d ground before she could turn. And if she didn’t, she’d lose her rudder trying to leave.”

“But light boats.”

“In this wind.”

“And another wind?”

“In another wind we use another place.”

Petros waited, chewing. Hervi smiled and began to explain properly.

The shelter was good from the open swell but poor when a hard wind came directly across the inlet. A boat could land with more water under her than she could expect for leaving. The shingle moved. People bringing loads down the path had to wait above a particular point until the pilot called them, or their weight would crowd the wrong side of the boat. Runa had known some of this, forgotten some, and never been told the rest because she had not been responsible for moving cargo.

She asked Hervi to repeat the instruction about the point. He did, using a different landmark. She asked which landmark he wanted in the note.

“Both, if you like.”

“If one moves?”

“If that one moves, don’t come down.”

It was a rock larger than Svanhild’s house. Petros laughed, and Runa put a small cross beside it on the sketch.

They returned along the track, measuring the places where a sledge could pass another without one being unloaded. Gerd carried the far end of the cord. He did not rush to the next point, which irritated Runa until she saw he was counting his steps and testing the surface with each one. At a hollow he stamped twice, listened, and went round it.

“Rot under that,” he said.

Petros knelt and pushed a peg into the ground. It sank farther than it should have. He made a note of his own.

They passed Varnvik before midday, but only just. Svanhild’s chimney was smoking. Runa could imagine the warmth inside, the sound of her mother asking Eydis to fetch something from a shelf. She had said she would eat at home.

“Shall we go on?” Petros asked.

She looked at the sky. Light lay low behind the cloud, with a bright edge that might mean clearing or worse weather beyond it.

“If we stop now, we won’t see the far houses.”

They went on.

The gut was narrower than the cove and more troublesome than it appeared. At low water the channel shrank between banks of mud and stones. At high water it spread behind a tongue of land, filling the places where reeds stood now. The footbridge crossed above the broadest reach, but its northern approach had been damaged. A person could step across the bad patch. A loaded cart could not.

Petros walked the bank, then came back and walked it again. He asked Hervi where boats passed during the high tides. The pilot showed him. He asked how often the mouth shifted. Hervi named two recent changes and a winter in which the channel had come farther inland than anyone remembered.

Runa wrote allow clear passage below. Gerd squatted beside an old stump and said the nearer bank would take a post if they went deep enough.

They discussed a permanent crossing, its height and expense, without pretending it could be built from what lay at hand. The immediate work would repair the approach and make a better landing for the ferry, so carts could unload without standing axle-deep in mud. Petros wanted the road raised along the bank. Hervi wanted room for boat lines. Gerd wanted to know who would move the old stones.

“Can they be moved?” Petros asked.

“The little ones.”

“And those?”

Gerd looked towards the higher bank, where three dark stones stood against the reeds.

“Ask the keepers.”

Petros marked them on his drawing. He did not ask why.

Before crossing, they followed the bank to the ordinary ferry landing.

A boat waited there with its painter tied above the wet line. Its owner was repairing a wicker basket beside it, using strips which had soaked long enough to bend readily. He asked whether the surveyors wanted passage. Petros said they wanted to see where loads were handled. The man looked at his half-finished repair and put it down.

“Bring that,” he told Gerd, pointing to an empty cask.

They carried it to the bank. There was no difficulty until they tried to put it aboard. The boat moved away from the hand that pushed it; the cask caught on the side; Gerd stood in the one place the owner needed for his foot. Nothing was heavy, and everything obstructed everything else.

The owner rearranged them. Runa held the painter from farther uphill while Gerd took the cask end on. This time it went aboard easily. Then they brought it back.

“Now full,” the man said.

Petros studied the bank. “I see.”

“You see it dry.”

He showed the marks left by higher water and the slippery place where he kept laying stones which winter carried away. He did not need a grand crossing for his own daily work. He needed somewhere to stand with a load while the boat found its place. Petros asked how long he had used this landing. The man answered by naming the master he had worked for before his daughter married.

Runa supplied the approximate years. The owner corrected her by one because the marriage had been delayed. He began explaining why, stopped, and said that had nothing to do with the landing.

Gerd put the cask back. Runa returned the painter to its post, making the knot carefully enough that the owner watched without interfering. When she had finished he pulled it once to test it. She felt absurdly pleased by the lack of correction.

He offered to take them across. They declined because Petros needed to examine the footbridge approach. The man picked up his basket again and asked them not to leave the cask where it would fill with rain. Gerd turned it on its side.

Walking back, Runa asked Petros whether the small improvement at the landing could begin before the permanent plan. He said it was exactly the sort of work the first allowance covered, provided the ground would hold what they put into it. They stopped twice more to examine that ground.

At the second stop a piece of bank came away beneath Gerd’s boot. It was no great fall, only a soft edge breaking where the water had undercut it. He moved back and pressed the next place with the heel of his shovel. Petros marked the sounder line. Runa did not record a mishap. She recorded the length that needed attention and stood farther from the edge while they finished.

They crossed the footbridge singly. Runa held her skirts above the wet boards. Halfway across, she remembered her daughter looking down from the same place, and resolved to tell her not to stop there. Then she realized she was stopping there herself and went on.

The nearer outer settlement consisted of houses tucked beneath a low ridge, with drying racks along the shore and a patchwork of fenced ground behind. The second lay farther round the point. Neither was remote when boats could move. On foot with a load, the distance changed.

At the first house they visited, a woman was cutting dried fish with such force that the knife struck the board after every piece. A little girl watched from the far side of the table. Each blow made her blink.

Runa explained their purpose. The woman wiped her hands and asked when work would begin.

“Some clearing can begin now,” Petros said through Runa. “The larger work needs the survey and funding.”

“Before the ice?”

“Not all of it.”

“What part?”

They went outside to look. The woman showed them where her husband had fallen carrying a sack, where she could get a sledge through if the ground were frozen but not if it thawed, and where people coming from the landing habitually cut across her fence. She had repaired that fence three times. A road would have to give them another way before she let workers take it down.

A string of shells and small carved pieces hung above her doorway. When Petros ducked under it on the way back in, she reached up to stop it catching on his collar.

“Will you want those gone?” she asked Runa.

“No.”
