“Your people sometimes do.”

“These people want to know where water stands.”

The woman looked at Petros, then at the child still watching the knife.

“Then let them ask before they dig.”

She fed them. There was broth with fish in it and bread cut thinner than at Svanhild’s table. Runa began to refuse a second piece, then saw that the refusal would be noticed by the child. She took it and ate slowly.

The missed autumn delivery had not left the household without food. It had left them using meal put aside for later, exchanging better dried fish for ordinary grain, and wondering how much a second small-boat journey would cost. The woman spoke more about the cost than the hunger. Her husband had gone to help a relative mend a roof and would return with food as part of the payment, if the weather held.

“People say you don’t want the Temple,” Hervi said, not accusingly.

“I don’t want their man in my cupboard. I want a path I can carry things on.”

Petros asked Runa what she had said. Runa translated exactly. He nodded and asked whether the cupboard was against the damp wall, because he could smell wet grain.

The woman stared at him, then led him to it.

By the time they left, Gerd had helped shift the cupboard a hand’s width away from the wall and Petros had shown the woman how to leave air beneath the sack. She gave him a small piece of fish for the road. He put it in his pocket with exaggerated care, and the little girl laughed.

They did not reach the second settlement. Light was failing, and Runa refused when Petros suggested walking another short distance to see the turn. He accepted at once, which made her sorry she had sounded sharp.

On the return, she asked about the emergency allowance.

“It pays for the first clearing, drainage and repairs,” he said. “I can hire men within it. I can buy a limited quantity of timber. If we find the ground worse than expected, I can spend it faster.”

“And after that?”

“We wait for the larger agreement.”

“Could the office refuse?”

“Yes.”

The answer came so easily that she looked at him.

“They sent us.”

“To find what it will cost.”

She knew this. She had copied those words into two letters. Still, she had imagined the sending itself as evidence of a settled intention.

“How much can we make useful before then?”

He took several steps before replying.

“A little useful work is still possible. I would not promise a road.”

At the damaged approach to the footbridge they met two men carrying a chest on poles. The chest belonged to the dead warning keeper’s house and was going to a relative in Varnvik. They had rested it on a stone while one man adjusted a cloth beneath the pole. Both were breathing hard.

Gerd took the rear position. Runa helped lift. The chest was heavier than its size suggested, and the man at the front said so as if someone might have changed it since they began. Petros offered to take his end, but he refused until they reached the far side of the bridge.

The transfer required more discussion than Runa wanted. There was no room to set the chest on the bridge, the poles projected too far for an easy turn, and one of the ropes had slipped. Everyone gave an instruction at once. Then Hervi told them all to shut up except the man who could see the ground ahead.

They got it across.

Runa’s arm ached from holding one corner steady. On the Varnvik side she helped retie the rope and stayed while the men rested. She could smell dinner smoke. Her stomach had begun to feel hollow again.

“What is in it?” Petros asked.

“Tools,” said the owner.

“What sort?”

“The heavy sort.”

Hervi laughed so hard that he had to put the lantern down.

They brought the chest to its destination in full dark. Runa left the others there and went home with mud halfway up her skirt.

Svanhild was sitting beside the fire, working at a seam. Eydis had gone to bed. A bowl stood covered on the table.

“I thought you were eating here,” her mother said.

“We went farther.”

“I gathered.”

“There was a chest to move.”

Svanhild looked at her hands. “Did you try to carry it yourself?”

“Not all of it.”

Her mother rose, uncovered the bowl and put the broth back over the fire. Runa began explaining the day: the track, the outer house, Petros’s questions. Halfway through she heard how much of it sounded like justification and stopped.

Svanhild stirred the broth.

“I have not accused you of enjoying yourself.”

“I did, some of it.”

“Good.”

Runa took off her wet stockings. Her mother brought a dry pair and laid them beside the chair. For several minutes they sat without speaking, while the broth heated and the mud began to flake from the hem of Runa’s skirt.

Chapter 7 The hearing agreed

Torve brought his papers in a sailcloth wrapper tied with a length of good line. Runa recognized the line because he had refused to let the ferry crew use it where it would drag across stone.

He set the bundle on the chapel table and untied it slowly. There were receipts, letters, a reckoning of timber, and one sheet so thick with corrections that Runa had to hold it towards the window to distinguish the original figures from later changes. The oldest letter smelled of smoke. Someone had folded it against a still-soft seal, leaving a red crescent on the opposite corner.

“My father kept these,” he said.

“Have you read them all?”

“Enough.”

She did not ask what that meant. He could read ordinary accounts, but a clerk’s elaborate southern hand would slow him. It slowed her, too, though she disliked doing it in front of him.

They began with the petition. Torve wanted the synod to hear the matter under the old harbour compact, which allowed disputes about supplied work to be submitted if both parties agreed. Runa had looked up the compact after the informal hearing. It did not give the court authority to seize a case from the Thing. It did give Torve something specific to request.

“Why should they agree?” he asked.

“Because their boats need work. Because they think the records support them. Because refusing may cost them more argument than a hearing.”

“Do the records support them?”

“I haven’t finished reading.”

“Then read.”

He sat opposite her. She wanted him to leave for an hour, so she could spread the papers and be wrong privately before giving him an answer. He stayed. She read.

Some letters treated the yard as an estate service holding. Others addressed Torve’s father as the holder of the landing without qualification. There were years of work accepted and paid for, and expenses recognized by stewards who had been dead for decades. Nothing on the first reading gave the hall a clear right to end the arrangement simply by moving its work elsewhere. Nothing she could safely rely on established every right Torve claimed.

She said so. He listened, rubbing the edge of the sailcloth between his fingers.

“So we ask.”

“Yes.”

“Write it, then.”

She wrote the formal opening. Torve watched the pen. He wanted his father and grandfather named. She included them. He wanted the improvements described. She asked which ones could be shown by witnesses and receipts. He said anyone with eyes could see them. She explained why that would not answer the question of who paid.

He became impatient. She became more precise, which made him more impatient.

At last he said, “Write that I will not be turned out of a yard my people made fit for theirs.”

She paused.

“That sounds as if you are refusing the court’s authority before it hears you.”

“Then make it sound like what I said.”

“I am trying.”

“Try with those words.”

The school bell had finished several minutes earlier. From the room beyond came the sound of children reciting a list, one voice consistently half a beat behind. Runa looked at the petition and crossed out the sentence she had begun.

She wrote Torve’s words as his declaration, following the formal request. Then she read the whole thing aloud, including the part she would have preferred to soften.

He nodded.

“Does Vigdis want to be named?” Runa asked.

“She is my daughter.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her for a moment, then gathered one of the letters.

“I’ll ask.”

Dagrin received the petition that afternoon. He read it twice, stopping at the declaration on the second reading.

“He could have said this without insulting the hall.”

“He believes he has been insulted.”

“The court won’t decide that.”

“Then the line needn’t trouble it.”

Dagrin looked up. Runa regretted the answer. It sounded too much like something she would repeat to her mother afterward to demonstrate that she had held her own.

“The reference is narrow,” she said. “Occupation arising from accepted service, and whether the present notice can end it. You needn’t submit every claim to the shore.”

“I know what the compact says.”

“Of course.”

He folded the petition and said Ingrith would return before the week ended. Runa left him with it.

During the days that followed, the yard continued working. Vigdis kept the measurements she had made. Torve supplied the ferry estimate with more allowance for replacement timber than Petros liked. Petros visited, looked at the exposed seam, and accepted the allowance. Runa copied the costs. The threatened loss of the yard did not make the damaged wood sound.

At home Svanhild asked whether she was helping Torve appeal.

“Ask for arbitration.”

“Is that different?”

“Yes.”

Her mother waited, and Runa heard the trap she had prepared for herself: either give a lecture nobody wanted or seem unwilling to explain. She tried a shorter answer.

“The estate has to agree to this hearing. We aren’t asking the synod to overrule the Thing without consent.”

“And when it agrees?”

“Then it has agreed.”

Svanhild cut a thread with her teeth. “Your mouth has gone southern.”

Runa wanted to say her mother’s mouth went wherever the company required. She swallowed it. Eydis was at the table, pretending not to listen while drawing a knot.

Ingrith returned in weather that brought sleet against the hall’s western wall. Runa saw her arrive from the school window: a small party, tired horses, people coming out to take reins and receive baggage. The jarl did not appear in the street to pronounce on the road. She went indoors. Runa’s appointment was for the next morning.

She wore her good coat, brushed clean, and carried copies rather than the originals. Dagrin admitted her to a room behind the hall where a brazier warmed one side of the table and left the other cold. Ingrith sat with a cup beside her and a leather packet open in front of her. She had the air of someone who had been hearing useful explanations since before breakfast and would prefer a useful silence.

“You are Svanhild’s daughter.”

“Yes.”

“I remember. You corrected a reckoning for my steward when you were a girl.”

“I may have.”

“You certainly did. He complained.”

Runa waited.

Ingrith picked up Torve’s petition. “Will your employer send someone to hear this before the sea closes?”

“Sister Ragnel is already on the coastal circuit. If the reference is agreed, she can hear preliminary statements here and set a date.”

“Preliminary statements.”

“To establish what both parties submit.”

“I am not submitting my coast to her.”

“No.”

“Then make that plain.”

Runa showed her the proposed wording. Ingrith read the limits with more care than the names. She asked whether a ruling would prevent her from employing Kolfin at all. Runa said she could still employ Kolfin. The court would decide whether ending Torve’s work for the estate also entitled her to take his yard.

The jarl leaned back. Dagrin stood beside the door, watching Runa rather than his employer.

“Torve has done good work,” Ingrith said. “He also takes a long time over it.”

“Some of the boats are old.”

“So am I compared with my first steward. Yet everyone expects me to work faster.”

Runa smiled cautiously. Ingrith did not.

After another question about enforcement, she agreed. The harbour needed its repairs. She would guarantee compliance with the award on the matter submitted. Dagrin would provide the service accounts. If Torve wanted to turn the hearing into a dispute over every fish dried on the shore, he could do so somewhere else.

Runa carried the consent to the chapel with sleet melting on the folded outer cover.

She found Vigdis alone at the yard, holding one end of a long batten against a frame while trying to reach a weight with her foot. Runa set down the consent and took the free end. The girl told her where to hold it, then made a mark with charcoal.

“Father’s at the ferry.”

“I can leave this.”

Vigdis looked at the folded paper. “Is it yes?”

“Yes to a hearing.”

The girl nodded and returned to the batten. Runa held it until the last mark was made. When she let go, it sprang away from the curve with enough force to startle her.

“Always wants to be straight,” Vigdis said.

“Does it?”

“Not all of it. That piece does.”

She laid it on the bench and put the weight on top. Runa asked whether Torve had spoken to her about being named in the petition. Vigdis said he had told her she might be called to describe the work.

“Did he ask whether you wanted that?”

“He asked whether I could remember.”

Runa waited. The girl moved a piece of charcoal out of a damp patch.

“I can remember. I made the measurements.”

“I know.”

“Will they ask Father first?”

“About some things. About your measurements, they should ask you.”

“What if he answers?”

“Tell the judge you made them.”

Vigdis looked towards the slip. Runa could almost hear the advice taking on the father’s actual presence and becoming harder to follow.

“I can help you put the figures in order,” Runa offered.

“They are in order.”

The girl fetched a narrow board. The measurements ran along it beside marks identifying each piece. Some had been crossed out. Runa asked why. Vigdis showed her the new edge of the timber, where taking away a damaged place had required a different length. Nothing in the account was obscure once the wood was beside it.

“Could you bring this?”

“To the hearing?”

“Yes.”

“It’s not a paper.”

“It is what you used.”

Vigdis thought about that. Then she wrapped the board and set it apart. She asked whether she would have to wear the dress kept for gatherings. Runa said she did not know what her family would expect, but she should wear something in which she could stand comfortably. Vigdis considered the answer inadequate and said so with a glance.

Torve returned carrying the repaired ferry fitting. He put it down before reading the consent. Runa watched his face while he read the limits. He asked about one phrase. She explained it. He said the same thing could have been written in fewer words. She agreed, which annoyed him because he had been prepared to dispute it.

Vigdis showed him the board she would bring. He reached to unfold it, then stopped when she said she had already wrapped it for keeping dry.

“I know what is on it,” he said.

“Yes.”

He let go. Runa picked up her case and gave them the hearing arrangements. At the door she heard Torve ask his daughter whether she wanted him beside her when she spoke. The girl took a long time answering. Runa left before hearing what she chose.

Ragnel arrived three days later, on a small boat whose crew had more to say about the crossing than she did. She wore a plain travelling cloak over a dark dress, with the Temple’s mark at her throat. Her hair was cut short enough to dry quickly. She greeted Astri as someone she knew, asked for hot water, and spent the first hour reading in a room where nobody interrupted her.

Torve and Dagrin came at the appointed time. Runa sat beside the table to record the agreed scope. Ragnel asked each man to state it in his own words.

Dagrin did well. Torve spoke about his children.

“I understand that is what matters to you,” Ragnel said. “I need to know you understand what I can decide at this hearing.”

“Whether he can put us out.”

“Whether the estate can end your present occupation on the grounds given in this notice. I cannot settle inheritance without evidence and a reference extending to it. I cannot grant you other people’s use of the shore.”

“I haven’t asked for that.”

“Good. Then we agree it is not before me.”

Her voice was quiet and unhurried. Torve looked less certain under her courtesy than he had under Dagrin’s objections. He had prepared to be denied something and now had to listen carefully to the thing being offered.

Ragnel explained that witnesses could speak in Linn, that the letters would be read aloud, and that each party could challenge the interpretation. Runa would copy the record but would not serve as the court’s sole translator, since she had helped prepare the petition. Another clerk from the circuit would do that work.

Runa had expected the arrangement. It still stung to hear her usefulness limited in front of the steward.

When they were finished, Torve asked whether Vigdis could attend.

“Yes.”

“She worked on the repairs.”

“Then she may also have evidence to give.”

He nodded, once, and stood.

Outside, Svanhild was waiting beside a cart of turnips. She had bought more than she could comfortably carry and refused to admit she had timed the purchase to meet Runa.

“Take that end.”

Runa took it. Together they carried the basket up the path, stopping at the bend to change hands.

“How was she?” Svanhild asked.

“Ragnel?”

“Unless you have acquired another judge.”

“Careful.”

“Those are the ones who make me tired.”

“She listens.”

“So do I.”

Runa looked at her. Svanhild shifted the basket and waited for her to lift.

They passed a little boy dragging a stick through the mud. He moved aside, then saluted them with the stick as if they were a ship. Svanhild gave him a command in a deep captain’s voice. He obeyed so enthusiastically that he nearly fell backwards. Runa laughed. Her mother kept the voice for the next few steps, issuing orders about the dangerous turnip cargo.

At the house they set the basket down too hard. One turnip rolled across the floor. Eydis came to catch it, missed, and chased it under the chair.

“Did the jarl agree?” Svanhild asked, in her ordinary voice.

“Yes.”

“Then we will hear what they make of us.”

“What they make of the notice.”

Svanhild began putting turnips in the bin. Runa joined her. They worked from opposite sides of the basket, choosing the sound ones first.

After a while her mother handed her a small crooked turnip with two long roots.

“Your old schoolmaster.”

Runa looked at it, tried not to, and laughed again.

Chapter 8 The people who go

Eydis woke before anyone called her. For a moment she could not remember why she had wanted to wake, and lay listening to the room above the kitchen: the faint scrape of ash below, a footstep, wind worrying the shutter.

Then she remembered the boat.

She dressed without waking Runa. This was more difficult than she had expected. Her stockings were underneath her mother’s coat, and the loose board by the bed gave a long creak when she stepped over it. Runa shifted, opened her eyes and asked the time.

“Early.”

“Where are you going?”

“With Grandmother.”

Runa closed her eyes again, then opened them more fully.

“Where?”

“The departure. I told you.”

“You told me about several things.”

“The boat. The one with the last trading loads.”

Her mother sat up, hair loose against her face. She looked younger in the half-light and much less certain than she did at the chapel table.

“Stay with her. Don’t go onto the landing while they’re loading.”

“I know.”

“Say you’ll do it.”

“I’ll do it.”

Runa lay down again. Eydis went downstairs with her boots in her hand.

Svanhild had bread waiting. She cut a piece of cheese so thin that Eydis could see a crack of light through it, then cut another and laid it on top. Her grandmother wore the heavy pin and had put her hair up beneath a hood. She was going to be seen.

Outside, the village was dark except for a few windows. People moved towards the shore in small groups, speaking softly until they met someone they knew. At the landing a lantern hung from a post. Men carried the last bundles down, and a woman stood over a chest making certain it was placed where she wanted it.

The boat was small enough that Eydis could imagine being aboard, and large enough that the thought frightened her. Its mast seemed too tall when seen from close below. Beyond the sheltered water lay a dark opening in which nothing could be distinguished.

Hervi stood beside the master. He wore a different coat from the one in which he had brought them through the skerries, patched at the elbows and cinched tight. He looked pleased to see Svanhild, then less pleased when she asked him about the wind.

“It’ll do,” he said.

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“It is what I have.”

Eydis watched his mouth after he said it. He was making a joke, but his lips were dry.

A young man came up carrying a horn wrapped in cloth. He gave it to an older sailor, who unwrapped it and checked the rim with his thumb. The young man then stepped away and went behind the shed. Eydis heard him retch.

She looked at Svanhild. Her grandmother was watching the boat.

The sound came again. A woman near Eydis began speaking loudly about a missing glove. Someone answered her, also loudly. The young man returned wiping his mouth with the back of his hand. Nobody looked at him directly.

Eydis knew there would be a count. Svanhild had told her enough to make her curious, then refused to rehearse it in the house. At school Eydis had heard people describe such things as reckless customs, or old customs, or customs that ought to be replaced by a sensible inspection. The boat had already been inspected. She had watched the men checking lines and cargo in the lantern light. It did not appear to have relieved them much.

The master called the crew together. The others stood back, making a space. Eydis moved closer to her grandmother so she would not be told to move later.

The young sailor gave his name. He said where he meant to go and named the thing he feared: being taken over the side without anyone seeing him leave.

His voice caught on the last word. He cleared his throat and repeated it.

The older sailor held the horn out, but did not give it to him yet.

The count began among the people close enough to hear. Eydis knew where a household count would stop. She felt that stopping place approach, passed one word towards it with the others, then went silent. The young man’s face had changed. He was staring at the horn so hard that he seemed not to see the man holding it.

At the end he reached. The older sailor kept his grip until their eyes met.

Then the young man took the horn and sounded it.

The note broke at first. He blew harder, angrily, and it came clear enough to carry over the water. Eydis felt it through the wood under her boots. Someone shouted his name. The master clapped him on the shoulder. The space opened and became people again: a woman embracing him, a man asking for the horn back, another sailor telling him to get aboard before they lost the water.

Eydis realized she had been holding the edge of her grandmother’s sleeve.

Svanhild covered her hand briefly.

“He did it,” Eydis said.

“Yes.”

“Was he always going to?”

“I don’t know.”

The answer surprised her. It seemed that her grandmother should know, having watched so many people leave.

“Would they have gone without him?”

“Perhaps.”

“Would he have—”

“Watch now. They’re moving.”

The boat left slowly. A line came free with a wet slap. One man jumped aboard later than Eydis liked, caught the side and hauled himself over without grace. Everyone on the landing appeared to find this ordinary. A woman called something about the chest. The master answered with a hand raised in irritation.

Hervi guided them past the first stones. The young sailor stood where he had been put, pale and busy. The people on shore called out while faces could still be distinguished. After that they remained, watching a shape in the dark.

Eydis was cold by the time Svanhild turned away. She had expected to feel splendid, having seen a thing most girls at her school had only heard about. Instead she wanted breakfast again and was ashamed of wanting it.

Her grandmother bought hot broth from a woman who had carried a covered pot down in a barrow. They drank from shared cups, standing beside the shed. The broth tasted strongly of fish and pepper. Eydis burned her tongue.

“Slowly,” Svanhild said.

“I am.”

“You are telling me.”

Eydis smiled into the cup. Her mother used that phrase too, without ever seeming to notice where it came from.

People began talking about the departure. Already the young man’s note had been steadier in one telling than Eydis remembered. Someone said he had not hesitated. A woman answered that he had been looking for the right place to hold the horn, which sounded kinder and was not what Eydis had seen either.

She looked up at Svanhild.

Her grandmother was listening to another woman describe a sail repair. She did not intervene.

Beside the broth seller sat the woman who had embraced the young sailor, fastening a bootlace with stiff fingers. A little girl leaned against her knee, half asleep.

Svanhild asked whether they had eaten. The woman said the child had. She herself would eat at home. The broth seller, overhearing, filled another cup and put it beside her without asking for payment yet.

Eydis stood with her hands inside her sleeves. She wanted to ask how long the boat would be gone. She knew the question could not be answered exactly and could think of no other way to enter the conversation.

The little girl looked at her.

“Did you see him?”

“Yes.”

“He is my brother.”

Eydis nodded. The girl reached into a fold of her dress and produced a small piece of carved bone. One end had a notch through it; the other had been rubbed shiny by a thumb.

“He said I could keep it until he comes back.”

“What is it?”

“For the line. I think.”

Her mother looked down. “It was once. He spoiled it making that notch.”

“He gave it to me.”

“I know.”

The child closed her fist round it and leaned against her mother again. Eydis thought of the wooden bird with the missing wing upstairs. It had been given to her after it broke, and she had never considered that she had received a spoiled thing until that moment. She disliked the comparison and pushed it away.

Svanhild asked whether the woman wanted company on the first part of the walk. She said no, then admitted that she did. They waited while she drank the broth. Eydis watched the seller rinse the empty cups in a bucket. Svanhild still held hers, turning it between her hands for warmth.

The four of them walked up from the shore. The little girl had woken enough to complain about the cold. Her mother offered to carry her, but she wanted to walk unless somebody else offered, so Eydis took her hand. They stepped over the same puddles. At one place the child insisted that a patch of pale gravel was ice and must be crossed carefully. Eydis let her choose the steps.

Behind them the two women spoke of the things the sailor had left unfinished: a hinge, a sack meant to go to his uncle, a promise to mend his sister’s bird trap. Eydis could hear affection in the complaints. She had heard the man name his fear before everybody at the landing. It was strange to learn that he had also been the person who had not mended a hinge.

At the fork the woman took her daughter’s hand. The child thanked Eydis with the solemnity of someone who had been reminded too often to do so. Then she wanted the carved bone admired again. Eydis said the notch looked like a little mouth. The girl opened and closed her own mouth to match it and laughed.

Svanhild stood until the woman had turned towards home. Eydis moved closer to her. She no longer wanted another explanation of the count, but the question waited and came back as they reached the chapel path.

They walked home by the chapel. The door stood open. Astri was sweeping the threshold, making a dark fan of wet dust on the stones. She greeted Eydis and asked whether she would join the lesson after breakfast. Eydis looked to Svanhild, who told her to ask her mother.

The bell rang while they were still on the path.

Its strokes came evenly. Eydis counted because she had spent the morning counting, and because the sound filled the space between houses so completely that it was difficult to think of anything else. The last stroke followed the others without a pause or a gathering. Astri continued sweeping. A man carried a sack past the door. Her grandmother’s hand tightened around the empty broth cup she had forgotten to return.

“That was nine,” Eydis said.

“Yes.”

“But he did nine.”

“Who?”

“The man at the boat.”

Svanhild stopped walking. Eydis wished she could take the question back, though she did want the answer.

“He called for it,” her grandmother said.

“So did the bell man.”

“The bell man is telling people the hour.”

“He knows how many.”

“Yes.”

Eydis looked up at the tower. She could not see the person who had rung it. The opening was dark, and the bell itself had stopped moving.

“Then why is it different?”

Svanhild looked towards the shore, where the boat was no longer visible from this part of the path.

“If you stand before us and say where you are going, we can hear you. We know what you’ve asked. We can remember you asking it.”

“And him?” Eydis pointed at the tower.

“I don’t know what he thinks he’s asking.”

“Perhaps nothing.”

“Perhaps.”

They stood for another moment. Then her grandmother noticed the cup and swore softly. Eydis laughed with relief.

“I’ll take it back.”

“We’ll both take it back. Your mother will say I lost you before breakfast.”

At home Runa was dressed and looking through a stack of papers. She asked whether the boat had left safely. Eydis said yes, then stopped because it had only left. Her mother glanced up.

“What is it?”

“Nothing. It went.”

Svanhild set bread on the table. Runa moved the papers before crumbs could fall on them, and Eydis sat in the place left clear.

Later she went to the school. Astri gave her a slate and did not ask her to demonstrate anything. There were children younger than her working at letters, and two older boys learning to check a measure. Eydis liked the mixed ages. At her southern school, being the oldest person at a task usually meant one had failed it before.

During the quiet part she took out the scrap on which she had copied the pole man’s line. A girl beside her asked what it was.

“A sailing account.”

“Can you say it?”

“Yes.”

“Now?”

“Not all of it.”

The girl waited. Eydis whispered the opening, keeping the rhythm against her knee with a finger. The girl leaned closer. At the place where the cargo shifted, she opened her mouth a little, anticipating the crash.

Eydis remembered to wait.

Astri looked over. Both girls bent to their slates, smiling. Eydis wrote three words she did not want to lose and underlined the one that carried the turn.

After the lesson the girl asked if she would say the rest another day.

Eydis said she would.

She went home carrying the slate under her arm, trying the next lines to herself. At the door she stopped to wipe her boots without being told. Her grandmother was inside, talking to Oddrun. Eydis waited for a pause, then asked whether there was more paper she could use.

Part Two What the book admits

Chapter 9 A name on another page

Selka had cleared the table for her copy. There was a clean cloth over it, a cup weighted with pebbles to hold the paper flat, and a place left for Runa’s case. Runa felt underdressed for the occasion.

“You didn’t have to do this.”

“I have to do it for something.”

Selka took the sheet by its edges. The room was bright with snowlight. The first snow had fallen overnight, only a shallow covering, but enough to change the windows and make every muddy object indoors look newly dirty.

Runa read the entry aloud. Selka’s name, the house, nineteen years of residence, the present household. The copy did not assert title to the house. Runa had explained this twice already and said it again now, gently.

“I know.” Selka held a finger beside the line. “It says I live here.”

“Yes.”

“And they have that in the chapel.”

“Yes.”

“Then when his brother says nobody knows what belongs to whom, he can go and ask.”

“He may still disagree.”

“He has had years of practice.”

The boy with the pegs stood close enough to see. Selka made him read her name. He got the first part right and stumbled over the southern ending. She corrected him in the accent she ordinarily hid. Runa heard the difference and was ashamed to discover that she found it beautiful only when it appeared unexpectedly.

Selka folded the sheet along the lines Runa showed her. She put it with the old passage receipt, then brought both out again to compare the names.

“They wrote this one wrong.”

“On the old receipt?”

“My father would have been furious.” She smiled. “He was furious often enough without knowing about this.”

Runa drank the hot water Selka offered, flavoured with a little dried fruit. They spoke about Eydis’s boots and a seamstress who might have leather suitable for the uppers. For once the visit ended without a new question about the road.

Selka accompanied her as far as the gate. Snow had collected in the top of a broken post, and the boy knocked it out with a stick while the women spoke.

“Will they spell it that way in the south?” Selka asked.

“I’ve copied your spelling.”

“They may think you’ve made a mistake.”

“I can note that you confirmed it.”

Selka considered this. “Would it be troublesome to have the wrong one beside it? The one people here use.”

“Not troublesome.”

“Then do that. Otherwise someone will tell them I’m not the woman they want.”

Runa took out the paper again, sheltered it beneath the lid of her case and added the local form as an identifying variant. Selka watched the pen with the same pleasure she had shown indoors.

“My husband used to say names wore smooth in travel,” she said. “Like the corners of a box.”

“Did you like that?”

“I liked him.”

Runa glanced at her. Selka was smiling at something private, not asking the answer to settle more than it could.

“He tried the full name when he wanted something. Badly. You could always hear the request coming.” She looked towards the house. “Now his brother uses it when he is annoyed with me. He says it correctly, too.”

The boy struck the post again. A piece of rotten wood came away with the snow. Selka told him he had improved the snow and worsened the fence. He put the stick down and inspected the damage as if it had appeared without his participation.

They went back inside to sand the addition before folding it. Selka found a scrap of cloth to wrap the paper in. Runa noticed that the old passage receipt already had its own wrapper, worn almost through at the creases. She offered to make a clean transcript of that too.

“Would I keep the old one?”

“Of course.”

“Then yes.”

The work took another quarter hour. Runa had not allowed for it in the day’s visits. Selka brought the hot water back and sat beside her, supplying the uncertain letters of a place name she had not spoken aloud in years. Once she stopped Runa and went to the window. She said the name to herself twice, quietly, before coming back.

“That is it,” she said. “I’d begun to say it like him.”

Runa waited until she had finished spelling it. Outside, the boy began repairing the post with a strip of cord so obviously inadequate that both women saw it and neither interrupted him yet.

At the next house, the man who had refused the folder let her in because Oddrun had told him she would come alone.

He had put three small bowls of grain on the table. One showed what remained, one what he expected from a debt, and one what he could exchange if necessary. It was a useful arrangement. Runa asked him to explain it, then wrote the figures on a small scrap instead of opening the official form.

His wife stood at the hearth. She had the child’s shoe in her lap, sewing a patch over the toe.

“Do you need our names for this?” the man asked.

“For where the stores are.”

“You know where.”

“I may not be the person sent.”

He considered that. He gave the name by which his neighbours knew him and the house’s ordinary name. Runa did not ask whether either was the one under which he had been born.

The regional form did ask. Back at the chapel, she wrote long resident of Varnfjord in the origin space and left it there. Petros, passing behind her, asked whether he could borrow the ruler. He had no interest in that column.

Ivara’s entry was harder because Runa thought she understood it.

She returned as promised before supplying a copy to Dagrin. Ivara was away helping a neighbour, and the niece answered the door. The girl had flour on her wrist and a tired baby balanced against her hip. She gave Runa the missing quantity of meal and corrected the number of people expected after the next boat.

“Your aunt said four.”

“She thought my brother might come. He won’t now.”

“Where is he?”

“Still with Father’s people.”

Runa checked the note. The girl had been entered with Ivara, which was where she slept and ate, but the hall’s list assigned her to her father’s household for service. She explained the discrepancy. The girl glanced at the baby, then towards the neighbour’s house.

“I’m here.”

“Yes. I’ll make the residence clear.”

“Will you ask her?”

“I’ll leave a note. It’s just the affiliation for the two lists.”

Runa heard herself say just. She remembered it later with unpleasant precision.

She wrote that the girl resided with Ivara but belonged for service to her father’s house. It seemed the way to prevent duplicate counting. She left a message with the corrected meal quantity and took the working list to the hall, where Dagrin’s assistant copied the entries needed for cart duty.

Three days later Ivara came to the chapel while Runa was calculating the amount of gravel a cleared drain would expose.

She did not sit.

“They’ve sent for her.”

Runa put down the pen. “Who?”

“Her father’s brother. To work at his house until the winter dues are done.”

“Your niece?”

“You put her there.”

“I recorded the service affiliation already in the hall’s list.”

“She has been here since the baby was born.”

“I put that too.”

Ivara stared at her. Runa could hear a child coughing in the schoolroom. The cough went on long enough for Astri to pause the lesson.

“He says the new list confirms it,” Ivara said. “He has a copy. With your writing at the bottom.”

Runa looked down at the figures she had been adding. They were suddenly difficult to read.

“I’ll speak to Dagrin.”

“You were meant to speak to me.”

“I came.”

“I wasn’t there.”

There was nothing useful to put after that. Runa stood, knocking her knee against the table, and asked whether the girl had already gone.

“Tomorrow.”

“Can she refuse?”

“With what money?”

They went to the hall together. Dagrin received them between two other matters and listened with a patience Runa resented because it was genuine. He had not ordered the girl away. He had allowed the service list to be consulted by someone whose household appeared on it. The uncle claimed support he had given during a previous winter. That claim might be disputed at the proper gathering. Runa’s distribution record did not establish or annul it.

“Then tell him that,” she said.

“I can.”

“In writing.”

Dagrin looked at Ivara. “Will that help?”

“It will stop him saying she has decided it.” She pointed at Runa without looking at her.

The steward wrote a short clarification. It took less time than Runa’s correction had. Ivara accepted it and left immediately.

Runa stayed.

“You could restrict the copies.”

“To whom?”

“To the uses for which the information was gathered.”

“You brought me a list divided by service household.”

“For avoiding duplicate provision.”

“And I used the division you supplied.”

She could not accuse him of misunderstanding. She had written the affiliation because she expected it to be useful.

Back at the chapel she separated the original interview notes from the working distribution sheets. She made a list containing only quantities and delivery places. Names required for delivery remained. The service column went. Birthplaces went. She wrote a request to the regional office asking which details were indispensable for the survey and which belonged to any later levy agreement. The request would travel with the next messenger. Until then it lay beneath a stone on her table.

The circuit clerk, a spare young man assisting Ragnel, watched her prepare a clean copy.

“Won’t they ask for the missing information?”

“Yes.”

“Then you’ll have to do it again.”

“I may.”

He shrugged and went back to his own work.

Runa visited Ivara the following evening. The niece had gone to her uncle’s house for the day and would return late. The clarification had not ended the claim. It had made the uncle angrier about having to explain it.

Ivara gave Runa the details she asked for, then said she had washing to finish.

“Could I help?”

“No.”

Runa stood on the threshold with her hands empty. She had rehearsed an apology on the walk over and could no longer bear its careful shape.

“I should have waited for you.”

“Yes.”

“I knew I’d said I would.”

Ivara pulled the wet cloth through her hands. Water struck the tub in a steady stream.

“Then remember it next time.”

Runa went home. Her mother asked why she was late and she said she had made trouble for someone. Svanhild looked up from the fire.

“Torve?”

“No.”

Her mother waited. Runa wanted to tell her, and wanted fiercely not to hear what she would make of it. She took off her coat and asked whether Eydis had eaten.

Chapter 10 A useful lesson

The boy’s fingers would not close round the chalk.

Astri pretended not to notice until she had given everyone else something to do. Then she set a warm cup beside him and told him to hold it while she found another slate. The other slate was unnecessary. Runa could see a stack of clean ones within reach. The boy took the cup with both hands and kept his eyes down.

Eydis sat near the window, copying a passage. She was pleased to have her mother present and determined not to show it. Whenever Runa looked towards her, the girl’s back became a little straighter.

Runa had come to compare the household tallies with the number of children attending regularly. Astri had asked her to stay through the lesson, since the tallies would make more sense afterward. She sat by the wall with her case on her knees and tried not to count the minutes.

The younger children were making letters. The older ones read from a small book whose binding had been repaired with green thread. A girl began confidently, lost her place and repeated the same sentence twice. One of the older boys smirked.

Astri put a finger on the page beside him.

“You may help by keeping her place.”

He looked aggrieved, then embarrassed. He followed the next line with his finger. The girl continued. When she finished, Astri asked him to read the following passage, which was harder. His confidence diminished within three words. Nobody laughed.

Runa liked the teacher’s management of it. She remembered schoolrooms in which a mistake remained attached to a child for weeks.

The reading concerned a fisherman who hid a patron’s image beneath his boat. During a storm he promised whatever the sea demanded if his son returned. The son returned, but the father continued leaving offerings in secret, and each offering made him more afraid. At last a Temple brother showed him that fear had taught him to worship what could not love him. He brought the image into daylight, abandoned the practice, and slept without dreams.

Runa recognized the bones of an older telling. In her mother’s version the father had made a promise he would not name, and the son returned with a habit of standing at the water after everyone else had gone indoors. There had been no brother. Runa had disliked that version as a child because it ended before anything could be put right.

Astri asked what the fisherman had done wrong.

“He hid it,” said one boy.

“He promised before asking,” said a girl.

“He trusted a false power,” said the older boy who had smirked.

Astri nodded at the last answer, then explained the distinction she wanted. The fisherman had placed himself in bondage to fear. The Light did not demand a secret price for the return of a child.

Eydis put down her pen.

“What happened to the son?”

“He returned.”

“After that.”

“The father ceased making offerings.”

“But the son.”

Astri looked at the book. “The account concerns the father’s error.”

Eydis glanced towards Runa. Runa gave the smallest shake of her head, meaning not now. The girl took up her pen again.

Astri turned the book so the older children could see the passage together. The paper at the lower corner had been repaired with a narrow strip, leaving one word partly hidden. The boy who had given the approved answer offered to supply it.

“Mercy,” he said.

“Read what is there,” Astri told him.

He bent closer. The word was morning.

A few children laughed. Astri waited until they stopped, then asked why he had guessed mercy. He shrugged and went red.

“It sounded right.”

“It may sound right and still not be the word.”

Runa watched Eydis look down at her own page. The child had used that argument with Svanhild after the first lessons. Now she seemed uncertain whether she was permitted to enjoy hearing it from Astri.

The teacher set the book aside and brought out the measures. The lesson changed from reading to grain, and the room grew easier. A narrow vessel held the same amount as a broad one, though several children refused to believe it until each had poured for themselves. The smallest girl spilled pebbles between the two and wanted them counted as missing from the measure. Astri asked where they had gone. The girl pointed to the floor.

“Then we know where to begin.”

Runa helped find them. Kneeling beneath the bench, she discovered a bone button, a knot of thread and a strip of paper on which someone had written the same insult several times, improving the letters as they went. She slipped the paper onto Astri’s table face downward. The teacher turned it over, sighed, and put it in her sleeve.

Eydis came to count the recovered pebbles. Mother and daughter worked side by side, the girl’s earlier anger temporarily occupied by the task. Runa nearly asked whether she understood the difference between a religious lesson and a question about the old account. Eydis had found another pebble beneath the bench. Runa held out her hand for it.

When the bowls were filled again, Astri invited one child to make a false measure and another to discover how it had been done. The children enjoyed cheating at a task in which cheating was required. One packed the smaller stones tightly. Another kept a thumb inside the vessel while filling it. The oldest boy, who had smirked earlier, moved his thumb so deftly that the girl checking him did not see. He grinned until Eydis asked him to pour into her hand first.

The trick failed. Everyone laughed, including him. Runa felt affection for the whole troublesome room, and then remembered the son left standing at the water in the older telling. She looked for Eydis, who was already setting up the next trick.

The lesson continued. The boy with the cold fingers finished the warm drink and managed a crooked line on his slate. Astri praised the part that was better without pretending the whole letter was sound. Runa stayed to help clear the benches afterward.

“You do well with them,” she said.

“Most days.”

“I mean it.”

“I know. That is why I added most days.”

Astri stacked the books. Eydis waited by the door, cloak over one arm. On the walk home she asked why her mother had stopped her.

“You were making the lesson difficult.”

“I asked what happened.”

“It wasn’t the subject.”

“It happened to him.”

Runa stepped over a frozen rut. “Astri was trying to teach them something.”

“Grandmother was too.”

They walked several paces. The child’s anger was still quiet, which made it harder to dismiss as temper.

“Astri didn’t invent the book,” Runa said.

“She said it was the right one.”

“She believes it is.”

“Do you?”

Runa did not answer quickly enough. Eydis pulled on her cloak as they walked, almost catching its hem beneath her boot.

“Stop and fasten it.”

“I can.”

“Eydis.”

The girl stopped. Runa reached to do the clasp and Eydis turned her shoulder away. They walked the rest of the way with a space between them.

Runa went to the clinic after midday. She had questions about households unable to supply labour, and she had promised herself not to acquire the answers from someone else’s list without asking how they were used.

Tova met her at the door with a basin in both hands.

“If it’s the tally, wait.”

“I can help carry.”

“Then open the door behind you.”

Runa did. Tova took the basin outside, emptied it into a covered drain and scrubbed it before bringing it back. She asked Runa to fetch clean water. By the time Runa had done that, a child had begun crying in the inner room and a man on the bench wanted to know whether he could leave.

“No,” Tova told him.

“I have work.”

“So have I. Sit.”

He sat.

Runa remained long enough to see the rhythm of the place. People came with old injuries made worse by cold, coughs that had lasted too long, a hand cut by a broken pot. The Temple sister in charge examined each person. Tova cleaned, warmed, held and fetched, remembering who had been told to drink and who must not put weight on a foot. She spoke briskly to adults and softly to a frightened child, then abruptly reversed that pattern for an old man who needed gentleness and a girl who would not stop picking at a dressing.

The man on the bench tried to leave a second time while Tova was in the inner room. He had put on one boot and was struggling with the other when Runa looked round. He froze with his hand on the leather, as though she held any authority to keep him there.

“She said wait,” Runa told him.

“My ride won’t.”

“Where is it?”

“At the lower house. He came to bring fish. He won’t come back up if I’m not there.”

Runa put down the folded cloth she had been given to sort. The lower house was not far. She could tell the driver the man was delayed and ask him to wait. The man agreed at once, too eagerly, and she understood that he had been hoping somebody would make the journey since before she arrived.

Outside, the first snow had become wet along the middle of the path. A sledge stood by the house, half unloaded. The driver was inside arguing over the fish. Runa waited at the door until the host invited her in, then gave the message.

“How long?” the driver asked.

“I don’t know.”

“I have two more houses before dark.”

“He can’t walk that far.”

“He walked it coming.”

Runa had no answer to that until the host said the man had fallen on the last part and was ashamed to tell his nephew. The driver swore. He was the nephew. He put down the empty fish basket and asked which foot.

She did not know. She was beginning to understand why Tova had no time to go on these errands herself.

The nephew agreed to collect him after the second house, if the clinic could keep him warm until then. Runa carried the message back. The patient said his nephew fussed. She said he had asked which foot. The man removed the half-fastened boot and sat back on the bench.

When Tova came out, Runa explained the arrangement. Tova thanked her, then asked the patient why he had not mentioned the fall.

“I did.”

“To whom?”

He could not remember. Tova brought the sister. Runa went back to folding cloth and listened to the quieter voices while the examination continued. A little bell sounded from the next room. Tova answered it, leaving the sister with the man.

There were cloths which could be mended, cloths which needed washing and pieces too worn to keep for their present use. Runa sorted them badly at first because the clean colour made one thin strip appear better than it was. Tova held it against the window and showed her where light came through. After that she tested each piece with her fingers as well as her eyes.

A woman waiting with a child began helping. She had come because the child’s cough frightened her at night. In daylight the girl looked almost well, which made the mother feel foolish for bringing her. Runa did not tell her she was foolish or that everything would be all right. They folded side by side while the child made a small house from the rejected scraps.

The nephew arrived before they finished. He had brought the sledge to the door and left the remaining fish under cover. His uncle protested at being helped into it until the younger man told him he would tip him out again if that was what he preferred. The old man laughed and stopped resisting.

Runa went to hold the door. Tova wrapped the patient’s feet before letting him go. The nephew listened to the instructions, repeated the important part without prompting and tucked the cloth so it would not trail in the snow.

The mother with the coughing child watched them leave. When her own turn came, she went into the inner room without apologizing for having come. Runa took the little scrap house apart and put the pieces in the washing pile.

When there was a pause, Tova ate standing up.

“Eydis said we kept you from a hot dinner,” Runa told her.

Tova looked puzzled, then smiled. “Your mother did. The girl was good company.”

“She likes going with her.”

“I remember someone else who did.”

Runa leaned against the table. On a shelf behind Tova stood a pair of small bells used to call assistance from the next room. They were muffled with cloth so that only one sounded.

“About the watch,” Runa said.

Tova stopped eating.

“I need to know how it’s supported. For the road costs.”

“It isn’t.”

“The hall list has the keeper’s household.”

“My father died in spring.”

“I know. I’m sorry.”

“The fish came until the summer account. Then it stopped. They said the next keeper had to be confirmed.”

“And the shrine?”

“They said the household had kept it before anyone paid fish.”

“Did they ask you?”

“They told me where the rope was.”

Runa looked at the floor. She had seen the vacant watch on the survey and made a note to clarify responsibility. The neatness of that phrase troubled her now.

“How much were they providing?”

Tova named the allotment and the small additional payment when bad weather kept her father at the station through the night. It was little enough to seem easy to restore. Runa nearly said so, then thought of the family whose service entry she had corrected.

“I can put a funded watch into the maintenance estimate. Petros needs a warning arrangement for the crossing.”

“When?”

“With the larger agreement.”

Tova took another bite, chewed and swallowed.

“They said the next account to my father when the roof came down. He patched it himself. Then the next steward said there was no repair left to pay for.”

“I won’t promise you money I can’t authorize.”

“I know.”

Runa wished she had said that less defensively. Tova was not asking her to be a better person; she was finishing a short meal before someone needed her again.

“Would you want the work if it were paid?”

“Some of it. Not all of it. I am paid here now.”

“And you would need someone to share it.”

“Yes.”

They discussed hours and the weather in which a second person would be necessary. This part was useful. Runa wrote a request with the figures, and read it back. Tova corrected one quantity and added that the roof still needed attention.

From the inner room came the sister’s call. Tova wiped her hands and went. Her food remained on the table, half eaten.

At home Eydis was copying the sailing account. Runa sat beside her and asked to see the line about the son in the schoolbook, which the girl had remembered well enough to write on the margin of another page.

“I didn’t mean you shouldn’t ask,” Runa said.

Eydis kept looking at the paper.

“I meant I didn’t want the lesson turning into an argument.”

“She could have answered.”

“Yes.”

“Do you know?”

“What happened to him?”

The girl nodded.

“No.”

Eydis moved her elbow so there was room for her mother’s arm on the table. They read the copied line together. After a while Runa noticed that her daughter’s spelling had improved since the journey, and almost said so. She left it for another time.

Chapter 11 Winter hands

The fish had to be turned before the light failed, which meant that everyone who claimed to have another task was given a smaller basket and told they could leave after that.

Runa received a large basket.

“You can still count with cold hands,” Oddrun said.

“I can also leave.”

“You can.”

Oddrun set a knife beside the basket and went to instruct someone who had cut too close to the bone. Runa sat on an upturned tub. She had done this work often enough as a girl, and expected the old movements to return. They did, but slowly, with an awkwardness in the wrist that made her conscious of everyone else’s speed.

Svanhild sat beside her. She inspected the first fish Runa prepared and said it would do if the eater were patient.

“The bones are small.”

“Then perhaps they won’t notice them until morning.”

Runa took the fish back and worked along it with the knife tip. Her mother let her finish before handing her another.

The shed was full of people. Nets hung from the rafters to dry. At the far end Vigdis was helping an older woman repair a torn edge, pulling the line through with an expression of concentration entirely different from her father’s impatient stoop. Solli carried baskets out to the racks. Eydis had been entrusted with keeping the empty ones apart from those still needed, a responsibility she performed grandly until a woman told her to move because she was blocking the door.

Kolfin’s cousin, a convert, complained that the proposed road levy would fall on houses already supplying work. A shrine keeper answered that workers still needed feeding even if their employers had splendid convictions. Someone asked whether the keeper meant shrine workers or road workers. He said he meant anyone holding a shovel while another man explained why payment was improper.

Runa enjoyed him. She had expected a solemn man because she had only seen him at rites. Here he had fish blood on his sleeve and was losing an argument about the best way to stack baskets.

One of the women asked Runa whether the southern clerks wore gloves indoors.

“Some do.”

“To save their hands?”

“To keep warm.”

“We manage.”

“You complain all day,” Svanhild said.

“That’s how we keep warm.”

Runa laughed. The woman grinned and passed her a fish whose damage made it easier to prepare. It might have been a kindness or a comment on her skill. She accepted it as the former.

The teasing became less pleasant when a man at the door asked whether Runa’s fine hand could still gut anything without writing to ask permission. She began a reply, but Svanhild spoke first.

“She can do it sitting down. You’ve been in the doorway for an hour.”

He came in and took a basket. The conversation moved to his boots, which were new and too expensive for a man who claimed not to be able to pay a levy. Runa looked at her mother, who was already examining the next fish.

By midday Runa’s lower back hurt more than her hands. She stood to straighten it and was immediately asked to lift a basket. When she set it down, Oddrun pointed her towards the less glamorous work of sorting fish already dried too hard at one end and not hard enough at the other.

“Those first. They’ll spoil if people admire the good ones all day.”

Solli joined her. He was quieter than usual. A man from his foster father’s household had stopped at the shed to say a boat would need him when the weather opened, and he had spent the hour since then doing small jobs too quickly.

“Do you want to go?” Runa asked.

He shrugged.

“That is an answer to more than one question.”

“I want the pay.”

“And the passage?”

“I’ve been before.”

“So have people who don’t want to go again.”

He glanced towards the door. She regretted speaking where anyone could walk in and decide what his silence meant. She bent to the fish and let him choose whether to answer.

After a while he said, “If I stay, they call me the boy at Torve’s. If I go, I’m the boy who hasn’t been far enough.”

Runa separated a piece damaged by a split in the rack. “How far is enough?”

“Farther than I’ve been.”

He smiled, making the complaint a joke before she could pity him. She remembered doing that at his age, and was careful not to tell him so.

A woman brought more twine. She asked Solli to help with a heavy rack outside. He rose at once. Runa watched him take the weight, shoulders not yet broad enough for the confidence he put into them. When he came back he sat without admitting he needed a rest.

“Torve says he can teach me the next kind of joint this winter,” he said.

“That sounds worth staying for.”

“He says many things while he wants someone to hold the other end.”

Runa laughed. Solli relaxed, then offered an imitation of the boatwright telling a plank how disappointed he was in it. He had the voice well enough that Vigdis looked over and shouted that the plank deserved it. The shed laughed with them. Solli’s ears reddened, but he continued for another sentence before returning to work.

Runa remembered to ask him later whether the roster should count him through the winter. He said yes, for now, and she wrote that qualification rather than making his answer permanent.

A rack outside began to lean before the last baskets were carried to it.

Solli called through the door. Oddrun got up so quickly that Runa put down her knife and followed. The lower support had sunk into a soft place left by a recent repair to the yard. The rack had not fallen. One side of fish hung close enough to the ground for the dog to become interested.

“Get him away,” Oddrun said.

Eydis ran to the dog, which took her arrival as encouragement to play. She caught its collar, lost it and caught it again. Meanwhile Solli held the upright while two women lifted the lower crosspiece. Runa stepped in where she was told. The weight came gradually onto her arms as the women shifted their grip.

“Not higher,” Oddrun said. “Only hold.”

Runa held. Fish brushed her sleeve, cold and faintly greasy. She could hear Eydis bargaining with the dog behind her. The animal ignored every offer until the girl produced a crust from somewhere in her clothing. It followed her away at once.

Gerd was not there to find sound ground for them. The shrine keeper brought stones and was told to bring flatter ones. He objected that stone was not made to order. Oddrun said he could have a private word with whoever managed his stones, then, because the fish needed somewhere to stand.

He came back with better pieces. The laughter made it harder to keep the rack steady. Runa’s wrists began to tremble. Svanhild noticed and took her place without announcing the substitution. Runa crouched to help pack the base.

They reset the upright. When the weight came off their hands, everybody stayed close for a moment to see whether it would hold. It did. Oddrun inspected the lowest fish, rejected two that had touched the mud and took them inside separately.

The keeper shook his head at the spoiled pieces. “That dog would be useful now.”

“He is never useful on purpose,” Svanhild said.

Eydis returned without the crust and with a muddy sleeve. She was pleased with herself. Runa began telling her how to wash it before the dirt set. Her daughter looked at the fish slime along Runa’s own coat. Runa stopped.

“We’ll wash both.”

“And Grandmother’s.”

Svanhild examined her cuff. “Mine has survived worse than either of you.”

Back in the shed the work had fallen behind. Nobody said so directly. The baskets were simply nearer the door than they had been, the light farther from the place it needed to be. Runa took a smaller knife and returned to the difficult fish. Her hand found a quicker movement along the bone. She tried it again, carefully, and it worked.

Her mother did not praise her. She passed the next fish in the right direction for the new grip. After a while they were working without looking at each other’s hands.

A woman at the far end began a song whose refrain allowed everyone to join without stopping. Runa had forgotten the second verse. She found it after the others, came in late and was mocked by the man with the expensive boots. Svanhild gave him the third verse alone. He had forgotten that one too. The shed took it up around him until he could recover, and Runa discovered she was singing louder than she had meant to.

The last verse required two voices to disagree about a borrowed cow. Her mother took one. Oddrun took the other, making the cow considerably less respectable than Runa remembered. Eydis laughed so hard she dropped a basket. The basket was empty. They let her laugh.

They stopped for a meal while there was still light enough to finish the racks afterward. Someone brought a pot; someone else produced bread and a jar of sour berries. They ate wherever there was room to sit. Eydis perched on an empty basket and fell off when it tipped. She came up laughing, then stopped when she saw Runa watching to see whether she was hurt.

“I’m all right.”

“I can see.”

“You were going to ask.”

“Yes.”

Vigdis offered her a place on the bench. The two girls bent over a piece of line, and Vigdis began showing her a knot. Runa watched without joining them.

Torve came in late. His hands smelled of pitch even after he washed them. He took the place beside Oddrun, who had put down a bowl for him without asking. The hearing had been set for the following week. Nobody mentioned it at first.

Then the shrine keeper asked whether a title from the synod would let Torve charge people to spread their nets.

“I’ve never charged you.”

“You’ve never had a title from the synod.”

“I don’t have one now.”

“But if you did?”

Torve ate a spoonful before answering. “I’d like people to ask before using the slip when I’ve a boat coming down it.”

“We do,” Oddrun said.

“Some of you.”

“Some of your boats come down when you said they wouldn’t.”

“Because boats don’t read my promises.”

The conversation drew laughter, but Oddrun kept watching him. Runa remembered the sheds she had seen beside the chapel path. There was less room on the shore than there had been. A right that everyone had exercised casually might become valuable when it had to fit between buildings.

“Where do you usually spread them?” she asked.

Oddrun took the line from Eydis and made a loose shape across the table.

“Here, when the wind comes that way. Here when it doesn’t. That part needs to stay open for turning a boat. This is where we set the stones.”

Torve moved one loop with a finger. “You set them farther in.”

“Only when your timber is there.”

“My timber has to be somewhere.”

“So do the nets.”

Nobody resolved it. The pot was empty, and work remained. Oddrun returned the line to Eydis and showed her how to undo the knot she had made accidentally while listening.

Runa spent the afternoon tying rather than cutting. She was good at following a pattern once someone showed her the first few turns. The older woman beside her corrected the tension twice, then left her alone. By the end of the light they had finished more than Runa expected.

On the way home her hands smelled so strongly of fish that Eydis walked ahead of her, claiming she could detect them from three paces. Runa caught her and put a hand under her nose. The girl shrieked, laughing, and ran to Svanhild for protection. Her grandmother held out her own equally fragrant hand.

At home they washed with warm water and ash soap. Runa’s fingers stung in the places where the knife handle had rubbed. Eydis stood beside her, examining a small rope burn with more interest than pain.

“Do you remember Mistress Venna’s sleeve?” the girl asked.

Runa did. A teacher at Eydis’s old school had caught her wide sleeve in a door handle while attempting to leave a room with great dignity. Runa had been waiting outside and had tried to free it without either of them acknowledging what was happening. The cloth would not yield. The teacher had continued telling the children to attend to their books while making small backward movements towards the door.

Eydis began imitating those movements. Runa tried not to laugh because she had forbidden the imitation at the time.

“You promised.”

“I promised not to do it at school.”

“That was not the full promise.”

“It was the part I remember.”

Runa splashed water at her. Eydis ducked, and the water struck Svanhild’s apron as she came past with a cup.

Her mother looked down, then at the two of them.

“Good. You can wash the floor while you’re at it.”

Eydis began to explain the sleeve. Halfway through, Runa had to sit down because she was laughing too hard to stand comfortably on the wet boards.

Chapter 12 The phrase she kept

Eydis had imagined applause, but not the silence before it.

She had dressed too carefully for a visit that was supposed to be ordinary. The blue ribbon came out twice, once because it made her look younger and once because her grandmother said nothing about it. At last she put it back in her hair and went downstairs. Svanhild was wrapping the borrowed bowl in a cloth. She looked at Eydis’s hair, smiled and asked whether she meant to carry the bowl or herself.

“Both.”

“That will take care.”

On the walk they met two women bringing mending to the same house. One asked whether Eydis had learned the account of the bowls. Eydis said she had learned most of it. Her grandmother said most was plenty if she remembered where the boat was. The women laughed, and Eydis thought this meant the afternoon would go well.

The house was warmer than theirs, with a low hearth and a window covered by thin horn that turned the daylight yellow. A child had arranged small stones on the sill, largest to smallest. The last stone was missing. Eydis wondered whether it had been lost or left off deliberately, then was distracted by someone handing her a sweet piece of bread.

Maret arrived while they were discussing the school. Eydis heard her name without keeping it. The woman set down a basket, greeted Svanhild and took a chair near the window where she could see to sew. She asked the girl whether she was enjoying the winter. Eydis said yes and immediately felt the need to prove it by naming three things. Maret listened, smiled at the account of the fish shed, and began work on a sleeve.

The little boy under the table had lost his wooden fish between the hearthstones. He tried to retrieve it with his fingers until the host told him to stop reaching near the fire. Eydis knelt to look. From her side she could see the fish’s tail. A thin stick might bring it out.

Svanhild gave her one from the kindling and made her wait while the host moved the little pot out of the way. The boy wanted to direct every movement. Eydis told him she could see it. He said she was pushing it farther. She was, though only a little.

Maret put down her sewing and showed her where to lift instead of push. The fish came free with a coating of ash. The boy wanted it at once. Eydis held it back until it had cooled, enjoying being the sensible person while he complained.

“Give it a breath,” she told him, using her mother’s voice.

The women laughed. Encouraged, she added the hand movement Runa made when a question interrupted her writing. It brought more laughter. Svanhild looked at her sharply and then smiled, which felt like permission.

Eydis gave them Mistress Venna caught by the sleeve.

She did not need to explain the whole school to make it work. The little backward step, the chin kept high, the attempt to continue instructing while being held by the door: the room understood before she reached the part where Runa tried to help. Her grandmother covered her mouth. Maret laughed with her head bent so that Eydis could see the straight part in her hair.

The fish was cool. The boy accepted it and said the lady should have taken her dress off. That made everybody laugh again, and Eydis had the pleasant sense that he had extended her success rather than stolen it.

When she returned to her stool, Svanhild touched her shoulder.

“Do not show that to your mother while she is trying to correct you.”

“I wouldn’t.”

“You might be tempted.”

Eydis was already imagining the temptation. She ate the last of the sweet bread and watched Maret pick up her sewing. She liked all of them. It seemed impossible that an afternoon which had begun this well could become uncomfortable.

There was a patch of quiet in which everyone could hear the rain against the shutter. The host asked Svanhild for a little of the southern voyage. Svanhild looked at Eydis instead.

“She has it fresh.”

Eydis’s stomach changed. She wanted to stand and wanted the request withdrawn. Her grandmother did not make it easier by announcing that a child must be indulged. She moved her own chair to give the listeners a clear view.

Eydis straightened her skirt. There was a crumb caught in her throat. She took water, swallowed twice and stood where Svanhild indicated. The paper in her pocket pressed against her hip. She had brought it because writing could not forget, and now wished she had left it at home so no one would know she needed help.

“From the rain?” she asked.

“From where it begins,” her grandmother said.

A listener smiled. Eydis took the first breath.

Six people were listening. Seven if she counted the little boy under the table, who had stopped pushing a wooden fish between the chair legs. The boy had a smear of honey on one cheek. He had not noticed.

Her grandmother sat near the hearth, one foot drawn back under the chair. Eydis could see the worn place on the toe of her shoe. She fixed her eyes there for the first line, then made herself look up.

The listeners knew the boat. They knew the mast, the bowls, the pole man. Eydis heard small sounds of recognition as she passed each familiar place. At the pause before the cargo shifted, she waited just long enough. The little boy looked up from beneath the table.

She felt the room come with her.

The dead sailor’s part followed. He had been sent to secure the cargo and had failed to do it before the storm. Svanhild’s teaching version gave him a sharp description, the man who kept his hands warm while others hauled. Eydis liked the line because its sounds sat well together. She delivered it clearly.

A woman beside the window lifted her head.

Eydis went on, but her grandmother moved.

“Who kept his place,” Svanhild said, softly.

Eydis stopped.

“What?”

“The line. Who kept his place while others hauled.”

“That isn’t what you said.”

Her grandmother’s face did not change much. It changed enough for Eydis to notice, and to feel a small, fierce certainty that she could prove herself.

“I wrote it down.”

“Finish the account.”

“But I have it.”

She unfolded the paper. Her hands were suddenly clumsy with eagerness. She found the line, showed the little mark before it, and read it aloud again. The words sounded harsher spoken without the surrounding rhythm.

The woman by the window stood.

“Do I need to leave for you to settle which insult to use?”

Eydis looked at her.

Svanhild said the woman’s name, quietly. It was Maret. Eydis had been introduced when they arrived, but had not connected her with the dead man. There had been no reason to, except that everyone else seemed to know.

“My grandfather,” Maret said. “Since no one has told you.”

The paper sagged in Eydis’s hand.

“I didn’t—”

“I know you didn’t.”

Maret picked up the shawl she had left over a chair. Svanhild rose, and Maret held out a hand without touching her.

“You can speak to me later. I won’t stand here while you explain me to the child.”

She left. The door admitted a cold gust. The little boy crawled out from beneath the table and went to his mother.

Someone asked Eydis whether she wanted a drink. She shook her head. Her grandmother sat down again, with more care than before.

“Go on,” she said.

Eydis could not.

She looked at the line below the one she had proved. It concerned the boat’s turn, something she knew well. The words might have been written by another person. She folded the paper, first correctly and then crookedly, and put it in her pocket.

The visit continued badly. Adults talked about a roof repair, making more effort than the subject required. Eydis sat beside Svanhild and wished someone would be angry at her, so that there would be a clear thing to endure. Her grandmother accepted a cup and drank half of it. When they finally left, Eydis had to remind herself to thank the host.

In the doorway the host gave Eydis the bowl they had brought back for Maret, filled now with something for Svanhild to take home. For a moment the girl could not understand why a returned thing was being sent away again. Then she took it because the woman was holding it out.

“Careful on the step.”

Eydis nodded. She wanted ordinary speech to help and found that it made the afternoon feel more unbearable. People could still discuss food while Maret was gone and the paper lay wrong in her pocket.

Svanhild spoke a farewell to the room. Her voice sounded like itself. Eydis wondered whether that took effort. Outside, her grandmother reached for the bowl and Eydis would not release it.

“I can carry.”

“I know you can.”

She held it all the way to the gate, though the warmth through the cloth had begun to hurt her fingers. At the gate Svanhild opened the latch and waited. Eydis stepped through, stumbled on the uneven stone and nearly dropped it. Her grandmother steadied her by the elbow.

Neither mentioned the stumble.

They walked home without talking until the bend in the path.

“Why did you tell me that line?” Eydis asked.

Svanhild kept walking.

“Grandmother.”

“Because it is a line in the account.”

“Then why did you change it?”

“Because Maret was there.”

“Was it wrong?”

“What?”

“The first one.”

Her grandmother stopped so abruptly that Eydis took another step and had to turn back.

“Must everything be settled before we get through the door?”

Eydis’s eyes filled. She hated it. Crying would make the question look like something else.

Svanhild saw and softened, but not enough to put an arm round her.

“You could have let the correction stand.”

“You said to remember it.”

“I said to listen.”

“I did.”

They stared at one another. Eydis had never been as angry with her grandmother as she was then. It felt disloyal and strangely adult. She wanted Runa, and did not want Runa to hear any part of it.

At home she went upstairs. She took out the copied sheets and spread them on the bed. The earlier marks were still there: wait here, breathe here, do not let the ship turn before the sail is taken in. Her grandmother had leaned over these same pages and helped her. Nothing in the writing showed where Maret would stand up.

Eydis gathered the sheets and put them beneath the mattress.

Runa came up later. She sat on the opposite bed and asked how the visit had gone.

“Badly.”

“Your grandmother said there was a misunderstanding.”

“She changed a line.”

“Sometimes a performer changes—”

“I know.”

Her mother stopped. Eydis kept her hands flat on her knees so they would not betray how hard she was trying not to cry.

“May I read it?” Runa asked.

“No.”

A small silence followed. Her mother looked hurt, which was unfair because Eydis had not done anything to her.

“All right.”
