Runa remained a moment longer, then stood. At the stair she asked whether Eydis wanted supper upstairs or below.

“Below.”

“We’ll call.”

After she had gone, Eydis lay down without undressing. Through the floor came the sound of Svanhild moving a pot. Her grandmother began humming a tune, stopped halfway through, and did not begin again.

Eydis pressed her face into the pillow. She wanted the room back as it had been before the line, with the little boy looking up and everyone waiting for her to bring the boat home.

Chapter 13 Before the candle

Torve arrived early enough to see the room prepared and regretted it.

Runa watched him standing beside the door while the circuit clerk arranged the papers. Every movement seemed to remind him that other people knew what would happen here. He had dressed in a good tunic which fitted badly across his shoulders. Vigdis stood beside him carrying the sailcloth bundle. She had tied it with an ordinary cord this time; the good line had returned to the yard.

“Where do we sit?” he asked.

The clerk showed him. Torve looked at the bench, then remained standing until his daughter sat.

There were two candles on the table. One was for light, the other for the witness observance. Ragnel had explained the distinction before the day. When a man asked whether the second meant a shrine adherent could not speak, she said it meant his words were being given before the court. He could affirm them according to his own practice, provided the undertaking was understood and recorded. The man seemed disappointed to have lost an objection he had prepared.

Svanhild sat among the listeners. Runa had saved no place for her and was relieved that someone else had. Their eyes met briefly across the room. Her mother lifted her chin, neither encouraging nor warning.

Dagrin entered with the estate accounts. Ingrith came after him. The room shifted as people stood or made space. She took a chair at the side rather than at the hearing table. Her presence made the bench beneath Runa feel narrower.

Ragnel began by reading the reference aloud.

The circuit clerk read it again in Linn, then asked both parties whether that was the matter they had agreed to submit. Torve said yes. Dagrin said yes on behalf of the estate. Ingrith confirmed his authority with a short answer which required no ornament.

Runa wrote the confirmations. Her hand steadied once it had work.

The first hour concerned service. Dagrin described the boats assigned to the yard and the benefits Torve’s household received in return. He did not call the boatwright incompetent. He argued that the hall’s right to allocate its work included the right to allocate the place in which that work was done. The holding had always served the estate. Continued occupation could not become an independent claim merely by lasting a long time.

Ragnel asked which document gave the estate the power described.

Dagrin produced a letter from an earlier steward. It instructed Torve’s father to make a repair and reminded him of the landing’s benefits. The letter was read aloud. Torve interrupted at the mention of unpaid service, saying the timber had been supplied late.

“We will hear that,” Ragnel said. “Let the letter finish.”

“It makes it sound—”

“Let it finish, Torve.”

He closed his mouth. Vigdis moved the bundle farther under the bench with her foot.

The letter did not state a power of eviction. Dagrin argued that such a power need not be stated in every instruction. Ragnel agreed that no single letter could be expected to contain the entire arrangement. She asked for the rest.

They went through payments, repairs, periods of delay and work accepted. A year in which no boat had been supplied turned out to be a year in which the estate had provided no timber and used the yard for storage. Another apparent gap was accounted for by a payment entered under the vessel’s master rather than the boatwright. Runa found the cross-reference in her notes and passed it to the clerk without speaking. He checked it before drawing Ragnel’s attention.

Torve began to settle. Evidence he had kept in a chest for years was being handled as if it had been worth keeping. He answered more slowly.

The question of a missing payment drew Vigdis’s board out of its wrapper.

Dagrin’s account showed the estate paying for two lengths of timber which the yard’s record appeared to charge again. Torve said one pair had been unfit. The steward asked why both pairs had been entered at the same length. Torve began describing the repair from memory, using his hands to show the curve of the hull. Runa knew the gesture well by now. The circuit clerk did not.

Vigdis put the narrow board across her knees.

“Those are mine,” she said.

Her father stopped.

Ragnel asked what she meant. Vigdis explained that she had made the marks when the replacement timber arrived. The old and new lengths were the same because the order had been for the same repair. What differed was the timber returned. She pointed to the crossed mark beside one pair.

The clerk came round to look. He asked whether the crossing-out meant the delivery had been cancelled. Vigdis said it meant the pieces had gone back.

“To whom?”

“To the men who brought them.”

“Do you have their names?”

She looked towards Torve. He supplied one. She shook her head, very slightly. He saw and checked himself.

“His brother?”

“The younger one.”

Dagrin consulted a receipt. The younger brother had acknowledged taking timber back, but the acknowledgement lay under a different vessel’s work. Runa watched him find it. He did not look pleased, though he laid it before the judge immediately.

Ragnel asked the clerk to read it. The timber had been returned for replacement, not bought a second time. She had the cross-reference entered.

Then she asked Vigdis whether the same mark was always used for returned material.

“On my board.”

“By everyone at the yard?”

“No.”

Torve shifted. The girl kept her eyes on the judge.

Ragnel nodded. “Then we must not use it to interpret the other boards without asking.”

Vigdis seemed surprised by the limit, then relieved. She had been allowed to be certain about what she had done without being made responsible for everything resembling it. She sat back. Runa wrote the explanation as the circuit clerk gave it, resisting the urge to make the small record sound more formal than the evidence.

Dagrin asked to examine the board once more. Vigdis handed it over. He traced the marks without touching the charcoal, then returned it and said that cleared the entry. Torve wanted to know how many other entries would clear if the estate kept its receipts together. Ragnel told him this one had been questioned and answered.

The next did not favour the yard. A small advance remained unaccounted for. Torve said the work had been done. No one present remembered receiving it. Runa found the promise in a letter but no acceptance. She had hoped the papers would produce one more neat reversal; they did not.

Ragnel had the unresolved advance kept in the record without treating it as proof of a breach sufficient to end occupation. Dagrin reserved the estate’s right to seek an account. Torve agreed angrily. Vigdis folded her board away.

The witness candle guttered while the clerk was speaking. He stopped to trim it, and for a moment everyone watched the small domestic act with more attention than it required. Runa flexed her hand beneath the table. She had begun to feel the morning’s length in the muscles between her fingers.

Then Ragnel asked Torve whether he had ever accepted compensation for a change in the holding, and he said no.

Dagrin immediately produced an account of payment for moving a store shed.

“That wasn’t the holding,” Torve said.

“It was a part of the yard,” Dagrin replied.

“It was in the way.”

“And you accepted payment to move it.”

“Because you wanted it moved.”

Ragnel let the exchange run long enough to establish the facts, then stopped it. She asked Torve to describe the shed, where it had stood and where it had been put. Vigdis answered one detail before remembering she had not been called. Her father told her to wait. Ragnel asked her to continue.

The girl stood. She kept one hand on the edge of the bench.

“The shed moved. We still used it. We moved the benches with it, and Father made a different door because the old one faced the wet.”

“How old were you?”

Vigdis gave the season, then corrected herself by a year. She looked frightened by the correction. Ragnel asked what helped her remember.

“My mother was still alive. It was before the winter she couldn’t come to the yard.”

Torve looked down at his hands.

The payment had compensated labour and materials. It had not ended occupation. Ragnel had the distinction entered, then called a halt so the clerk could catch up.

People moved in the room. Someone opened the door to let out the heat and admitted too much cold. Runa flexed her fingers. Svanhild came to the table with a cup for her, setting it well away from the ink.

“Drink.”

“Thank you.”

Her mother looked at the pages. “You have written a great deal.”

“There has been a great deal said.”

“Yes.”

Neither made more of it. Svanhild returned to her place.

During the pause Torve went outside. Runa saw Vigdis follow and was relieved that the girl had chosen to. She remained at the table checking the order of the letters until Dagrin came to ask whether one payment had been entered twice.

It had not. The second entry acknowledged receipt of a balance. She showed him the different dates and the word indicating the balance. He accepted the correction, then hesitated.

“I did not bring this to ruin him.”

Runa looked up.

“Then withdraw the notice.”

“I have no authority to do that on my own.”

“Ask for it.”

“You think I haven’t spoken to her?”

The question caught her. Dagrin glanced towards Ingrith’s empty chair. The jarl had gone to another room during the pause. Her steward’s expression closed before Runa could decide what had been disclosed.

“The western yard has men without enough work. Kolfin has a household too,” he said. “A coast is more than the people standing nearest your table.”

“I know.”

“You know it when it is said. I have to arrange it when no one is saying it.”

He returned to his place. Runa sat with the corrected payment before her. He had not answered the objection to eviction. He had made it harder for her to imagine that every person pressing it had never considered anyone else’s winter.

Vigdis came back alone. She put her father’s papers in order, following a sequence Runa had shown her the previous evening. The girl’s face was pale.

“Is he coming?” Runa asked softly.

“Yes. He wants air.”

“Do you need anything?”

Vigdis shook her head, then changed her mind. “If they ask me again, do I have to stand?”

“You can ask to remain seated.”

“Will that look weak?”

Runa thought of saying that no reasonable court would judge her for it. She had seen enough rooms to distrust the promise.

“Say you can answer better sitting down. Ragnel will hear that.”

The girl nodded. When Torve returned, she shifted along the bench and left him space without looking at his face. Runa saw him touch her shoulder before he sat.

The witnesses followed. An older boatman described repairs accepted without complaint. A former storekeeper confirmed that the estate had supplied timber irregularly and expected the yard to wait. Svanhild gave the account she had given at the earlier gathering, with fewer flourishes because the clerk needed to repeat it. Ragnel asked whether she understood the phrase concerning service to permit removal without a failure of service.

Svanhild took time before answering.

“I have not heard it used that way before.”

“Have you heard it expressly forbid it?”

“No.”

Torve made a sound under his breath. Ragnel glanced at him but did not interrupt the witness. Runa kept writing. She wanted to look at her mother and could not do both accurately.

When everyone had spoken, Ragnel asked for the letters once more. She read in silence while the room endured the particular discomfort of waiting for someone who was not performing their thought aloud.

A child outside began knocking a stick against the wall. Someone went to stop him. Ingrith changed the position of her feet. Vigdis picked at the loose edge of the sailcloth until Torve put a hand over hers.

Ragnel gave the ruling without raising her voice.

The submitted evidence established an ongoing occupation maintained through service accepted by the estate. The present notice alleged no breach sufficient to end it. Reassigning the work did not, by itself, extinguish the boatwright’s occupation. The estate could pursue a breach if it believed one existed, with evidence and the opportunity to answer. It could negotiate a different arrangement. It could not use this notice to remove the household at discretion.

She then stated what she had not decided: descent of the holding, the full extent of the old grant, rights over shared shore, and future allocation of estate boat work except where that allocation was being used as the sole grounds for eviction.

Torve remained still.

Runa knew he had heard the victory, but not yet how much room there was around it. Dagrin asked a question about the small shed. Ragnel answered that the present occupation remained until lawfully altered; the move proposed in the notice could not be compelled on its present basis.

Ingrith rose.

“I guaranteed the submitted award,” she said. “It will be observed.”

Her anger was visible in the care with which she put on her gloves. She spoke to Dagrin, not to Torve, about obtaining a full copy. Then she left.

The room released its breath unevenly. People wanted to congratulate Torve and ask the judge questions about unrelated claims. Ragnel refused the latter, politely at first and then with the weariness of a woman who had been sitting for hours. Torve thanked her. He stumbled over the form of address, began again, and finally said he was grateful she had heard the papers.

“I heard you as well,” she said.

He nodded, but it was the papers he gathered first.

Runa stayed to check the record. By the time she took a copy down to the yard, daylight had begun to fail. Vigdis was carrying tools from the small shed back to the main bench. Solli helped, asking where each belonged and being told that it belonged where it had been before he moved it.

Torve stood beneath the hull with a lamp.

“The ruling,” Runa said.

He wiped his hand and took it.

“Where do I put this?”

“Somewhere dry.”

He gave her a look. She laughed, embarrassed.

“With the old letters.”

Vigdis took the sheet from him and carried it indoors. On the way back she brought the measuring cord she had knotted for the benches. She wound it round her palm, then put it on a hook.

The next morning Svanhild said, while cutting bread, that turning them out would have been wrong.

Runa looked up.

“Then why didn’t you say so at the first hearing?”

“I said what the account was.”

“And yesterday?”

“The judge asked another question.”

Her mother put a piece of bread on Eydis’s plate. The girl looked from one woman to the other and bent over her bowl.

Runa took the bread she was offered. She had a copy to deliver and three calculations to finish. For that morning she let the answer stand.

Chapter 14 What the road includes

By the dark middle of winter the road had acquired a workshop in Runa’s house.

It began with one drawing brought home because the chapel lamp had gone out. Then came a measuring cord that needed drying, a list of timber prices and three pegs Eydis mistook for firewood. Svanhild put the rescued pegs in a cup and asked whether they required an honoured place at dinner. Runa promised to clear everything in the morning. In the morning the cord was still wet.

One evening mother and daughter worked side by side at the table, Runa comparing quantities while Svanhild mended the edge of her performance cloak. Eydis had fallen asleep upstairs. Snow pressed against the lower shutter. The house was quieter than the southern rooms Runa rented, where another family’s footsteps always passed above her own.

Svanhild asked what the finished road would look like.

Runa showed her. She used the backs of spoons for the houses and a piece of cord for the gut, laughing when her mother put Torve’s cup exactly where the new crossing would be.

“He will object,” Svanhild said.

“He objects now.”

“He does it well.”

For a few minutes they arranged the little valley together. Svanhild moved one spoon to account for a path Runa had omitted. Runa showed where the higher bank would carry the winter haul. They disagreed about the distance between two houses, then discovered that one had been rebuilt since Runa left.

“Things do happen while you’re away,” her mother said.

“I had begun to suspect it.”

Svanhild smiled and returned to her sewing. Runa left the spoons in place a little longer. Looking at them was easier than looking at the figures which would turn the route into work someone had to pay for.

The next morning the complete proposal arrived wrapped in cloth that had once held something aromatic. Whenever Runa turned a page she caught the faint smell, warm and dry, entirely unlike the room in which she read it.

Petros sat opposite, working through the quantities. The messenger was asleep in the schoolroom, boots beside the stove. He had come by land for the last part of the journey and brought news of a broken axle, two marriages and a harbour quarrel he found more interesting than either. Runa had sent him food before opening the packet.

She knew the separate documents. The revenue estimate had passed through her office in the south. The jurisdiction schedule used familiar language. The household liability form was the one she had carried north. Together, attached to Petros’s costs and the proposed district boundary, they looked larger than she remembered.

She read the schedule twice.

Petros looked up when she stopped turning pages.

“Which part?”

“The levy.”

“They’ve kept the amount.”

“They’ve extended the period.”

“For maintenance.”

“And put default under the district court.”

“Yes.”

She laid the sheet flat between them. “The whole district.”

He leaned over it. “Contract default.”

“The jurisdiction schedule isn’t confined to this contract. It establishes the court for the incorporated district. These are linked.”

Petros read. He frowned, then reached for the covering letter.

“They said the works would require a stable authority.”

“They have provided one.”

Neither spoke for a while. Water dripped into the bucket beside the wall. Runa had grown used to that sound, hearing it only when it changed.

She went through the proposal aloud. The advance would cover the larger road, permanent work at the gut, maintenance stores and a credit allowance for grain. In return, the district would accept a uniform levy, a household roll establishing liability, and defined synod jurisdiction over a wider class of disputes than the harbour compact. Officials would have power to pursue default through property and future receipts. Some protections limited seizure; others depended on the district meeting its own obligations on time.

Petros asked whether the local Thing would disappear.

“No. Not for everything.”

“For what, then?”

She found the schedule and read the exceptions. Hearing herself list them, she understood why the question mattered more than the reassuring answer she had given first.

“Did you know this was required?” he asked.

“I knew these were the usual provisions.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

She looked at him sharply. He held her gaze, then rubbed his forehead.

“I’m sorry. I’ve been asking myself the same thing.”

They drafted questions for the finance office. Some answers could be found in the papers. The rest would have to travel south and back. Petros refused to lower the estimate simply to make the offer smaller. He could design a lesser road, but it would be a lesser road.

Runa agreed. She did not want a cheap number concealing expensive work.

The messenger woke while they were still drafting. He came in with one boot on and asked whether anyone had moved the other.

It stood beneath the stove. Petros had shifted it away from the hottest place when the leather began to smell. The messenger thanked him, pulled it on and asked whether he would be returning with a reply that day.

“No,” Runa said.

“Tomorrow?”

“We need to consult the jarl.”

He sat down. His name was on the covering receipt, but Runa had looked at the arrival time and the seal before looking at it. She found it now, then decided that using it suddenly would make an uncomfortable display of attention.

“How long can you stay?”

“How long will you pay?”

She checked the allowance. He watched her finger move down the column.

“I can lodge at the store if this room is needed. I can’t eat a promise all the way back.”

She gave him the number of days she could authorize without writing south. He considered it, asked whether he would be expected to carry other packets on the return, and agreed when she said their weight would be recorded separately.

Then he unwrapped the news he had brought. The marriages required people Runa did not know. The axle concerned the road. The harbour quarrel had interrupted the packet’s journey because a receiver had refused delivery until someone witnessed a broken seal on an unrelated parcel. Runa asked how long that had cost. The messenger said half a day and a meal for which nobody yet considered himself liable.

Petros laughed. The messenger did not.

“I ate it,” he said. “I can remember eating it. Somebody can decide whose fault it was without waiting for me to go hungry again.”

Runa entered the meal under journey expenses. He watched until she had finished. Then he asked where he could wash.

She showed him the basin and went outside for more water. The sky had begun to clear. Beyond the chapel wall two boys were making a slide on a patch that somebody else had scattered with grit that morning. They looked up guiltily when she passed. She warned them about the step, then kept going. They resumed as soon as her back was turned.

At the well Astri was speaking to the widow who had begun attending lessons in the evening. The woman held a scrap with three words on it. She had written them herself and wanted to know why one looked different in the book. Astri compared the letter shapes. Runa stood with the bucket while the explanation took longer than either teacher or pupil expected.

The widow laughed at a sound she had given the word. Then she tried again, carefully. This time she got it. She folded the scrap and tucked it away with obvious pleasure.

Runa waited until she had gone before mentioning the district terms. Astri listened, drawing water while Runa held the bucket steady.

“Will the school be maintained?”

“It is included in the provision.”

“And if they refuse?”

“The first allowance has a term. After that we need another arrangement.”

Astri stopped turning the handle.

“How long?”

Runa told her. The teacher looked towards the path the widow had taken.

“Don’t make me tell them the letters were a trial.”

“I haven’t asked you to.”

“No. I am asking you.”

They lowered the water into Runa’s bucket. Astri steadied the rim until it stopped swaying. When Runa began to explain the options, the teacher said she had children waiting and would read the papers after the lesson. Runa carried the bucket back with the explanation still forming in her mouth.

She wrote the school provision on a separate sheet. She did not remove its end date.

Ingrith summoned her to explain the proposal publicly before the reply went south. The jarl had read enough to know that people would accuse her of surrendering powers if she accepted and obstructing relief if she did not. She wanted the Temple’s clerk to say what the Temple asked.

Runa went to the hall with every page marked.

Her mother was there. So were Torve, Oddrun, the woman from the outer house and several people who had not attended the boatwright’s hearing. The offer concerned them in a way that another household’s title had not. Gerd stood at the back with two labourers. Petros sat near the door, making room for late arrivals until he had almost none himself.

Runa began with the works: drainage, raised stretches, the crossing, maintenance. This part went well. People asked practical questions. She could answer some and refer others to Petros, who stood when called and gave figures plainly.

Then she came to payment.

A man asked whether the levy replaced his present cart duty. She explained where duties would be credited and where they would remain. A woman asked whether an absent husband would still make his household liable. The answer depended on how his household was recorded. Runa could feel Ivara listening somewhere beyond the lamplight.

“What if the crop fails?” someone asked.

“There are provisions for assessed hardship.”

“Assessed by whom?”

She answered.

“How long?”

She answered again, less confidently, because the schedule specified the authority more clearly than the time it would take to exercise it.

The outer-house woman stood before Runa finished the next section.

“When does the road start?”

“Limited work has started under the first allowance.”

“The part I can take a cart over.”

“After the agreement and the necessary materials—”

“So if we argue until spring, we carry until spring.”

There were murmurs of agreement. A better-dressed man near the brazier replied that being able to carry a sack was little comfort if one’s grandchildren were still paying for the privilege. The woman said she would like her children to reach the age at which they could complain about their own taxes. Runa waited through the exchange, refusing to make either answer stand for everyone on that side of the room.

When she reached jurisdiction, the hall became quieter.

Dagrin asked her to read the relevant sentence again. She did. Ingrith asked whether disputes over levy seizure would return to the Thing. Runa explained the court of appeal. The answer travelled away from Varnvik with each stage.

Svanhild folded her hands in her lap.

Runa hated that small movement. It looked like the careful suppression of satisfaction. Her mother had warned her about the Temple’s reach without understanding these documents, and now would feel entitled to every warning she had given. Runa knew the thought was mean. It remained with her while she finished.

Petros spoke at the end. He said the offer would make possible work they could not fund from the first allowance. He also said the first allowance did not require the district to accept everything proposed. He would continue the work already authorized while the terms were considered.

That helped. It did not make the room easy.

Afterward people came to Runa with individual questions. Some wanted to know whether a particular debt would be found. Some wanted a court that could find it. A widow asked whether she would have to name the man who supplied her household, because he was married elsewhere. Runa said they should discuss her case privately and felt the inadequacy of that promise before she finished it.

By the time the hall emptied, her head hurt. Svanhild approached while she was stacking the papers.

“You explained it well.”

“Did I?”

“Yes.”

“Then that is something.”

Her mother drew back slightly. “You needn’t speak to me as if I wrote it.”

“No. Only as if you are pleased by it.”

Svanhild’s face hardened.

“There are people who have asked these questions without being given a southern coat.”

Runa gathered the papers too quickly. One slipped to the floor. She bent, picked it up, and could not bear the prospect of going home beside her mother with this conversation still alive between them.

“I’ll eat at the chapel.”

“Eydis is waiting.”

“Tell her where I am.”

She regretted that at once. It made her daughter another message someone else had to carry. But she went to the chapel anyway.

Astri found her sitting at the table long after the lamp should have been lit. She brought a bowl of beans and a piece of bread. Runa ate without much taste.

“Bad meeting?” Astri asked.

“Useful.”

“That bad.”

Runa laughed once, despite herself.

Astri sat on the other stool. She did not ask for the whole account. They listened to the messenger snoring in the next room until the sound changed so absurdly that both of them smiled.

At home, much later, Runa found a piece of fish under a plate and Eydis’s handwriting on a scrap beside it: Grandmother says it is yours even if you are not hungry.

She ate it cold before going upstairs.

Chapter 15 A feast in the dark months

The first argument concerned where to put the musicians. The second concerned whether the men in question deserved the name.

Svanhild had been asked to lead the winter gathering at a household above the landing. By the time Runa arrived with a pot to return, her mother had moved two benches, offended a man with a stringed instrument, and persuaded him that the insult came from the room’s poor arrangement rather than her opinion of his playing.

“Sit there,” she told him. “Then the sound comes along the wall.”

“So everyone can hear my mistakes?”

“So I can hear the tune.”

He tried a phrase. It did sound better, though Runa could not tell whether he had changed how he played. Svanhild nodded approval and immediately asked him to stop while someone carried hot food past.

The room was low and full of steam. People had brought what they could spare, which produced an abundance unlike the hall’s orderly provision: two pots of the same stew, too much pickled cabbage, a small dish of smoked meat everyone pretended not to notice too soon, and a cake sweetened with something the host would not name because she had been saving it since summer. Someone had hidden a second cake for later. Several people knew where.

Eydis went straight to Vigdis. Runa watched them negotiate a place on the bench between older girls, then lost sight of her daughter behind a woman’s shoulder. She resisted the urge to call her back for an instruction she did not need.

Ragnel had come in ordinary clothes beneath her travelling cloak. Without the hearing table between them, she seemed shorter. A man who had argued with her clerk at the court greeted her warmly and asked whether she played at bones. She said badly. He said that was the correct answer. They sat down together.

Petros arrived carrying a small jar of olives from the south. People examined them with suspicion. Svanhild tasted one and said it had the courage of a much larger fish. The engineer laughed, explained that they were fruit, and watched three people reconsider whether they had eaten one.

Runa liked him for bringing something that might be disliked. She had brought a safe contribution and placed it among the others without drawing attention.

The meal took a long time. There were not enough spoons at first, then too many because three people had fetched more at once. Solli ate on the floor until Oddrun told him he was too old to be comfortable there, at which he stood and discovered that she had been right. The three-legged dog was excluded, admitted by another door, excluded again and finally allowed to remain beneath a bench on condition that nobody fed it. This condition failed almost immediately.

Runa sat between Astri and a man who knew her only as Svanhild’s daughter. He asked what she did in the south. She told him. He listened, then asked whether she had children, although Eydis had just called her Mother across the room.

“One.”

“Boy or girl?”

“Girl.”

“That’s well. Easier.”

Astri bent over her bowl. Runa could see her shoulders moving.

The man turned towards the music before Runa could answer, sparing them both. Astri whispered that she would tell the boys they were more difficult next morning and see whether the knowledge improved them.

After the bowls were cleared, a young sailor stood to give an account of a southern crossing. He was not the man who had called the horn at the last departure; that crew had sent word of reaching its first port and remained away. This sailor had returned with an earlier boat and was enjoying the length of time during which nobody could check his southern adventures.

He began well. He could imitate a harbour officer and knew when to stop doing it. People laughed. He described a night squall, a line lost overboard and his own journey along a spar to recover it. At this point a man near the hearth put down his cup.

“You were on the other boat.”

The sailor paused.

“For the outward leg.”

“And the inward.”

“I changed at the middle harbour.”

“There wasn’t a middle harbour. We anchored off the point.”

The room grew attentive in a new way. Runa felt sympathy for the young man before deciding whether he deserved it. He had been doing well, and now could not get back to the place where he had stood a moment earlier.

Svanhild leaned forward.

“Which line did you recover?”

“The forward one.”

“Then begin there. We have heard enough harbours to berth a fleet.”

The young man took the opening. He described the line. The older sailor interrupted again, but this time to correct the knot. Svanhild asked him to demonstrate it. Someone passed a cord. Within a minute the two men were arguing over the work rather than the young man’s courage.

Eydis leaned forward to see the knot. Svanhild shifted her cup out of the girl’s way.

The young man’s account ended smaller than it had begun. It still received applause. He sat red-faced beside a woman who gave him a drink and said something that made him laugh reluctantly. The older sailor was soon distracted by someone else’s mistake at bones.

Svanhild performed after another musician had played. She chose a comic account of a bridegroom whose gift animal ate the wedding ribbons. Runa knew it, but not the new verses. Her mother gave the animal the precise manner of a local official without naming him, and Dagrin, who had arrived late, laughed loudest.

For a while Runa forgot the proposal. She watched her mother’s face change as each voice entered it. Svanhild was vain; she enjoyed knowing she had them. She also paid attention with an intensity that Runa rarely saw directed at any single person. A cough near the door, a child growing restless, a listener missing a word: she adjusted without appearing to. When the child began to wriggle, she brought the animal closer. When the old man near the window cupped his ear, she turned so he caught the next line.

The bridegroom had bought the goat on the advice of a man who was selling it.

Svanhild allowed the listeners a moment with that information. Then she gave them the groom, arriving at the bride’s house with a hand on the goat’s neck and a speech about milk, increase and the prosperous household they would establish together.

“Your household has begun on my washing,” the bride’s mother said.

The goat was eating a sleeve.

Someone near the hearth supplied an insult before Svanhild could. She nodded to him, accepting the contribution, and made the groom pull the cloth from the animal’s mouth with the anxious care of a man who had praised its manners on the walk up.

“It knows good work,” he said.

“It will know the back of my hand.”

The mother’s voice was low and practical. The bride’s was lighter, more interested in seeing how far the groom would follow his own assurances. She asked whether the goat came from a good household. He said an excellent one. She asked why they had parted with it.

“To benefit ours.”

Runa laughed before the next line because she knew the pause. Her mother glanced at her and made the pause a little longer.

The bride gave the goat a ribbon. The groom objected that it ought to be tied, not fed. She said a gift should be made in the manner the recipient preferred. Behind the bride, her mother removed the rest of the washing from reach.

At the table Dagrin leaned forward with his cup held between both hands. He had forgotten to maintain the useful reserve he brought to meetings. Petros watched Svanhild’s hands. Some words escaped him, but the goat’s consideration of a second sleeve required no translation.

The groom declared that he could manage the animal. He had bought it, walked it over the ridge and brought it safely to the house. The goat at that moment put its forefeet on the bench and began examining the wedding meal.

Svanhild rose half out of her chair, chin lifted, eyes moving along an imaginary table. It was the same solemn inspection Dagrin had given the grain figures. The steward recognized enough to laugh, but perhaps not everything. Runa looked down into her cup rather than tell him.

The bride’s youngest brother offered help. He was given the rope. He immediately demanded payment for holding it, having observed how profitable services became when everyone was dressed too well to perform them. The groom promised him a share of the feast. The boy said he was already invited.

“What did you give him?” the bride asked.

“My word.”

“He has heard you use it on the goat.”

By then the child near the door had stopped wriggling. He was waiting to see where the goat would go next. Svanhild let it stand still. That made the boy look from the animal’s imagined place to her face, suspecting another movement she had not yet granted him.

The bride’s mother found a sound post. The groom tied the rope to it. He stepped back, pleased with a workmanlike answer to the whole difficulty, and brushed his sleeves. The bride examined the knot.

“Will that hold?”

“Until I loose it.”

“How fortunate that you are to remain with us.”

The goat ate the end of the rope.

It did not chew through the whole thing at once. Svanhild made the meal proceed around the slow destruction. A blessing. A cup passed. An uncle speaking too long. The groom sitting taller whenever somebody mentioned good husbandry. Between each, the soft movement of the animal’s jaw, performed with such innocent deliberation that the room began laughing before anything had gone wrong.

When the goat finally walked away, the groom was making a promise to the bride’s father. He broke off halfway through it. The father waited. The bride waited. The goat went towards the ribbons laid ready for the ceremony.

“Finish,” the bride said.

He tried. The ribbons vanished by degrees behind Svanhild’s pursed lips.

Runa had heard the account as a girl and remembered wanting the groom to catch the goat before he was shamed any further. Tonight she was enjoying the bride, who knew exactly how long he could endure remaining seated. She also remembered her mother telling it to a smaller room, making the animal eat an old cloth because there had been no wedding ribbons handy for the demonstration. She could smell that room for a moment, warm bread and the oil of the same lamp now upstairs in its box.

The groom stood. The bride’s father informed him that he had not yet completed the promise. He sat again. The little boy by the door shouted that the ribbons were going, and the adults’ laughter swallowed his warning.

Svanhild let the groom escape at last. He pursued the animal through three places, in each acquiring more advice and less ribbon. She used no great speed in the telling. She allowed the listeners to see him choose a course, commit to it and discover the goat had already changed its mind.

The bridegroom finally gave up chasing the animal and married wearing half a ribbon. The bride took the other half and used it for a purpose that made several adults laugh before the younger listeners understood. Eydis glanced at Vigdis, who whispered an explanation. Both girls dissolved into helpless laughter.

Runa looked away before her daughter could think she had disapproved.

Later there was dancing, though the room had never been large enough for the steps people insisted on taking. Runa was persuaded up by Astri, then abandoned to a partner who moved as if every turn were a small emergency. He apologized each time he trod on her. By the third apology she was laughing too hard to be graceful herself.

Astri found a better partner for her next time, a woman who knew the steps and had no patience with anybody who counted aloud. Runa had been counting under her breath. The woman touched two fingers to her own lips, grinning, and pulled her into the turn before she could apologize.

It helped. For a few measures she stopped considering where to put her feet. The floor seemed broader when she trusted that the person coming towards her would turn at the right moment. Then a man at the end forgot which way to go, three people collided, and everyone had to begin again.

The musician refused to restart the tune.

“Find me where I am,” he said.

They tried. Svanhild called the turn from the wall. Runa entered a beat late and recovered by taking two quick steps which her partner immediately copied, making the mistake appear intentional. By the end she was warm enough to loosen the fastening at her throat.

The woman offered her a drink. Runa accepted before realizing that the cup was the woman’s own. They stood near the cooler doorway, passing it between them, while the next set assembled.

“You dance better when you stop listening to your feet.”

“My feet disagree.”

“They’ll forgive you tomorrow.”

The woman had a small scar through one eyebrow and a way of looking directly at Runa which made her briefly forget the room behind them. She asked how long Runa would remain in Varnvik. Runa gave the answer she had given officials: through the present works, subject to their progress. The woman raised the scarred eyebrow.

“That sounds a cold bed.”

Runa laughed, startled enough to swallow wrongly. The woman waited while she coughed, then took back the cup with great dignity.

“I’ll ask your feet next time.”

She went to join the dance. Runa remained by the door, smiling in a way she hoped nobody was watching. She had not been propositioned, perhaps. She could still accept another dance. For several pleasant minutes the decision occupied her more completely than the district proposal had all day.

Astri came to retrieve her for a game at the other table. Ragnel was losing with the fierce concentration of a person who had expected to lose less. The man who had invited her claimed she owed him a forfeit. She asked him to repeat the rule. He did, and another player immediately pointed out that he had altered it.

“Local practice,” he said.

“Local invention,” Ragnel answered.

He offered her the cup instead. She drank and rolled again.

Runa sat on the end of the bench. Within three turns she had lost a small sweet cake which she had not yet received, been accused of hiding a winning throw with her sleeve, and discovered that the man beside her kept up a muttered correspondence with the bones as though they were disobedient sons. She began addressing hers just as sternly. Ragnel told them both that intimidation did not work.

“It works on witnesses,” the man said.

“Only bad ones.”

“Then these ought to be excellent.”

The table laughed. Ragnel laughed with them, throwing the bones while he was still talking. Her throw won. She took his cake first.

Across the room Eydis had found the three-legged dog. The animal’s head rested on her shoe, and she was stroking it with exaggerated innocence while a piece of bread disappeared under the bench. Runa caught her eye. The girl froze. Runa looked away and reached for the bones.

Her mother watched from the wall. Runa caught her smiling and did not look away.

The gathering thinned slowly. Children went home asleep on shoulders. Adults who claimed to be leaving found another subject at the door. Petros discovered someone interested in his olives and spent ten minutes describing trees. Torve and Vigdis carried benches back to their ordinary positions, arguing over which end to lift first.

Runa helped clear the dishes. Ragnel came beside her with a stack of bowls.

“Where do these go?”

“Through there. Unless they’re borrowed, in which case someone will tell us after we’ve put them down.”

They were told. They carried the bowls back and sorted them by the host’s directions. Once the water had been heated, they washed at a low table near the back door, where the steam could escape.

“You spoke well about the proposal,” Ragnel said.

“I spoke accurately.”

“That helped.”

Runa rubbed a dried piece from a bowl. “Some of it sounded worse aloud.”

“Some agreements do.”

“Do you think they should accept?”

Ragnel put a bowl on the drying cloth and took another.

“I think a woman ought to know which court will hear her before she is injured.”

“That isn’t the same question.”

“It is part of my answer.”

Runa waited. Through the doorway she could hear Svanhild trying to persuade the host not to save a particular portion of food until it spoiled. Ragnel turned a cup in her hands.

“When my younger brother died, I could not go to his burial by the direct road. It passed a house whose men said my presence would renew a demand. My elder brother had already been killed. I was not making a demand. I was carrying a shirt.”

Runa stopped rubbing.

“I went another way,” Ragnel said. “By the time I arrived, they had wrapped him.”

She examined the cup’s rim and set it aside.

“Were they made to answer?”

“Some were. By different men at different times. That did not make the road safe.”

“And the Temple did?”

“It gave me somewhere I could leave without asking whose cousin would meet me.”

Runa looked down into the cooling water. She had been preparing an objection about distant authority. The objection remained valid. It also seemed a poor thing to place beside the shirt.

“I can understand why you want one court,” she said.

“I want a court that cannot become nobody’s responsibility when the wrong household objects.”

A tired boy appeared in the doorway holding a blanket. He had been told to sleep in the back room and could not find his brother. Ragnel dried her hands, went to help him, and returned several minutes later with the blanket folded differently.

“Found him?” Runa asked.

“Asleep under the coats.”

They resumed washing. The conversation did not return to the road.

Svanhild took Eydis home first. Runa stayed to finish the bowls, then helped the host divide the leftovers among people who had lent pots. She carried a small portion for their breakfast. Outside, snow reflected enough light to show the track without a lantern.

Her feet hurt. She had been stepped on more often than she realized. Halfway home she stopped to adjust the lace of one boot and began laughing again at the remembered expression of her partner, who had treated every collision as a personal moral failure.

When she reached the house, Svanhild was still awake.

“You enjoyed yourself,” her mother said.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

Runa put the food on the shelf.

Chapter 16 The page withheld

Maret’s bowl was blue on the outside and plain within. Eydis carried it in both hands, although it had survived many journeys in a basket and did not require such care.

Runa had offered to go with her. Eydis refused. Svanhild had said Maret would not eat her, which made the visit sound easier and harder at once. Eydis had waited two days longer than she meant to, then taken the bowl before either woman could ask again.

Maret opened the door with a piece of cloth tucked beneath her chin. She removed it when she saw who stood outside.

“The bowl,” Eydis said.

“I see.”

“Thank you.”

“You didn’t borrow it.”

“No. But we had it.”

Maret took it and stepped back. Eydis did not know whether this was an invitation. She remained on the threshold until the woman said, “You’ll let the heat out.”

Inside, cloth lay across a table with a row of small weights along one edge. Maret was cutting pieces from it. Eydis could see chalk marks and the shape of a sleeve. A little girl sat on the hearthstone, brushing the hair of a doll made from scraps.

“Sit there,” Maret said, pointing to a stool well away from the cloth.

Eydis sat. She had rehearsed the apology in bed. It began with the fact that she had not known who Maret was, then explained that she had been trying to show she remembered what she had been taught. Now that she could see the woman reaching for her scissors, the explanation sounded too much like asking to be excused.

“I’m sorry I read it again.”

Maret kept cutting.

“I didn’t know he was your grandfather.”

“No.”

“I wouldn’t have if I’d known.”

“Would you have read it somewhere else?”

Eydis looked at the little girl, who was concentrating on the doll.

“I don’t know.”

Maret nodded as if that answer were useful. She finished the cut, set the scissors down and shook the scrap free.

“Your grandmother has told it differently in my hearing for years.”

“Was he brave?” Eydis asked.

Maret gave her a tired look. Eydis flushed.

“I mean, did he really leave the cargo?”

“I wasn’t there.”

“But people told you.”

“People told me many things.”

The woman folded the cut pieces. Her hands were quick, pressing the edges without needing to look directly at them. Eydis wanted her to choose one account and say it was true. She could feel that wanting becoming visible and tried to hide it.

Maret sat down at last.

“He was dead before I was born,” she said. “My mother remembered him. When she was little, he could not bear to hear her cry at night. He would get up and carry her round the room. My grandmother said he made it worse because the moment he put her down she began again.”

Eydis listened.

“He wore a path in the rushes between the bed and the door. My mother said she remembered the buckle on his belt against her foot. That is probably a memory she acquired from hearing it told. She was very young.”

“Still,” Eydis said.

“Yes. Still.”

The little girl brought her doll to Maret, who untangled the yarn hair without interrupting herself.

“My mother told that when people spoke as if he had spent his whole life deciding whether to tie one cargo properly. I don’t know which line your grandmother first learned. I know what it is like to sit in a room while people enjoy a man being smaller than everyone listening.”

Eydis stared at her knees.

“I didn’t enjoy that.”

“What did you enjoy?”

The answer was too embarrassing. She tried to find another one and failed.

“They were listening.”

Maret looked at her for a while, then returned the doll to the child.

“Yes,” she said. “They were.”

There was no rebuke after it. Eydis found the absence harder to manage than one would have been. She asked whether she could help gather the scraps.

Maret let her. They sorted pieces large enough for patches from strips that could be used for ties. The little girl claimed a blue triangle for the doll. Maret allowed it, then had to defend every other blue piece from the same claim.

Eydis laughed. The woman smiled without turning it into forgiveness.

The little girl wanted Eydis to make the doll a sleeve. Maret said there was no cloth to waste. Eydis held up one of the scraps already assigned to ties and asked whether a very small sleeve would count as waste.

“It depends how badly you make it.”

The child took this for permission and fetched a needle case. Maret took it from her before she could open it. She selected a needle, threaded it, and gave it to Eydis with a piece of cloth so small that the task became more difficult rather than less.

Eydis folded it as she had seen the woman fold the dress pieces. The doll’s arm was only a twist of stuffed linen. She tried fitting the sleeve without turning it, pricked herself and put her finger in her mouth. The child watched with a disapproval which seemed much too old for her.

“Your grandmother can sew.”

“So can I.”

“Not this.”

Maret told the child to hold the doll still if she wanted the sleeve finished. Eydis began again. The little arm kept bending at the wrong place. At last she laid the doll on the table and discovered she could work with both hands if she did not insist on holding it upright for an audience.

Maret passed her a short piece of thread. Their fingers met over the scraps. It was an ordinary kindness, offered while the woman was thinking about her own work, and Eydis felt suddenly less trapped by having come to apologize.

The sleeve was crooked. The child liked it. She asked for another. Eydis looked at the remaining cloth and said the doll could have one good arm for now.

“Which is the good one?”

“The one I haven’t sewn.”

Maret made a small sound which might have been laughter. The girl considered the doll and announced that it had been wounded at sea. She carried it to the hearth and began explaining the accident to somebody Eydis could not see.

Eydis gathered the last scraps. She had been inside long enough for the light to move across the table. The house smelled of warm cloth where Maret had pressed the seam near the hearth, and of something cooking slowly in a covered pot. She wished she could stay until the apology ceased to be the reason she had entered.

Maret asked her to put the weights back in their bowl.

One was a little smooth stone with a hole through it. Eydis held it against the light. The hole was crooked; looking through it made the window seem very far away.

“That came from the shore,” Maret said.

“Did you find it?”

“My husband did.”

Eydis set it down carefully. She knew nothing about the husband and could not tell from the woman’s voice whether asking would make her welcome or impossible. She put the other weights beside it.

Maret lifted the bowl to its shelf. The little girl returned with the doll and wanted Eydis to see that the wounded sailor could still stand. It could not. They propped it against a cup, and she accepted that.

At the door Eydis thanked her for the account of her grandfather.

“Don’t put that one in your pages,” Maret said.

Eydis’s hand tightened on the latch.

“Why?”

“Because I am asking you.”

“It would make him—”

“I know what it would make him.”

Eydis stopped. She had already imagined the buckle, the path worn in the rushes, the room becoming quiet as she told it. She wanted the detail more because it did what the sailing account had not.

“All right,” she said.

Maret held the door while she stepped out.

On the walk home Eydis repeated the detail to herself so she would not forget it. Then she became frightened that remembering was already a kind of theft, which was absurd because Maret had told her. She could not ask Runa without repeating it. She walked more slowly, leaving a clear print beside each older footprint.

Svanhild was alone when she returned. Her grandmother looked up but did not immediately ask how the visit had gone. Eydis appreciated this so much that she nearly told her everything.

“She took the bowl.”

“Good.”

“She is making a dress.”

“She makes fine seams.”

Eydis hung her cloak. The copied pages remained under the mattress upstairs. She had not looked at them since the gathering.

“Can we do the turn again?” she asked.

Svanhild put down her needle.

“Which turn?”

“The boat. Before the mast.”

Her grandmother moved a chair closer to the hearth. Eydis sat, and for a little while the lesson was easy. They worked on breath and the place where the words crowded. Svanhild made her say one line while walking from the door to the table, so the movement would stop her rushing. Eydis took exaggeratedly long strides and made her laugh.

“No. You are not crossing the entire southern sea. Again.”

Eydis tried again. The rhythm settled. She could feel it returning as a pleasure in her mouth, and relief made her careless.

“Did Maret’s mother ever hear your old teacher tell it?”

Svanhild reached for the stocking.

“I expect so.”

“Would she have asked him to change it?”

“We’re working on the mast.”

“I know, but—”

“The mast, Eydis.”

The girl’s face grew hot. Her grandmother looked tired rather than angry, which did not make the refusal easier. Eydis tried the line. She got it wrong. Svanhild corrected the word but not the flatness that had entered her voice.

They stopped soon afterward.

Upstairs, Eydis took out the pages. She read the disputed line without saying it aloud. Below it there was room enough to add the little domestic memory, and she imagined the handwriting there so clearly that she reached for the pen before remembering she had promised.

She put the pen down.

Instead she wrote the date at the top of a clean sheet and copied the part about the pole man. She made the turn mark where her grandmother had told her to put it. After two lines she stopped and went downstairs to ask whether the man braced against the side or against the cargo.

Svanhild answered at once, standing to show her. Eydis held an imaginary pole while her grandmother shifted her shoulders into position. They nearly knocked over the stool. Runa came in during the demonstration and had to wait in the doorway until the boat was clear of the rock.

Part Three The account that failed

Chapter 17 The old singer’s chest

The water had entered behind the chimney and found the one box nobody thought could be reached by rain.

Astri came for Runa because there were papers in it. By the time they reached the store, two men had moved the chest beneath the dry part of the roof and were arguing about whether to empty it before carrying it downstairs. Runa settled that by trying to lift one end. It did not move.

They opened it.

The old singer had been dead for three winters. His niece kept the room and had intended to sort his effects before each of them. There were winter cloaks belonging to people nobody could now identify, a broken instrument wrapped in cloth, cups awarded at gatherings, and packets of paper tied with cord that disintegrated when touched. The niece stood in the doorway looking ashamed.

“He said they were arranged.”

“They may have been,” Runa said.

“Then I moved things.”

“We all do.”

They carried the dry material first. Runa separated pages that could be unfolded from those which needed to dry undisturbed. Astri brought boards from the school. The men made trips downstairs with bundles of cloth, complaining less once they could see progress. By midday the contents had spread through two rooms.

The papers did not form a book. Some were accounts of payments. Others contained fragments of performed verse, words set down with spaces where a tune might have carried more than the writer needed to remember. There were names without explanations and explanations without names. Runa recognized three stories because her mother had told them. The written versions were too bare to be read aloud to anyone expecting pleasure.

Astri found a complaint about a borrowed horse and laughed.

“He kept that?”

“Apparently.”

“He kept the horse too, according to my father.”

Runa laid another page on a board. It concerned repairs after a storm, with a list of boats on one side and a fragment of a grant account on the other. Torve’s grandfather was named. She read the name twice, checking the shape of a letter that had spread where the paper became wet.

The words following it did not match Svanhild’s account.

She did not say so. She asked Astri to look at the damaged phrase. The teacher held it near the window and supplied a possible reading. It was the same one Runa had found: the landing to the boatwright and those of his house who came after him, while the hall stood above the shore.

“That could be a flourish,” Astri said.

“It could.”

“Do you need a copy?”

“Yes.”

Runa copied the whole page, including the list of boats. She marked the doubtful word rather than silently choosing it. The reverse contained a reckoning of timber from a year she could place by an old steward’s death. The account did not prove itself. It did give her a question she could no longer avoid asking.

The niece said her uncle had learned the grant from an older singer and performed it when Torve’s father repaired the winter hall’s boat. She had heard it as a child. She remembered the hall roof leaking during the gathering because someone had caught the drips in a cup and drunk them to make people laugh.

“Was my mother there?” Runa asked.

“She was young then. She used to come to him for the older lines.”

“Do you remember her learning this one?”

The niece shook her head. “I remember her laughing at the cup.”

Runa put the copy in her folder and returned to drying the papers. It was tempting to leave at once, but the work remained. Astri passed her another bundle. This one contained nothing except household purchases and a letter from a woman who had been angry enough to use the back of an expensive sheet.

She went back the following morning to finish the wet bundles before following the grant. The niece met her at the door with an expression so anxious that Runa thought more water had entered.

“I sold one of the cups,” the woman said.

“All right.”

“The old silver one. Last winter.”

“It was yours to sell.”

“He said they ought to stay together.”

Runa took off her cloak. The room smelled of paper slowly drying and the stale damp of heavy cloth. She could see the clean place where the cup had stood, surrounded by objects that no longer looked distinguished out of their wrapping.

“Did you need the money?”

“Yes.”

“Then he had an expensive wish.”

The niece laughed in surprise and looked towards the chest as if someone might disapprove. Runa did not ask what she had needed the money for. They worked together sorting the remaining papers. A fragment which looked significant turned out to be part of a list of debts the singer had owed rather than payments he had received. The niece became more cheerful as they read it.

“He used to say he could remember every promise made in this valley.”

“Perhaps he remembered these.”

“He remembered them badly when people came for them.”

Runa copied names where the ink was fading, leaving the amounts as they were rather than trying to resolve them. It was not her task to collect a dead man’s old debts from his niece. She asked whether the woman wanted the list separate from the performance material. The answer was immediate.

“Yes. Keep it where I can find it.”

Near midday Astri arrived with a pupil who could read the older hand better than either of them. The girl was seventeen and ordinarily reluctant to speak in a room of adults. Over the letters she became decisive. She showed them how the singer joined two forms that looked like one word, then demonstrated on a slate until Runa could distinguish them herself.

“You ought to be at the court table,” Runa said.

The girl flushed. Astri waited until she had gone to fetch water before speaking.

“She is good at this. She is also tired of being told where she ought to go whenever she does something well.”

“I meant it as praise.”

“She will know. It can still get tiring.”

Runa nodded. When the girl returned, she asked whether she could pay her for a morning checking the damaged letters. The girl asked what the rate would be, an answer which pleased Runa more than gratitude. They agreed the work and left her future out of it.

By evening the papers could be stored without ruining one another. They carried the heaviest chest downstairs empty and refilled it there. The niece wanted the private letters kept beneath the accounts. Runa put them where asked, including the angry woman’s letter she had enjoyed reading. She did not copy it.

Runa spent the next two days asking questions outside the hall’s usual circle. She began with an older woman whose husband had worked beside Torve’s grandfather, then visited a man on the outer shore who remembered the same gathering and insisted the cup had been a wooden bowl. They disagreed about who drank from it. Both remembered the grant as extending beyond a single man’s service.

The woman had a phrase Runa had not seen on the damaged paper. The man supplied half of it before she prompted him. That made her sit forward. She wrote their accounts separately and read each back.

On the third day she returned to Torve’s old correspondence. He let her examine it without asking what she expected to find. He was busy shaping a timber and seemed reluctant to look away from a piece of work which would do what he asked if he asked it properly.

One letter addressed his father concerning repairs to your inherited landing. It was not a formal grant. Another referred to the estate’s boats as guests of the yard while timber was being delivered. A third assumed service continuing through the household without describing the holding as revocable. Individually they could be explained. Together with the older account, they made the narrower version harder to sustain.

“Who told you to look?” Torve asked at last.

“No one.”

He put down the tool.

Runa told him what she had found. She did not promise a title. She did not tell him what her mother must have known.

Torve listened without satisfaction. When she finished, he asked whether the old singer had written anything about the years his father spent repairing the slips after the estate moved timber onto them.

“I haven’t seen it.”

“Look for that as well.”

She said she would.

On the second visit to the outer shore, the old woman made Runa wait while she finished a repair to a leather strap. Her grandson was leaving with it, and every question Runa asked required the needle to stop.

“Tell me when you have finished,” Runa said at last.

“Thank you.”

It was said without emphasis. Runa sat back and looked round the room. A string of dried herbs hung above the window. The old woman had placed a blue bowl on the shelf with its cracked side turned towards the wall. Two small children played a game with pebbles at the far end of the table, altering the rules whenever the younger one seemed likely to win.

The grandson tried the strap. His grandmother made him pull harder. The stitching held, and she sent him away with a warning not to use it to lift anything heavier than the thing it belonged to.

“What was it you wanted?” she asked Runa.

“The grant. The words you heard.”

“I’ve told you.”

“You said the household after him. I want to know whether those were the singer’s words or how you understood them.”

The woman examined her. “You think I might have made the better words later.”

“I think any of us might.”

“Did your mother?”

Runa kept her eyes on the page. “That is what I’m trying to establish.”

The woman took the paper and held it at a distance to read. Her eyes watered in the window light. Runa offered to read it aloud, and she waved the offer away until she reached the damaged place.

“Here.”

Runa supplied the words. The woman repeated them with a rhythm Runa had not used. One phrase shortened; another opened into a stress that made the succession unmistakable.

“That is how he said it,” she told her. “I remember because my husband laughed. He said the old jarl would promise a man’s grandchildren the moon if the man had his best boat off a rock.”

“Did he believe the promise?”

“He believed he’d heard it.”

“Would he have said the grant was permanent?”

“He’s dead. You have to ask me.”

Runa put down the pen. The old woman set the paper between them.

“I won’t give you more certainty by making him speak for me. I heard the words. I understood them to mean the boatwright’s house would keep the place. I didn’t go round asking which steward could empty it later.”

Runa read back the statement she would record. The woman corrected one word. Then she asked whether this would take the landing from somebody else.

“It may limit what the estate can claim.”

“And the people who dry nets?”

“That is separate.”

“Keep it separate, then.”

On the way home Runa stopped at the lower path to watch the tide. She had acquired a statement clearer than the one she brought, and less convenient. It required her to preserve uncertainty as carefully as the phrase she believed her mother had suppressed.

She copied the woman’s correction that evening before doing anything else.

The evidence that Svanhild had known came from a smaller paper in the second bundle. It was in her mother’s young handwriting, more rounded than the writing Runa knew from letters. She had copied part of the grant for the old singer and noted a disputed word in the margin. Below it he had supplied a correction. The disputed word concerned the children of the house.

Runa recognized her mother’s habit of pressing too hard at the beginning of a line. She had inherited it.

Astri was putting the last dry pages in a new wrapper. She saw Runa staring.

“What is it?”

Runa passed the sheet across. Astri read it, looked at the handwriting and said nothing.

That evening Runa arrived home carrying more paper than she had taken out. Svanhild asked whether the chest had been saved.

“Most of it.”

“He never threw anything away.”

“No.”

Her mother held out a plate. Runa took it. Eydis was describing something from school, and Svanhild was listening with half her attention while watching the pot. Runa ate what was put before her and waited for a time when the girl would not be in the room.

It did not come that evening.

Chapter 18 The wind behind the road

The water stood where yesterday there had been a dry wheel mark.

Gerd met them on the path and pointed without explanation. Petros walked ahead, boots sinking into the soft edge. The first raised stretch lay beside the reeds, built from cleared material and edged with timber where the ground required it. They had not finished surfacing it. They had finished enough to keep water on the wrong side.

Runa could see men waiting with shovels. No one was working. The wind pressed their clothes flat against them and pushed the reed heads inland.

Petros went to the far end, then to the culvert opening they had cleared. Water moved through it, but not fast enough. He took out the level. Gerd held the staff. Runa opened her folder and found the earlier measurements with fingers that had become clumsy in the cold.

The numbers had not changed. The water had.

Oddrun arrived carrying a basket she intended to take to the outer houses. She stopped at the edge of the pool.

“You’ve shut its back way.”

Petros looked up. Runa translated before remembering he understood enough Linn to catch it.

“Which way?” he asked.

Oddrun pointed through the reeds towards a shallow hollow beyond the line of the raised track.

“In this wind it comes round there. Not from the channel first. Round behind.”

Hervi, called from the landing, stood beside her and frowned.

“It doesn’t affect the boats.”

“It affects my nets.”

Petros asked them both to show him. They walked the edge, sometimes together and sometimes separately, while Runa followed with the drawing. Oddrun had spent winters retrieving net weights from places the pilot never needed to stand. Hervi knew where boats could pass when the same wind raised the channel. Petros followed the netmender down to the hollow he had left off his drawing.

At the hollow Petros crouched and pushed a reed aside. Water moved through the old passage in a shallow sheet. The new raised material interrupted it. Farther inland the water was finding another course towards a low house.

“Open this section,” he told Gerd.

The labourer looked at the work they had just paid to make.

“How far?”

Petros measured with his stride and marked the ends. The men began removing timber. Runa recorded the hours because they would have to be paid for undoing it as well as building it.

One man asked whether the office would accuse them of doing the first work badly.

“No,” Petros said.

“Put it down.”

Runa looked at him. He met her gaze without apology. She wrote that the alteration followed the engineer’s revised instruction after a wind test, then read it aloud. The man nodded and returned to his shovel.

Before the first opening was complete, the woman from the low house came out carrying a rolled sleeping mat. She was not shouting. She wanted to know whether to move the bed frame as well.

Petros looked at the level of water against her threshold. He asked Gerd how soon the opening would run. Gerd answered in work still to be done, not minutes: the second timber free, the packed material loosened, the channel behind it cleared.

“Move what you cannot dry easily,” Petros told her through Runa. “We may get it down. Don’t wait for us to be certain.”

The woman handed Runa the mat.

Inside, a man too old to carry much was trying to lift a chest by one handle. Runa set down the mat and took the other end. Together they brought it to a bench above the wettest part of the floor. The man wanted it carried outside. The woman said the yard was no drier, and for a moment their old disagreement about where to keep the chest filled the room while new water crept beneath the door.

Runa asked where they would like the bedding. The woman pointed to a loft opening. A boy went up first and pulled while Runa passed bundles. Dust fell into her face. She coughed, blinked and continued. There were toys among the things on the floor, a little stool with one leg bound, a pot cooling with its lid off. None of it looked like the property at risk in her report.

Petros came in to see the seepage at the far wall. He knelt with a narrow rod, probed the soft earth beside it and asked whether it always dampened in this wind. The woman said yes, but not across the room. He did not ask her to distinguish the increase more exactly. He went back to the men.

They freed the second timber. Runa heard the calls through the open door. The water at the threshold did not immediately fall. The old man stood watching it, taking each little movement forward as evidence the workers had failed. Runa stayed beside him without saying what the engineer could not yet promise.

After a while the edge stopped advancing. A floating chip began to move towards the door instead of away from it. The woman saw and sat on the raised bed frame, holding a blanket to her chest.

“Leave it up there tonight,” she told the boy in the loft. “I don’t trust it yet.”

When Runa went outside her skirt was wet with more than rain. The men had mud to their knees. Gerd showed Petros where the release had begun scouring too quickly, and they put stones in to check it before the escaping water made a new problem lower down.

By midday the pool had begun to fall. Wet spoil lay along the path. Several good timbers had been saved; one split while being levered free. Petros swore in his own language and then told the man holding the lever that he had warned them not to hurry.

The man lowered the tool.

“You told us to get the water off the house.”

Petros opened his mouth, closed it and turned away. Runa followed him far enough to ask whether he wanted the men to stop.

“No.”

“Then tell them what to do next.”

He stood with rain gathering on his eyelashes. For a moment she thought he would be angry with her too. Then he went back.

The work took the rest of the day. They broke for food in a shed where the roof rattled and nobody had enough room to stretch their legs. Oddrun shared a piece of bread with Gerd. Hervi argued with one of the men about the wind turning before dark. Petros ate silently, looking at the revised sketch.

Later he spoke to the man with the lever. Runa heard only the beginning: “I was unfair.” The man shrugged, listened, and eventually offered him the tool so he could see where the timber had caught. They examined it together.

The foreman wanted the men’s pay settled before they left.

He had heard too many assurances that a failed job would be accounted for later. Runa brought the wage sheet into the shed and read each name. One man had left early to fetch timber; another had joined after midday. Their hours overlapped on the rough tally because the person recording the change had written the replacement beneath the man replaced.

“They both worked,” the foreman said.

“I know. I need the hours.”

“You have them.”

“I have the same hours twice.”

Orri, the man who had asked for the revised instruction to be recorded, sat forward. He had worked beside the first man and could say when he left. Gerd confirmed the timber journey. Runa altered the sheet and let them look at the change before signing it.

The foreman relaxed when he saw no hours had vanished. He took the coin packet, counted it and counted it again. Petros remained outside measuring a higher line. No one spoke appreciatively of his decision to pay for the work he had ordered. It was expected of him.

Orri lingered after the others left, trying to mend a split in his glove with a needle too blunt for the leather. Runa offered the smaller awl from her case. He used it, thanked her and held the glove up to the light.

“Another week,” he said.

“Will it last?”

“Until I pay for the next thing that won’t.”

She smiled because he smiled. He flexed his fingers inside the glove, testing the patch. His wife made better repairs, he told her, but had enough work without his arriving every night with another piece of himself falling out of his clothes. Runa offered him the remainder of her thread. He accepted only what he needed, winding it around the needle.

When Petros returned, Orri asked about the higher line. The engineer showed him with a finger on the wet sketch. Orri asked where the spoil would go. This time Petros did not answer immediately. He looked towards the reeds and said they would walk the runoff together in daylight.

Runa put the paid sheet in its wrapper. Her hands had warmed indoors. She felt the cold more sharply when they went out again.

Before leaving the works, she went back to the flooded house.

The water had gone from most of the floor. It had left a line of dark grit against the legs of the furniture and a smell which the woman was trying to drive out by opening both the door and the shutter. The room was colder than the path. The old man sat on the bed with a blanket over his knees, objecting each time the woman passed that she was letting the warmth escape.

“There isn’t any,” she answered.

The boy had come down from the loft and was scraping mud into a pan. He worked from the centre of the room towards the edges, leaving islands behind him. Runa took another flat piece of wood and started at the door. When they met, they could see where the remaining dirt lay.

The woman came to look. She was carrying a little sack that had been stored beneath the bench. Its bottom was dark.

“That was for next month.”

Runa asked what it held. Meal. She set down the scraper and touched the sack. The wet had reached farther than it showed from outside. They opened it over a bowl. A dry layer came first, then clumps which broke under the woman’s fingers.

“Can the dry be kept separate?” Runa asked.

“It was separate. In the sack.”

Runa nodded. She helped lift the remaining meal into another vessel. The wet portion made an unexpectedly small heap. She could have carried it in one hand, and it was still enough to make the woman stand motionless over it.

Runa named the relief store.

“I don’t want relief.”

“The work caused the water.”

“Then call it that.”

“I will.”

The woman fetched a measure. They agreed the amount in front of the old man, who interrupted to say the sack had contained more when he bought it. The woman told him they had eaten since then. He looked offended and pulled the blanket closer.

Runa could authorize replacement of the food against the works allowance. The bedding was harder. Some would dry; some might sour. The floor had been poor before the water crossed it. She could hear the questions the office would ask, and she could not bear to ask them in that room while the boy knelt in mud.
