She wrote down what she could see and asked to return after the house had dried. The woman read the list slowly, with a finger beneath the lines.

“You’ve put two mats. There are three.”

“Where is the other?”

“In the loft. He took it up first.”

Runa added it, then marked which had been wet. The woman made her remove the qualification until they had brought the third mat down and inspected it together. One corner was dark. Runa recorded the corner. It seemed absurd to spend so long naming a wet corner while the whole house stood open, but when she gave the woman the copy, the woman folded it carefully and placed it above the hearth.

The boy asked whether they could bring the bedding down that night. His mother tested the floor with the heel of her hand. She shook her head.

“You’ll sleep up there.”

“With him?” the old man asked.

“You’ll stay where you are. I’ll put dry boards down.”

He began another objection. She told him that if he could climb the ladder he was welcome to exchange places. The boy laughed, then stopped when his grandfather looked at him.

Runa helped move the bench against the bed. They laid boards across the gap to make a dry place for the old man’s feet. He tested it cautiously, then asked for his boots. They were wet. The woman gave him a different pair, too large, which he accepted with the air of a man conceding an important point.

When Runa had carried the dirt pan outside, she found the boy beside her. He wanted to know whether the new road would be taken away.

“Part of it is being moved.”

“All of it?”

“No.”

He looked down the path towards the workers. “I liked the high bit.”

“So did I.”

“You can see the boats from it.”

She had been thinking of wheels. She looked at the bank he meant and saw how little height a child needed to gain a different view. The removed timbers lay beside it with wet earth packed between them.

“Don’t climb there tonight.”

“I know.”

He went inside before she could supply another instruction. She emptied the pan and brought it back, then closed the shutter when the woman asked. The room remained cold, but the blanket stopped lifting over the old man’s knees.

The safer line rose inland. It also entered ground held by the shrine keepers, where grazing, a warning path and several marked stones occupied what the first drawing had shown as an unfilled space. Petros put the drawing before Runa that evening.

“More length,” he said. “More drainage. More money.”

“And permission.”

“Yes.”

She copied the changed quantities. The emergency allowance had looked sufficient a week earlier. Now it had a visible end.

“Could we have known?” she asked.

Petros rubbed mud from a brass fitting with a soft cloth.

“We could have asked differently.”

“I translated what you asked.”

“I know.”

She wished he had made the answer an accusation. She could have defended herself against one. Instead she remembered standing on that bank, satisfied that everyone had understood everyone else.

They left the boots to dry around the chapel stove. The measuring rods stood against the wall, cleaned and still useful. Petros went to fetch more fuel, and Runa stayed long enough to finish the record of the men’s pay.

Chapter 19 What she knew

Eydis had been late leaving that morning because she could not find a clean neckcloth. Runa found one in the wrong drawer. Svanhild said it had always been the right drawer for clean cloth. The disagreement was so small that the girl laughed at them both, took it and went out with the books she had promised to return.

For a few minutes Runa almost let the morning remain ordinary. Her mother asked whether the survey allowance could pay for the low household’s damaged floor. Runa said she would have to distinguish direct works damage from old weakness in the house. Svanhild told her the woman would have difficulty doing that while standing in the water. It was a fair objection. Runa could have spent the hour discussing it and postponed the paper again.

Instead she drew her mother’s young handwriting out of its wrapper.

Svanhild looked at the edge of the sheet before Runa laid it on the table. Something in her posture changed. She went to the shelf for the onions although dinner was still far off.

They had perhaps an hour before Eydis returned from Astri’s.

“I found your copy,” Runa said.

“My copy of what?”

“The old grant. In his chest.”

Her mother wiped her hands before touching it. Runa noticed that care and disliked herself for noticing. Svanhild read to the end, then turned the sheet as if the reverse might contain something she needed.

“He corrected that word.”

“Yes.”

“He had learned it differently.”

“You wrote the children of the house. He corrected which children, not whether there were any.”

Svanhild set the sheet down.

“He was particular.”

“So were you at the hearing.”

The knife stopped. From outside came the scrape of someone moving a basket along the wall. Runa waited until the sound had passed.

“You knew the fuller account.”

“I knew several.”

“You gave one.”

“People expect me to give one they can follow.”

“They followed it far enough to tell Torve to pack his benches.”

Her mother looked up sharply. Runa felt a brief satisfaction and was ashamed of it before it faded.

“Do not bring me a good line and think you have brought the whole matter.”

“Then tell me the rest.”

Svanhild reached for another onion. She took off its outer skin, found a bad place underneath and cut it away with more force than necessary.

“The old jarl promised things in storms. He promised what men wanted to hear when they had saved his boats. Then his son had to keep a hall, feed the people attached to it, pay crews and mend what winter broke. A landing given forever is still a landing someone needs next winter.”

“Torve’s family needed it.”

“They had it.”

“Until someone wanted it.”

“They worked there for generations.”

“Under a claim you helped make smaller.”

Svanhild put down the knife. Her hands lay flat on either side of the board.

“You think I had the power to give it back by saying a verse.”

“You had enough power to help take it away.”

The words came harder than Runa intended. She had not meant to arrive at an accusation so quickly. She had also meant exactly that accusation.

Her mother stood and went to the hearth. She adjusted the pot, although it did not need adjusting. Runa remained at the table, looking at the half-peeled onion.

After a while Svanhild said, “There were people the hall supported who had no landing to claim. No boats. No old paper in a chest.”

“Why did his household pay for them?”

“Everyone paid.”

“Not the same.”

“No. Not the same.” Her mother turned. “Do you want me to say I chose? I chose. I carried the account that would keep the service with the place. I thought that was what the valley could live with.”

Runa had wanted an admission. Having it did not make the next question easy.

“And you never told him.”

“What would you have had me tell him? That there was a phrase he could spend years fighting over while nobody repaired the boats?”

“Yes.”

Svanhild laughed once, without pleasure.

“Yes. Of course. You could have put it in a book, and then it would have been his proper choice.”

“He could have made it.”

“Could he? With whose food while he fought? Whose men at his door?”

Runa stood. Sitting while her mother moved about had begun to feel like being a child waiting for judgment. She carried the onion skins to the scrap bucket, then realized she was helping with the meal in the middle of the quarrel because she did not know what else to do with her hands.

“The hearing mattered to you,” she said. “You knew what your account would do.”

“I knew what I had told before.”

“That is not an answer.”

“You asked what I did. I have told you.”

They faced each other across the room. Runa could hear her own breath. She had imagined sadness, perhaps a difficult confession over the paper. She had not imagined wanting to shout at her mother until the neighbours heard.

Svanhild spoke first.

“You were fed by that hall. You learned your letters with a full stomach because people I spoke for made room. You went south wearing cloth bought with winter payment. Did you ask which promise it came from?”

Runa stared at her.

Her mother looked as if she wanted to stop and could not yet bear to surrender the words.

“You have had the benefit of a great many things you now find ugly.”

The bag her mother had sewn for school came into Runa’s mind with such unwelcome clarity that she had to look away. The good needle. The lamp on the stool. She had kept the bag for years after its strap broke.

“You said I could go.”

“I did.”

“You wanted me to.”

“Yes.”

Runa picked up the copied grant. Her fingers shook enough to make the paper sound. She hated that her mother could hear it.

“I didn’t know I would be asked to agree to this afterward.”

Svanhild’s face changed. “That is not what I said.”

Runa went to the door.

“Runa.”

She pulled on her coat without finding the second sleeve properly.

“Don’t go out with it open.”

The ordinary instruction almost stopped her. Then it made her angrier. She found the sleeve, opened the door and left.

Eydis was coming up the path with two books against her chest. She smiled, saw her mother’s face and stopped.

“Where are you going?”

“To the chapel.”

“Are you coming back for dinner?”

“I don’t know.”

The girl’s eyes moved towards the house. Runa wanted to tell her this was not about her. Saying it would give her another thing to worry about. She touched her daughter’s shoulder and went past.

At the chapel she found the door locked. For a moment she stood outside with all her anger and nowhere to put it. Then she sat on the cold step, folded the paper against her knees and waited for Astri to return.

Chapter 20 Ground that already has work

The keepers met them without ceremonial dress. One wore a patched coat Runa had seen at the fish work; the other carried a spade.

Petros had brought the revised line on a drawing. He kept it rolled until they had walked the ground. Runa was grateful for this, though she could not have said whether he had learned it from the failed alignment or simply judged that morning differently.

The land rose behind the reeds in a broad, uneven shelf. Sheep had grazed it close during the summer. A footpath crossed towards the warning station. Beyond a stand of small birches, three stones stood around a shallow basin where water had frozen under the last clear nights.

The keeper with the spade showed them the summer landing first. In one wind it was safer than the ordinary shore. People carried boats across a narrow strip of turf to reach it. A road bank across that strip would make the carrying difficult. Petros asked how wide a passage they needed and paced it with him.

Then they examined the grazing. Winter use was limited, but the right mattered in spring. The keepers did not claim every blade of grass could remain undisturbed. They wanted to know who would restore the broken ground and who would answer if a later crew widened the track.

“The agreement can name the work,” Runa said.

“Name the people too.”

“Some may leave.”

“Then name the people who replace them.”

The finance office’s proposed title would have made the work enforceable by successors without reopening consent. Here succession was the point at which someone needed to return and speak. Runa could translate the words. She could not make the two expectations identical.

Beyond the grazing they met two women moving a hurdle. One was shrine-devout enough to correct the keeper’s careless step near a marked root. The other wore a small Temple token and corrected him just as readily about where the sheep had been. Their authority seemed to rest on having come out in the weather before anyone carrying drawings.

Petros offered to help. The hurdle was caught on a root beneath the turf. He pulled from the wrong angle, stopped when told, and then supported the weight while one woman cut away a tangle with her knife. Runa held the far end. When they finally lifted it clear, a small animal darted from underneath and vanished into the grass. The woman with the token cursed it for frightening her, then checked that it had not left young behind.

“Which side do you need?” Runa asked, looking at the new road line.

“For the sheep? Both. We bring them up when the lower ground wets.”

“If the road is fenced?”

“Then a gate that opens. People draw gates and forget sheep are on the other side of them.”

Gerd said he could build one with a latch that would not freeze as easily as the old one. The women asked who would mend it. He said whoever was responsible for the fence. They looked at Runa. She wrote the question down without supplying a name she had no right to assign.

The keeper was impatient to continue, but one woman made him wait while she described a dispute from the previous spring. He had promised another strip for grazing during a rite that required the ordinary field to be kept clear. The substitute was wet. She wanted the promise remembered before they made new ones.

“I remember,” he said.

“Then this year remember before the lambs are standing in it.”

Runa watched him accept the rebuke. She was used to seeing his office at gatherings; she had rarely seen someone whose sheep inconvenienced it.

They carried the hurdle to its new place. Petros asked whether the women would inspect his proposed drainage before work began. They agreed, provided he sent word on a day when they were not moving animals. He wrote the day in his own small book. The keeper waited until the appointment was settled, then led them on.

At the basin the keeper asked them to wait while he cleared leaves from the ice with a bundle of twigs. Beneath the surface lay pale cuts, marks too clean for the weathered stone around them. Runa recognized two as witness signs. Others meant nothing to her.

Petros crouched, then stopped before touching.

“How old?” he asked.

The keeper named an event rather than a year. Runa supplied an approximate interval. Petros looked at the thin ice and the marks within it.

“Does it freeze like this each winter?”

“Yes.”

“And the cuts?”

“They stay.”

A drip fell from a branch onto the surface. The small circle it made widened over a mark and disappeared. Petros remained crouched for another moment, then stood. He put a symbol on his private sketch to keep the place clear of the works.

Runa thought of Torve’s grant and the ease with which a person might wish for an unquestionable record. She did not ask the ice anything. The keeper covered one exposed patch with leaves again before they moved on.

They ate at a low shelter used by people keeping winter rites. The food was ordinary: bread, a little fat, hot water flavoured with a bitter leaf. Petros took a second cup after the first had surprised him. The keeper with the spade told a story about a sheep that had repeatedly found a way through a gate everyone considered sound. Gerd explained how the gate must have been hung. The keeper listened until he finished and said the sheep was dead now, so the matter could rest.

Runa laughed harder than the joke required. She had slept badly and had been holding herself ready for another dispute all morning.

Before they left the shelter, a man arrived carrying a narrow plank wrapped in cloth. He set it on the bench and unfolded it with the care Runa had seen used for children too sleepy to stand.

Three names had been cut into the wood. One was old, its edges polished by handling. Two were new enough that pale shavings remained in the deeper strokes. The keeper with the spade washed his hands, though there was little warmth in the water, and asked who had come with the man.

“My sister was to come.”

“Where is she?”

“Her son is ill.”

The keeper looked towards the other keeper. Neither spoke for a moment. The man stood with one hand on the cloth, keeping it from blowing over the names.

“Can you wait?”

“Until the afternoon. Not another day.”

Runa had begun to move away. The man asked her to stay. She looked at the keeper, who nodded.

“He needs someone who can hear him clearly. Your engineer may stay too if he will stand where I ask.”

Petros came to the doorway when Runa translated. He removed his gloves and waited. The keeper told him that he need not repeat words he did not understand. Then they went out to a sheltered place behind the low wall.

The man carried the plank. He held it higher than was comfortable, both palms beneath it. When the keeper named the first person, the man answered with a place and a season. At the second name his voice failed. He tried again, too quickly, and was told to take his breath before speaking.

Runa watched the wood. One of the new names had a corrected letter, the first cut still faintly visible beneath the second. Whoever had carved it had not been able to take back the mistake. The man shifted his thumb away from the correction and spoke the place.

At the third name the keeper asked a question Runa had not heard before. She knew the words separately and could not be certain what the answer promised. The man gave it without hesitation.

They stood through a silence. Wind lifted the edge of the cloth. Petros caught it before it covered the plank and held the corner without otherwise moving. The man nodded his thanks but did not look away from the names.

Nothing happened that Runa could identify. She was aware of being cold, of one foot beginning to ache, of the keeper’s breath showing white and then disappearing. A bird called beyond the wall. The sound came again from farther off, or from another bird.

At last the keeper touched the plank. The man lowered it with visible relief. He wrapped the cloth around it himself.

“Do I take it home?”

“Until she can come.”

He nodded. His face had changed, but Runa would not have known what to call the change if asked. Back in the shelter he drank the remainder of the warm water and spoke about the sick boy’s appetite. The keeper recommended a woman nearby who had helped another child keep food down. Petros offered to send word to the clinic on his return. The man accepted.

While the man put on his gloves, Runa asked whether her own presence needed to be entered anywhere.

“He heard you answer when I asked if you could hear,” the keeper said. “He knows where you stood.”

She remembered making the answer almost without attending to it. That unsettled her more than the marks in the ice. She had expected to be told when the important part began.

The man thanked her at the door. He thanked Petros too, using the engineer’s name carefully after Runa supplied it. Then he set off with the wrapped plank beneath his arm, walking at the ordinary pace of a person with a long way to go.

Petros watched until the turn hid him. He asked only whether the boy’s house was on their return route. It was not. Gerd said he could carry the message.

After the meal one keeper took Gerd to inspect a place where turf might be stacked. The other asked Runa to walk the warning path with him. Petros followed until the keeper stopped at a narrow turn and said only one of them was needed for this part.

The engineer waited without offence. Runa was less comfortable. She did not want to be selected merely because she could be presumed to share beliefs she had allowed to become unfamiliar.

The keeper showed her where the path crossed a low wall. A small flat stone lay beside it, its upper face worn smooth. She remembered standing here as a child while Svanhild spoke to someone out of sight.

“Will the workers use this way?” he asked.

“If it’s the shorter route.”

“Tell them to go below the wall.”

“Is this closed?”

“To their loads.”

“And to them?”

“If they are coming here, they may come here. If they are going somewhere else, let them take the other path.”

Runa considered the distinction. The detour was small. On a drawing it would look arbitrary, and someone farther south would ask why she had accepted it.

“Does anyone have to stay to direct them?”

“For the first days. Afterward they should know.”

“We would need to pay for that time.”

The keeper nodded. He did not appear offended by the practical answer. She was relieved enough to dislike how much relief she felt.

On the return they met a young woman carrying a bundle of dry rushes. She greeted the keeper and went past Runa without waiting for an introduction. At the wall she set down the bundle, removed something small from her sleeve and placed it where the stone had worn smooth. Runa looked away before she could identify it.

Petros was kneeling beside a drainage hollow when they returned. He had taken advantage of the wait to examine the ground. His knees were wet again. Runa told him the workers would need the lower path for loads and a local attendant for the first days. He asked the cost, marked the route, and did not ask what had been left at the wall.

They marked a provisional boundary for drainage work. No stone would be moved without the keeper present. The warning path would remain passable. Turf would be stacked where it could be used again. These conditions allowed the men to begin before the permanent land agreement was settled. Petros repeated them to Gerd, and Runa wrote copies for both sides.

One keeper would not put his mark to a phrase giving authority to any future engineer appointed by the office.

“I don’t know him.”

“He would be bound by the same terms.”

“Then he can come and say so.”

Runa crossed out the phrase for this provisional work. It would return when the finance office read the larger agreement. She left enough room in her notes to explain why.

On the way home Petros asked whether the keeper had been angry.

“No.”

“He smiled while refusing.”

“He liked you.”

“That is encouraging.”

“He still refused.”

“I noticed.”

At the turn towards Svanhild’s house Runa said she needed to visit the outer tally. It was true that she had a question there. It was also a long way round. Petros offered to accompany her, then saw her expression and said he would go back to his drawing.

She walked alone until the house was hidden behind the rise. Only then did she slow down. Her feet were wet. The hot leaf water had left a taste in her mouth which she found she wanted again.

That evening she found herself explaining more of it in her report than either man had required. She crossed out two sentences. The lower path added a short carrying distance. The cost belonged in the estimate. The rest had not been entrusted to her to make persuasive.

Chapter 21 Two places to eat

Eydis brought the message herself, though no one had asked her to.

“Grandmother says the spare blanket is in the chest if you’re cold.”

Runa looked up from the chapel table. Her daughter stood in the doorway with a book tucked under her arm, providing a second reason for the visit. It was late enough that the school had emptied.

“I’m only working here.”

“I know.”

“I’m sleeping at home.”

“I know.”

Runa moved a stool for her. Eydis sat without putting down the book. Her cheeks were red from the walk and her expression had the guarded patience Runa remembered using with adults when she was young.

“What has your grandmother—” Runa stopped. “What have you been doing?”

Eydis looked at her.

“Learning the mast.”

“The one in the bowls account?”

“Yes.”

“Is it going well?”

“Sometimes.”

The small answers hurt. Runa knew she had invited them by beginning the wrong question.

“I’m sorry. I shouldn’t ask you to bring things between us.”

“You didn’t.”

“No.”

“I wanted to see you.”

Runa put down the pen. She reached for Eydis’s hand, and the girl gave it without moving closer. Her fingers were cold. There was a spot of ink near the nail of her smallest finger.

“Stay while I finish this. We’ll walk back together.”

“Are you eating there?”

“Yes.”

Her daughter put the book on the table.

Runa did not finish the work that evening. She got far enough to leave it intelligible to herself in the morning, then went home before the promised time became another disappointment. Svanhild had made soup. She asked whether the shrine keepers would allow the new line. Runa gave the practical answer. Nobody mentioned the grant.

The first dinner was uncomfortable mostly because everyone tried to make it easy.

Svanhild asked Eydis about a book whose title she already knew. Runa praised the soup before tasting it. The girl answered too fully, describing every part of the day’s lesson until it became clear she was afraid to leave a silence. Runa wanted to stop her and could not find a way that would not make the effort visible.

At last Svanhild asked for the salt. Eydis passed the whole bowl and dropped the spoon. It struck the floor with a little clatter. Nobody had done anything terrible, but the girl burst into tears.

Runa rose. Svanhild rose too, and both stopped because their simultaneous movement made Eydis cry harder.

“I’ll get it,” the girl said angrily.

“Leave it,” Runa answered.

“I said I’ll get it.”

She picked up the spoon, took it to the washing bowl and scrubbed it with more force than necessary. Runa remained beside her chair. Her mother sat down first.

When Eydis returned, Svanhild moved her own cup aside and gave the girl a little more room. No one asked what was wrong. They ate for several minutes before Runa said she had mishandled the tally at the hall and let Oddrun make a fool of her. The account was funny enough without alteration. Eydis began to listen. By the time Runa reached the third explanation of where Solli ate, the girl was smiling against her will.

Svanhild supplied one detail Runa had forgotten. Their eyes met briefly. The conversation continued until the bowls were empty.

Upstairs, Eydis asked whether her mother would sleep at the chapel if the argument grew worse.

“No.”

“You eat there.”

“I work there.”

“You eat there.”

Runa sat on the bed. “Yes. Sometimes I have eaten there because I didn’t want to quarrel here.”

“Then you come back and don’t quarrel loudly.”

The accuracy hurt. Runa drew the blanket over the girl’s knees, then stopped fussing with it.

“I don’t know how to make this comfortable yet.”

Eydis nodded as if she had known that already and needed her mother to know it too.

“Will you tell me before you go anywhere?”

“Yes.”

“Even if you’re angry.”

“Yes.”

Runa stayed until the girl no longer seemed to be waiting for another answer. Downstairs Svanhild had washed the spoon again and laid it with the others. Runa picked up a cloth and dried the bowls.

For several days they managed like this. Runa ate some meals at home, others at the chapel. She gave reasons each time, many of them true. Eydis learned which reasons could be questioned and which would make both women too attentive to her face.

The outer tally changed when a crew returned unexpectedly.

They had meant to winter farther south. A dispute over payment and a damaged mast brought them home instead. Their families were glad to see them, then had to feed them. Runa walked out with revised sheets and found an entire house occupied by people drying clothes. A man slept upright beside the hearth. Another was telling the same part of the voyage for the third time, improving his own answer to the harbour master with each repetition.

The host disliked the Temple. She said so while ladling broth for Petros, who had come to inspect a drain nearby.

“Your brothers sold us dear grain after promising cheap.”

“Which house supplied it?” he asked.

She named the harbour.

“That was not our store.”

“It had a Temple man beside the scale.”

“He may have witnessed the measure.”

“Then he saw the price.”

Petros accepted the bowl. “I don’t know that transaction.”

She seemed to prefer this answer to his first. She gave him more bread.

Runa counted the returned crew by speaking with the person managing the food rather than asking each man where he belonged. This worked better. It did not prevent an argument about a sailor who said he would stay with his brother while his brother’s wife shook her head behind him. Runa waited until they had settled it themselves.

Afterward the talk moved to a marriage proposed before the voyage. The sailor concerned had returned with a fine belt and very little money. His intended bride had apparently acquired a preference for a quieter man while he was away. He insisted the quieter man was not quiet on principle, only slow to think. The host said thought had its attractions.

Petros asked Runa to translate when the table laughed. She could explain the words, but part of the joke concerned an incident at a sheep sale years earlier. Hervi, who had brought the crew in, tried to supply that too. By the time he finished, Petros was laughing at the effort of explanation rather than the original insult.

The man with the fine belt asked Runa to write a letter.

He began as though dictating to a clerk at a counting house, telling the woman he had hoped to marry that circumstances had delayed his return. The host snorted into her bowl. He glared at her and said it was a private letter.

“You are dictating it across my dinner.”

He looked towards the other room. There were three sleeping men in it, one of whom had just begun to snore. Runa asked whether he wanted to wait until later. He said no, he had already waited too long.

She found a scrap and asked for the woman’s name. He gave it. Then he said my dear and stopped.

“Does she let you call her that?” the host asked.

“She used to.”

“Then perhaps begin with her name.”

Runa wrote the name alone. The sailor described a payment he expected to receive, a house he could take a share in, and the belt, which he had brought for himself but would sell if necessary. Runa wrote none of that until he told her which part he wanted the woman to hear.

“All of it.”

“She will wonder why you bought the belt.”

“I had the money then.”

“Then say that.”

The table had quieted. He rubbed the edge of the leather with his thumb, lifting the bright buckle slightly to examine it.

“It looked finer where I bought it,” he said.

Petros, who had followed very little of the exchange, smiled at the belt. The sailor smiled back automatically, then shook his head.

“Don’t write that.”

Runa had not. She asked what he would say if he were at the woman’s door. For once the host let the question remain unanswered until he was ready.

“I thought I’d be back before the winter gatherings.”

Runa wrote it.

“I bought things as if I would.”

She wrote that too.

He drew a breath. “I don’t expect you to wait because I was a fool.”

The host put down her bowl. Nobody laughed.

Runa read the three sentences back. He asked whether they made him sound weak. She said they sounded like what he had said. He read the sentences once more. He added a request to meet, gave a day he could keep, and took the paper to place his mark beneath it.

The host fetched a little food to go with the messenger. She had been mocking him throughout the meal; now she told him which boat might carry the letter without waiting for the whole crew’s accounts to settle. He thanked her awkwardly.

When he went outside, she looked at Runa.

“She may say no.”

“Yes.”

“Don’t make him a better promise when he comes back.”

Runa folded the unused part of the sheet. She had no better promise to give him. She hoped he would not come back to ask.

The quieter man, somebody said after a while, had fine sheep. The host threw a cloth at the speaker. Conversation resumed, unevenly at first, then with the eagerness of people released from an unexpected silence. Petros asked whether Runa had been writing an agreement. She said a letter. He glanced at the doorway and seemed to understand enough not to ask whose.

Runa stayed longer than she intended. Someone handed her a bowl to dry. A child fell asleep against her knee, preventing her from moving until the mother came to lift him. For a while she had no useful task to organize, and no one needed her to reconcile an account.

When she left, the host wrapped a portion of food for Svanhild.

“She likes this.”

Runa took it. “Thank you.”

“Tell her I haven’t forgotten the year she brought fish when my roof went.”

“I will.”

The parcel grew cold on the walk. At home her mother opened it and smiled before she could prevent herself.

“From the outer house?”

“Yes.”

Svanhild tasted a little from the edge. She made a small appreciative sound which Runa had not heard for days.

“I said I’d give Petros the revised figures,” Runa said.

“Now?”

“He needs them.”

Her mother put down the food. Runa left before the warmth of bringing it could be mistaken for a settlement she had not made.

Chapter 22 Nine doors

The chapel lamp was still burning when Runa returned for the folder she had forgotten.

Petros sat at the table with a row of small iron fittings before him. He moved one from the left-hand pile to the right, paused, and moved it back. Runa stood in the doorway long enough to see him begin the same count again.

“Are there too few?”

He looked up abruptly.

“No.”

“Too many?”

He smiled without amusement. “That would be a useful difficulty.”

She came in and closed the door. The room was warm near the stove and cold everywhere else. His drawing lay under a board to keep its edges flat. Beside it stood an untouched cup.

Runa found her folder on the shelf. She could have left then. Instead she took off her gloves.

“What are they for?”

“The temporary landing. If we use it.”

“You’ve counted them already.”

“Yes.”

She sat. He gathered the fittings into a cloth, then opened it again because one had fallen beneath the drawing. His hands were steady, but his face looked exhausted.

“Bad news?” she asked.

“A dream.”

Runa waited.

“I had it before coming here. I mention that because I don’t want you to think the place is doing it to me.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“I know.” He rubbed the bridge of his nose. “There is a passage. Doors along it. I know where I must go, or I think I do. By the time I reach the end, I have counted them.”

“How many?”

He looked at her.

She wished she had not asked. “And then?”

“I wake.”

“Every time?”

“Sometimes I am still walking.”

The stove made a settling noise. Runa drew one foot closer to it.

“What is behind them?”

“I don’t know.”

“Are they shut?”

“I think so.” He laughed faintly. “You see how little I have to be frightened of.”

She did not agree with him. As a child she had been told to leave a bad dream uncarried until daylight, then speak it where ordinary work could interrupt. Her mother would have opened a door, made her wash something, asked which way the dreamer had been facing. In the south she had heard different reassurances, equally confident. None seemed useful to offer Petros as a completed remedy.

“Do you want company?”

“For a while.”

The cup beside Petros had cooled until a skin formed on the surface. Runa offered to warm it. He shook his head, then changed his mind and handed it over. She took it to the stove, poured it into a small pan and found the fire lower than either had noticed.

“Have you been sitting here long?”

“I couldn’t tell you.”

“Before the last bell?”

“Yes.”

She added fuel. The first piece smoked. Petros came to adjust it, bringing an engineer’s unnecessary concentration to a stove he had already used all winter. For a moment she nearly told him she could manage a fire. Then she made room.

He found a dry piece underneath and placed it where the air would reach. They stood watching until a flame took hold. The ordinary success seemed to help him more than anything she had said.

“I sometimes walk in the dream with someone,” he told her.

“Who?”

“I never see enough to know. I am certain while I am there, and afterward I cannot say.”

“Do they speak?”

He frowned, trying to remember. “I think I am the one speaking. Explaining where we have to go.”

Runa waited. He looked embarrassed by the smallness of what he could give her.

“I have led people through bad places awake,” he said. “That doesn’t improve the dream. It makes me think I ought to know what I’m doing in it.”

She poured the warmed drink back into the cup. It was slightly scorched. He drank anyway.

“Have you spoken to the sister?”

“About sleeplessness.”

“And the doors?”

“No.”

“Why?”

Petros looked towards the drawn curtains. “I don’t want someone else inside it yet.”

Runa understood that better than any account of the passage. She had brought enough private things into other people’s records during this winter to feel the force of the refusal.

“All right,” she said.

He nodded, and neither raised the question again.

They repaired a strap on the instrument case. The buckle tongue had bent, making the leather difficult to release. Petros held it against a small block while Runa tapped it with the back of a tool. The first attempt went too far. He bent it back, carefully, and they tried again.

After a few minutes he asked about the departure count.

“Hervi told me the young man chose it.”

“He did.”

“And the people knew what he meant.”

“Yes.”

“The ordinary bell troubles your mother more.”

Runa checked the buckle. “It troubles her differently.”

“Can you explain?”

She thought of Svanhild’s hand on Eydis’s sleeve, of people raising their voices while a sailor was sick behind the shed.

“Not as well as she could.”

“Would she?”

“Perhaps not to a man who begins by knowing she is mistaken.”

Petros took that without protest. He threaded the strap through the repaired buckle. It held.

“I don’t know what I think about your mother,” he said.

“Neither do I at present.”

He smiled then, properly. She smiled back. The fittings remained wrapped on the table. He did not count them again while she was there.

When Runa left, the lamp was still lit. Petros had begun a letter and seemed glad of the task. She walked home under a clear sky, listening to the crunch of old snow beneath her boots. At the house she shut the door softly so she would not wake Eydis, and paused with her hand on the latch until her breathing had slowed.

Chapter 23 A rhythm for stopping

The bell rope was easier to move than Eydis expected and harder to stop.

The attendant, Osvin, stood beside her with one hand above hers. He had shown her the counterweight and made her walk round the frame before touching anything. Now he let her feel the first movement, caught the returning pull and told her to keep her feet where he had put them.

“Don’t follow it up.”

“I wasn’t.”

“You were thinking of it.”

Everyone seemed able to tell what she was thinking in Varnvik. Eydis leaned back slightly. The bell swung, and its sound filled her chest so abruptly that she let go.

Osvin caught the rope.

“That is why we practise.”

Below, Runa stood with the works crews. Petros had explained the signal in both languages. Ordinary hours were rung with an even interval. For withdrawal from the temporary works, the bell would give three quick strokes, another three, and another three, followed by a pause and repetition. The men had to stop loading, secure what could be secured without delay, and move to the marked safe ground. Hearing the bell was only the first part.

Eydis had been allowed upstairs because she was carrying the tally sheets between her mother and Osvin. She had asked whether she could try. Osvin said yes after Runa agreed, then spent so long showing her how that she began to wish she had asked something simpler.

Now he let her try again.

The rope resisted, yielded, and came back. She felt the weight through her arms. The sound was enormous at this distance. Osvin called the intervals, using his hand when she could no longer hear him properly. She completed one group and stopped. Her palms tingled.

“Enough for the moment.”

“I can do more.”

“I know. We are going to let the men hear me do it correctly.”

She surrendered the rope, embarrassed and relieved.

The first rehearsal below went badly. Two men continued lifting a load because they thought the signal meant prepare to stop after finishing. Another moved to the wrong bank. Runa sent everyone back to the beginning and had the instructions repeated. A shrine worker refused to take part until the nine had been discussed.

It had already been discussed, but that did not mean he had agreed.

Eydis came down in time to hear him ask why eight could not be enough.

“Because the crews from the south have used this rhythm,” Runa said. “Some of the men here have learned it with them. If we change it, we must make sure everyone changes.”

“Then teach them.”

“We are teaching this one now.”

“That is not the same answer.”

“No,” her mother said. “It isn’t.”

Petros stood nearby without intervening. Eydis expected him to argue that the ninth did nothing. He asked instead whether the man would withdraw when he heard it even if he would not join the practice. The man said he was not a fool. Petros thanked him and asked where he would take the people beside him.

They went over the safe ground together.

The second rehearsal was harder for Eydis to watch because she now knew which movement should come next.

A load was placed at the shore as though ready to go aboard. The men took up their usual positions. Petros called that this was still practice, then signalled to Osvin. When the groups began, the men on the stage secured what they could in reach and moved back. One worker tried to drag a sack away. Torve told him to leave it.

“It’ll get wet.”

“It is full of stones.”

“I mean on the day.”

“On the day you’ll have other work.”

They walked him through the path again. Eydis could see how the sack narrowed the space for the next person. Without it there was room to turn and take someone else’s arm. With it there was only the load and a man trying to keep his feet.

Jost, beside her, whispered that he would never leave a whole sack of grain. Eydis said he would if it was wet already. He said she would say anything now she was the bell girl. She elbowed him, then regretted it because he had a bruise there from carrying a kettle.

“Sorry.”

He shrugged, pretending it hadn’t hurt. They watched the third attempt without talking.

This time the workers reached the marked ground in good order. Then one man said he had forgotten his brother. The brother was perfectly safe at the other post, but the remark started a useful argument about who accounted for each crew. Runa brought out the roster. Petros had them point to the places where they would gather. Torve wanted no man to go searching alone beneath the stage. Someone would always think he had seen someone else there.

Eydis listened until the names became a blur. The signal had seemed the whole task when she first held the rope. Now the adults kept discovering further things that had to happen after its sound. She rubbed her sore palm against her skirt and watched her mother crossing out a crew position.

At the end Osvin came down carrying his own list. He and Runa compared duties. The bell man’s ordinary errands would be taken by another clerk during the passage. He would look from the high opening at the interval between checks of the incoming tally, and a runner would bring anything he needed from below. Eydis was to help with marks, not be left as the lookout.

Her mother made her repeat that. Eydis did, impatiently, then felt ashamed when Osvin thanked her as seriously as he had thanked the men.

Osvin took Eydis back upstairs to show her where the tally would lie.

There was a shelf just inside the opening, out of the worst weather. The corner near the wall was damp. He put the board across two blocks so the paper would not touch the stone and weighted its lower edge with a smooth piece of iron. Eydis wanted to know why he did not keep it downstairs. He said he usually did, but on a working day the marks needed checking without a journey for every question.

“People will still ask.”

“They will.”

“Even if it’s written.”

“Especially if it is.”

She laughed, expecting him to join her, but he was adjusting the blocks. The shelf wobbled. He took a wedge from his pocket and made it steady.

He showed her the difference between a load received at Varnvik and a load delivered beyond the gut. The first had one mark. The second had another. A sack which had been brought to the store and then sent onward appeared in both places without becoming two sacks. Eydis said she knew that. He handed her the practice tally.

The first question was easy. The next contained a crossed-out load which she counted because the original mark remained visible. He waited while she found the mistake herself. Her face grew hot. She corrected it and asked for another.

“You aren’t being examined.”

“I know.”

He gave her another anyway. This time she answered too cautiously, checking every line twice. He told her where he would put a separate list of damaged cargo so that it did not disappear among the ordinary deliveries. Then he showed her the bell rope’s resting place, where a loose end could lie without catching a person coming through the door.

“Not round the wrist,” he said.

“I didn’t.”

“People do when they are tired.”

She looked at the rope. Downstairs it was a sound everyone could argue about. Up here it had a worn place where hands had smoothed the fibres and a pale strand lifting near the knot. She wanted to ask whether it had ever carried somebody upward. She did not want him to think her afraid of trying again.

Osvin followed her eyes.

“I’ll bind that before the work.”

“Did it break?”

“No. I would replace it if it had.”

He took a length of finer cord from a box. As he wound the loose strand down, Eydis watched the same movement repeat. His hands were broad and reddened. One nail was split almost to the quick. He worked without appearing to feel it.

“Do you ring at every hour?”

“Not at night.”

“Grandmother says people ought to know what they’re asking.”

Osvin paused with the cord between his fingers. Eydis wished she had put the question differently.

“I ask them to come to the lesson,” he said. “Or to stop for the service. Or, on this day, to leave the works.”

“Do you have to think it?”

“Think what?”

“What you’re asking.”

He pulled the binding tight. “It is easier to remember what I am doing if I do.”

That was not an answer about the ninth. She had not quite asked about the ninth. She looked through the opening instead, following the white rock to the dark seam beyond. From this height both were small and sharp.

Jost called from below. He had brought the wrong practice board and wanted to know whether he should take it back. Osvin went to the stair and asked him to bring it up so they could see what was wrong with it. Jost arrived breathing hard, carrying a board so large it would not fit on the shelf.

“I said it was wrong.”

“Yes,” Osvin said. “You did.”

They used the back to work out the loads. Jost was better at remembering which man carried which sack than at following the rows of marks. Eydis corrected a number. He supplied a house she had forgotten. Osvin let them dispute the total until they discovered that each had included the same returned load in a different place.

It took longer than doing it alone. Eydis liked it better. When they carried the board down together, she let Jost choose which end went first through the door.

Svanhild watched from the path. Eydis saw her before Runa did and felt, absurdly, as if she had been caught doing something behind her grandmother’s back. Osvin had done nothing secret. Her mother had agreed. Yet Eydis wanted to hide the red line the rope had left on her palm.

Svanhild asked to see it when the practice ended.

“It doesn’t hurt.”

“That is not what I asked.”

Her grandmother turned the hand over, examined it, and let it go. She did not tell Eydis she should not have tried.

They walked towards the warning station afterward. The old footbridge had a new temporary rail where the handline had sagged. Workers were clearing a drain on the higher alignment. One greeted Eydis as the bell girl. She liked it until she saw Svanhild’s face.

At the white rock her grandmother asked for the mark. Eydis found it immediately. She found the second point too, where a slanting seam met the water behind the bank. They walked farther uphill, then looked back from another angle.

“From the tower you can see both,” Eydis said.

“Yes.”

“Osvin showed me the window.”

“Good.”

“Will Tova come back here?”

“She is at the clinic.”

“I know. I mean later.”

Svanhild drew her hood closer. “They must settle who keeps it.”

“Mother has put money in the estimate.”

“Your mother has put many useful things in the estimate.”

Eydis could not tell whether that was praise. She did not ask.

Below them the channel watchers were changing places. One climbed onto a low post to see over the nearer bank. From where Eydis stood, he looked as though he could see everything. Then she moved down a few steps and lost the dark line behind the reeds.

She climbed back up. Svanhild waited while she looked again.

Chapter 24 What winter leaves available

The thaw did not begin with open water. It began with dripping in rooms that had been dry all winter.

Runa woke to a bowl being set beneath the wall. By midday the path had become a channel of dirty water between banks of old snow. The higher ground remained white and hard. Down by the landing, ice loosened from the posts and turned slowly against the shore.

The main approach was still closed. Swell struck the headland from beyond the sheltered inlet, breaking high enough for people in Varnvik to see the spray. Hervi went out to examine the inner route and returned with a clear account of what a small boat might do and what a grain ship could not.

There was grain at the southern store. Runa had the receipt for it and a message confirming the amount. That was reassuring until she laid the delivery route beside the household figures.

The hall could last longer than the outer houses. Some families had fish but little meal. Others had grain which had taken damp, or additional mouths from the returned crew. Runa and the provisioners began moving what was already in Varnvik before asking anyone to risk the sea. The first carts went out on repaired stretches and stopped where the crossing remained difficult.

At the hall store Runa asked for the oldest sound sacks to be brought forward. The provisioner said they were already forward. They went inside together, and he showed her the sequence of marks on the cloth: what had arrived before the hard freeze, what had been moved from a leaking corner, what belonged to a household rather than the common store.

She apologized for beginning with an instruction before asking what he had done. He accepted the apology absently, too occupied with the work to reward it. They checked the bottom of one sack and found it sound. The next was damp where it had rested against a beam. He cut a small opening, took a sample and gave it to her to smell.

“Can it be used?”

“Soon. Warm and spread. Otherwise less of it every day.”

They arranged to bring the damp grain to the larger kitchen. The cooks had not been waiting idle for the opportunity. One was preparing food for people coming off the works, another keeping a pot for the clinic, and the woman who could judge the grain had gone home to tend her own hearth. A boy was sent to fetch her. While they waited, Runa helped clear a table that had been intended for bread.

Dagrin came in wanting a count of what could leave the hall before the relief returned. Runa gave the working quantity, not the total in the store. He objected that she had reserved too much for drying losses. The provisioner answered by setting a handful of grain on the table between them.

“Eat that if you want to lend it whole.”

Dagrin picked up a few grains, smelled them, and put them back. He accepted the reserve. He also refused to release one family’s privately stored meal without speaking to them, although Runa knew they had more than several of their neighbours.

“They are away,” she said.

“Their daughter is here.”

“She cannot authorize—”

“She manages it when they are away. Ask her.”

Runa found the young woman in the back room making up bundles of cloth. She agreed to lend a defined amount, then asked who would repay it if the grain boats failed to return. Runa named the undertaking being assembled and admitted that the answer was not yet secure. The woman reduced the amount by a sack.

Runa disliked watching it go back into the private reserve. She could also see the younger children asleep by the wall. She recorded the smaller loan and carried the receipt to Dagrin without asking him to press for more.

Outside, two men had begun harnessing an animal to the wrong sledge. Gerd stopped them before they tightened the traces. Nothing had gone badly wrong yet, but the work was full of little opportunities.

Runa accompanied the first local load as far as the outer turn, where it had to be divided for carrying.

The grain had not travelled by sea. It had come from dry stores within sight of the hall, and still they needed five people and the use of a neighbour’s straps to get it where it was wanted. The narrowest part of the path held old ice beneath a skin of running water. Gerd had scattered grit, but feet had already carried much of it downhill. Runa walked behind a woman whose two small sacks were balanced across a pole. Each step set them swinging a little in opposite directions.

She offered to take one. The woman asked whether Runa could take both. One alone would make the pole unmanageable. Runa could not, and said so.

“Then tell me if the back one loosens.”

Runa watched the knot for the rest of the climb.

At the receiving house the little girl from their first survey came to the door. Runa remembered her blinking with every blow of the fish knife. Now she wanted to know whether the sack was theirs. Her mother told her to make room. The girl stepped aside but continued watching the grain as if its ownership might change while she looked away.

The mother checked the amount before signing the receipt. She had borrowed from a cousin already and wanted this entry kept distinct. Runa read out the lender and the promised use of the arriving southern grain for repayment. The woman asked whether she would be charged if the sea prevented that grain arriving. Runa explained the household had received a distribution, not contracted a private loan. She wrote the distinction in the margin at the woman’s request.

While the carriers rested, the woman opened a cupboard and took out a small bowl of meal. Runa thought she meant to compare it with the delivered grain. Instead she poured it back into a separate sack and tied the neck.

“I was keeping that for him.”

“Your husband?”

“The child.”

A boy lay asleep beyond the hearth. Runa had not seen him on her earlier visit because he had been with a relative. He was older than Eydis, all knees and long feet. The woman said he had been working on the higher path until the crust broke under one of the men. Now there was no pay from that route.

“Does he know?” Runa asked.

“That I keep some? Of course. He complains about it and eats it.”

She tucked the little sack away. The girl at the door asked whether they could have bread that night. Her mother said they could begin preparing it. The child’s delight was so immediate that Runa looked down at the receipt rather than watch it.

They stayed long enough to drink something warm. The carrier with the pole loosened her belt and complained of a pain between her shoulders. The host pressed her thumb beside the place while the woman leaned forward, and they discussed whose roof might need work once the thaw came properly. The boy woke, listened and said he could help. His mother told him to wake his feet before promising them to anyone.

On the way back Runa carried the empty sacks. They weighed almost nothing. Their ease made her ashamed of how tired she felt until she reached the icy stretch and had to concentrate on keeping upright. The carrier caught her elbow once, then let go. At the turn they could see the sea breaking beyond the headland. No grain ship could come through that whiteness.

The woman with the pole asked whether Runa thought the small boats could do it.

“Hervi is choosing the turn.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“I hope they can.”

The woman nodded. She had wanted no larger assurance. They walked back into Varnvik with the empty sacks flapping against Runa’s coat.

People helped. They also asked when their own stores would be replaced. Runa wrote each transfer down and gave copies to those who wanted them. She did not call every loan a gift because the hour was urgent.

The higher path could not take laden sledges without days of clearing. Men sent to inspect it returned after one had broken through crust to his thigh. He was not badly hurt, but the report ended the suggestion that they could simply go round the water.

Petros and Torve examined the ferry. The ordinary boat would not serve alone at the necessary pace. A raft arrangement using available hulls, a controlled crossing line and a temporary stage could move more. They would need timber, suitable fastenings and rested crews. Petros refused to tell Runa how many days it would take until Torve had inspected the hulls offered.

Hervi wanted small boats at the lee cove on the next suitable turn. He would lead them through the skerries from the southern store if the wind held in the expected quarter. The grain would then come by sledge along the raised track to Varnvik and onward to the gut. Every part depended on another part being ready.

Runa made a list of boats and crews from the roll.

By evening three entries had failed.

One boat was already pledged to carry timber for a house farther inland. Its owner could not release it without speaking to the people who had supplied the winter repair. A crew listed under one master would not sail under the man Runa had assumed could command them. Two cousins with useful experience refused to share a crossing watch. Neither denied the need; each offered an account in which the other man’s presence was the unnecessary complication.

Runa had written their names neatly in adjacent lines.

The difficulty with the cousins occupied most of an hour she had thought available for something else.

They came separately to the chapel, each expecting his own explanation to settle the matter. The first said he had no quarrel that should interfere with feeding neighbours. He then described three things the other man must not be allowed to do. The second said he would serve anywhere, provided no one made him answer to a thief.

“Has he been found to have stolen?” Runa asked.

“He has been found with my knife.”

“By whom?”

“By me.”

“And did he return it?”

“He said it was his.”

Runa closed her eyes briefly. She was tired enough for the knife to seem intolerably small. When she opened them, the man had seen her expression.

“It was my father’s.”

She apologized. He accepted without becoming more willing to work beside his cousin.

She tried separate shifts at the same station. One would arrive before the other left, and neither trusted the overlap. She tried giving both equal responsibility. That produced two people each refusing to let the other decide how a boat would be handled. The need for grain remained undisputed throughout.

After they had gone, Runa took the roster to Petros. He read it, asked whether two different posts were possible, and pointed to the store leg of the route.

“They won’t want to be sent away from home merely because the other is difficult,” she said.

“No.”

“Do you have someone who can speak to them?”

He looked at her without answering. She knew whom he meant, and resented him for being sensible.

Outside, people were already trying to repair a sledge for a journey which still lacked enough boats. The sound of hammering followed her as she walked.

She went home with the list folded so often it would no longer lie flat. Svanhild sat near the fire mending Eydis’s cuff. The girl was asleep upstairs; the day’s tally work had tired her.

“Have you eaten?” her mother asked.

“Not yet.”

“There is soup.”

Runa remained by the door.

“I need your help with the boats.”

Svanhild put down the cuff.

“Which boats?”

Runa took out the list and moved a cup so she could lay it on the table. Her mother rose to stand beside her. The room was warm. For the first time in days Runa did not immediately want to leave it.

Part Four The winter passage

Chapter 25 The boats already pledged

Svanhild read the list with a finger beneath each line. Twice she asked who had supplied a name. Once she laughed, briefly, and then apologized because Runa was too tired to find it funny.

“He died last winter.”

“The boat is still entered under him.”

“The boat is still afloat. He is less available.”

Runa crossed out the man’s name and left the vessel. Her mother fetched a stool. They sat side by side, with the soup between them growing cool.

The first pledged boat belonged in practice to three households. One supplied the hull, another the sail and rigging, and the third the men who had repaired it. Runa had entered the person who paid the landing dues. Svanhild knew which woman had to release the timber promise before the boat could carry grain.

“Will she?”

“If she is asked before everyone is told she must.”

Runa wrote the name on a separate sheet.

The crew would follow the dead master’s eldest daughter for ordinary work but not for this passage, because she had never piloted the skerries in breakup. They would follow Hervi if their own steersman stood beside him. Hervi might agree. He would dislike learning that someone else had arranged it before he was consulted.

“And the cousins?” Runa asked.

Her mother leaned back. “Keep them apart.”

“We need both shifts covered.”

“Put one at the store and one at the gut. The coast is wide enough for two fools.”

They worked until the list had become a page of journeys Runa needed to make. Some Svanhild could make with her. Others would be better made without the hall’s singer standing in the doorway. Oddrun would speak to the returned crew. Selka knew one of the boat owners through her husband’s kin. Torve’s daughter could tell them which hulls were actually sound.

Runa ate the soup cold while her mother found the clean cloak she wore when asking favours.

“Not tonight,” Runa said.

“Some tonight. Before they hear five versions from other people.”

They went out together.

At the first house Svanhild talked about the promised timber before mentioning the grain. The woman who could release the boat listened with her arms folded. She had a roof to repair. The timber was not a luxury she could nobly surrender without consequence. Runa asked when it had to arrive and what could cover the gap. A smaller cart load already on the inland side would keep the roof work going for a few days. Gerd knew where it was. He could be asked in the morning.

The woman released the boat for a limited passage, provided the substitution came first.

Runa recorded it and read it back. Svanhild watched, saying nothing until they were outside.

“That was useful.”

“So was knowing whose door.”

Her mother nodded. Neither made the exchange larger.

The substitution could not be made by writing that it would be made. They went with the woman to the inland stack next morning, where she selected the pieces suitable for her roof. Gerd rejected two she wanted because their ends had gone soft. She accused him of keeping the best for the road. He took a knife to the wood and let her see the damage.

She accepted different pieces, then found a man to carry them who said he had promised his morning elsewhere. Svanhild asked where. He named a household whose boat Runa hoped to borrow. By the time they had walked between the two places, it seemed to Runa that the whole coast had already been lent to itself.

They settled it over a meal the woman had not intended to share. The man would carry the roof timber now and work later at the other house; a boy would take his place there for the morning at a smaller task. Runa wrote the wages separately so the boy did not disappear inside the exchange as free labour. Svanhild remembered which grandmother had to be told before anyone sent him beyond the landing.

The woman watched them making these arrangements at her table. At last she asked whether Runa had eaten. Runa said she had, which was true of bread before daylight. The woman put a bowl in front of her anyway.

Svanhild sat beside her daughter and ate with an appetite Runa had not seen since the grant argument. For a little while they could be two people tired from walking, disagreeing about whether the broth needed salt. Runa passed the bowl when her mother asked. The roof owner listened to their quarrel and said the younger woman was right, which made Svanhild laugh.

The boat was released that afternoon. Its owner came to the yard and told Torve in person. He inspected the hull, named two repairs needed before the passage, and accepted the work only after the woman agreed that a repair made now would remain her household’s benefit afterward. She thought this obvious. He said obvious things were best spoken while everybody still agreed.

Hervi came to the yard while they were still beside the borrowed boat. The household’s own steersman followed him, a younger man with a fur cap pulled so low that only the tip of his nose showed beneath it. He had agreed to sail, but not to have Hervi put a stranger in his place. Hervi said nobody had suggested that. The young man said his aunt had heard otherwise.

Svanhild looked at Runa. Runa had spoken to the aunt about experience in the skerries and could remember the sentence which had become this one in transit.

“I explained it badly,” she said.

The young man pushed up his cap. He wanted to know which of them would give the orders.

Hervi picked up a loose peg and pointed with it to the mouth of the inlet. “Inside those rocks, you will know your boat. I will know the passage. If I tell you left, you turn left by the means that keep her afloat.”

“And outside?”

“We will be rowing. You may arrange the oars without me.”

The young man smiled slightly. Runa could not tell whether the answer satisfied him or merely gave him something he could accept in front of the women. He went aboard to show Hervi the repair by the stern. The older pilot followed. Their heads bent together over the timber.

Svanhild caught Runa’s sleeve before she went after them.

“Let them find the rest.”

They waited on shore. The men spoke for a long time. Once the younger man laughed outright. Hervi climbed out first and told Runa which tide he wanted them ready for. The steersman remained aboard, testing a fastening he had already tested twice.

On their way to the next house they passed the cousins’ uncle, who had evidently been told that an ancient family grievance stood between the valley and its food. He demanded to know which grievance. Svanhild told him they were discussing work at different places and not proposing a reconciliation.

“They’re both willing?”

“Yes.”

“Then why are people coming to me?”

“Because you’re at home.”

He considered that a good answer. He invited them inside. Svanhild refused the full meal but accepted a cup, and Runa found herself seated beneath a string of dried fish while the uncle explained the history of the knife.

There had been two knives. The cousins’ fathers had bought them from the same trader. One had a split in the handle, repaired with a band of darker wood. Years later the other handle split and was repaired by the same man. The uncle claimed he could tell them apart. Neither cousin accepted his account of which was which.

Runa laughed before she could stop herself. The uncle looked at her.

“They each remember a father using it.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

She put down the cup. He had no wish to hurt her; that made the rebuke harder to discard. She thought of her mother’s sewing bag and of the little things she would be capable of defending badly.

Svanhild asked whether the uncle could lend a sound knife to the man assigned to the store. He had three. He brought them out, selected the least worn, then reconsidered and gave them the second best.

“He will use it to pry things.”

“He probably will,” Svanhild said.

They took it to the cousin before he left. He was packing clothes while his wife laid food in a cloth. Runa explained the work at the store: receipt of the grain, the sequence of loading, the message he would bring back if Hervi chose to wait. He listened closely. Only when she mentioned the far-bank crew did his face close.

“You’ve put him there.”

“You offered to go to the store.”

“I offered to serve.”

Svanhild laid the borrowed knife on the table. He looked at it, recognized it and picked it up.

“My uncle gave you this?”

“For the work. He wants it back.”

“He always does.”

His wife handed him the food. The little domestic interruption gave Runa time to stop trying to improve his temper. She named the departure hour and the person who would receive his first message. He repeated both correctly. She thanked him and got up.

At the door he asked whether his cousin had objected to going to the far bank.

“I haven’t asked him yet,” she said.

He nodded as though this were useful intelligence. His wife rolled her eyes behind him. Runa looked away quickly enough to preserve the last scraps of his dignity.

The other cousin wanted to know who would record the returned sacks. She told him. He asked whether his name would be on the receipt. She said all receiving crew would be named. He accepted, then began describing how his cousin would miscount unless watched. Runa interrupted with a question about the shelter on the far side.

He knew the shelter. One wall needed closing before men could sleep there. He could bring boards. By the end of the visit they were discussing food and the dry place for spare clothes. The knife did not return to the conversation.

Walking away, Runa felt absurdly pleased. Nothing had been healed. Two men would be at useful work far enough apart to make their own decisions, and she had three more households to visit before dark. Svanhild took the list from her to read the next name.

“This one first. She’ll be out later.”

“Where?”

“Arguing for a different boat, if we don’t catch her.”

At the next house they failed. The crewman’s wife refused to let the household’s only sound boat go until a previous loan was answered. Svanhild tried a reminder of kinship. The woman replied with a reminder of the last time kinship had left her mending alone. Runa stopped her mother from trying again.

“We’ll find another.”

Outside, Svanhild was angry.

“She would have listened.”

“She did listen.”

They walked several paces in silence. Then her mother said, “Yes.”

By the following afternoon there were enough boats in principle. In practice two needed repairs and one needed a different fastening before it could take the passage. Torve would have to supply work, timber and his own craft at the very moment the hall had tried to displace him.

He heard them out in the yard. Vigdis stood by the rack, holding a tool she had stopped using.

“I’ll do the paid repairs already agreed,” he said. “I won’t lend the yard because the hall has discovered it needs me this week.”

“The need is beyond the hall,” Runa said.

“So was my family when they told us to leave.”

Svanhild looked at the bench rather than him. Runa had brought the older account, but had not taken it out. She would not offer it as a reward for boats.

“What would you need to undertake?” she asked.

Torve named the practical terms first: payment for damage, who would direct the boats, where the timber could be taken and which hull he would refuse to risk. Runa wrote them. Then he looked at Svanhild.

“And what am I holding while I do it? A favour until the next steward changes his mind?”

Her mother raised her head.

“I will give the older account before witnesses.”

Runa’s pen stopped.

Torve did not thank her. “Which older account?”

“The grant to your grandfather and his house after him.”

“Did you forget it?”

“No.”

Vigdis lowered the tool to the bench with care.

Svanhild stood still. Runa could not tell what the admission had cost her, and distrusted the impulse to measure that before thinking of the people who had paid for the silence.

“Tomorrow,” Torve said. “Before we take the boats out.”

“Yes.”

“And the terms of the passage are witnessed separately.”

“Yes,” Runa said.

They left the yard without an agreement to supply every boat, but with a time to return. On the path Svanhild asked whether Runa thought he would help after the account.

“I don’t know.”

“He will.”

“Then why did you ask?”

Her mother walked on. Runa wished she had let the question be a request for company instead of treating it as another error to correct.

At home Svanhild went upstairs to check on Eydis. Runa sat at the table and recopied the passage terms so Torve could read them without following the crossings-out.

Chapter 26 The line restored

Svanhild had three ways to begin that would make the correction sound smaller.

She knew them while dressing. She knew them while fastening the heavy pin. By the time she reached the hall, she had made one of them almost honest: there had been several accounts, and the one given recently had not contained the fullest words. It would let listeners blame the frailty of memory. A sympathetic person would supply that explanation before she had to.

Torve stood near the door with his daughter. Neither came to greet her.

The hall was crowded enough to be uncomfortable. News of the relief had brought people who needed work assigned, boats released, food promised. News that Svanhild would speak about the yard had brought the rest. Ingrith sat at the far end, expression unreadable. Her cousin, whose household paid part of Svanhild’s winter support, had chosen a place close beside her.

Runa laid the copied account on the table. Her hands were steady. Svanhild looked at them and remembered teaching those fingers to tie a bag closed before school. She had used that memory badly already. She made herself look elsewhere.

Dagrin called for quiet.

Svanhild stood in the familiar space beside the hearth. She knew where a low voice would carry and which listener would lean forward first. Usually that knowledge was a comfort. Today she was tempted to use it to make people admire the difficulty of what she was doing.

She began plainly.

“The account I gave of Torve’s holding was incomplete. I knew an older account that granted the landing to his grandfather and those of his house who came after him.”

Someone shifted a bench. She waited for the scrape to end.

“I learned it from the old singer. I copied part of it. Runa has found the copy and other people have remembered the grant independently.”

Torve watched her without moving.

“I chose to carry the narrower account. I thought the hall needed to keep the service attached to the place. I did not tell his household what I had left out.”

There. She had said it without the three softer beginnings. Her mouth had gone dry.

A man near the door asked why she had not said it at the hearing.

“Because I continued what I had done before.”

That answer brought no relief. She could feel people waiting for a reason that would turn it into something else. She had reasons. They were still reasons. She did not trust herself to give them now.

Runa read the older wording. Another witness supplied the phrase he remembered. They did not agree on every sound. Svanhild corrected one name automatically, then stopped and asked the witness whether he meant the father or the son. It was the son. He was right.

Ingrith questioned the extent of the grant. The whole shore could not be conveyed merely by a description of a landing. Public use remained. Torve said he wanted his yard recognized, not the sea. Oddrun made a noise which suggested that they would speak about the shore later.

The jarl agreed that the hereditary claim required proper recording and boundaries, not another discretionary notice. She did not restore Svanhild’s standing by praising her courage. She asked Dagrin to arrange the witnesses for the separate relief undertaking.

Her cousin rose before that began.

“My household has no further use for this singer’s winter account.”

Svanhild felt the words physically. The payment had bought cloth, lamp oil, food not obtainable through a joke and a visit. She had expected anger. Expecting it did not make the loss abstract.

The cousin added that her place at his next gathering would be given to someone whose memory could be trusted.

Svanhild inclined her head. She would not answer him with a performance. When she sat, her knees were less reliable than she liked.

Torve came to the table for the passage terms. He offered two boats, repair work on the third, and timber within a named limit. He retained the right to refuse an unsafe load. Damage would be assessed separately; no one could claim the emergency itself discharged the debt. Runa recorded the men who witnessed the undertaking and made certain the terms did not quietly enclose the shared shore.

Svanhild listened while people she had entertained for years asked exact questions about wood and wages. Their questions gave her something to do with her attention besides watching who would no longer meet her eyes.

Before she could leave her seat, the cousin’s wife approached.

Svanhild had known her since the woman was a child. She had sung at her marriage and helped settle a dispute over a burial when her first son died. The woman stood with her gloves held together in one hand. She did not look towards her husband.

“Will you send the cloak back with our man?” she asked.

Svanhild looked down at the cloak she had hung beside her chair. It was her own, bought with payment, but the woman meant another garment: a costly travelling wrap lent for journeys made in the household’s service. It hung in Svanhild’s chest at home. She had begun to think of it as available without remembering its owner.

“Yes.”

“Not today. When you have time.”

“I can send it today.”

The woman nodded. Neither had named the meals, rides and winter invitations which would stop with it. Svanhild remembered the woman’s little son feverish on her lap and was ashamed to feel, first, the practical cold of losing the wrap.

“I heard what you said,” the woman continued.

Svanhild waited.

“I don’t know what I think of it.”

“You needn’t tell me now.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

A little of their old manner returned, then failed. The woman put on her gloves. Svanhild wanted to ask whether she would still come to the house when her husband was away. Asking would force an answer into this public place. She let her go.

Runa was speaking with Dagrin at the table, both of them bent over the separate undertaking. Her daughter had heard none of the exchange. Svanhild was glad and lonely about that in the same moment. She picked up her own cloak and fastened it with fingers which would not quite obey the pin.

Afterward Torve spoke to her near the door.

“You knew my father.”

“Yes.”

“He asked about that wording once. Do you remember?”

She did.

“I told him the account had always varied.”

Torve nodded slowly. He seemed to be fitting her answer into a memory that had remained awkward for a long time.

Then he went to his daughter.

Svanhild stood alone until Oddrun came for her. A crew needed someone to speak to the two cousins before they found a reason to meet at the same boat. Svanhild went.
