She was useful there, though neither cousin thanked her. One accused her of favouring the other. She told him he could have the colder post if he preferred, and he stopped.

The rest of the day passed in errands. She found a woman who had been omitted from a crew discussion and asked which young men would listen to her. She persuaded a house to lend blankets by naming who would return them. She sent someone back for a pot because men could not row on a promise of food.

At the blanket house she was kept waiting outside the sleeping room while the owner decided which pieces could be lent. Svanhild had stood in that doorway many times and ordinarily would have gone in to help. Today she waited to be asked.

The woman emerged with two thick coverings and one thin one. Svanhild thanked her. She had hoped for four.

“There are men who will sleep away from their own fires.”

“There are people who sleep here.”

“Yes.”

The woman lifted the thin covering. One corner had been mended with cloth of a different colour. Svanhild remembered the cloth as a child’s skirt and almost mentioned it, then stopped. She did not want to pull another dead child into service because she needed a blanket.

“Who will bring them back?”

Svanhild named the store cousin. The woman asked whether he was the one who fought about the knife. Svanhild said they both fought about it, which did not help identify either. At last she named his wife. The woman was satisfied and made him responsible for returning them dry.

Svanhild tied the bundle herself. It was broad enough to make the next few visits awkward. At one house she had to put it down in the snow while finding the message in her sleeve; at another the door was too narrow to admit her with it held across her body. A boy offered to carry it to the store. She refused because she had promised the blankets to a named hand. The boy looked offended and went away.

She was being absurd. She knew the boy’s mother; he was carrying other things there already. When she called him back he had gone too far to hear without her raising her voice, and she did not want people turning to look.

By the time she reached the store her shoulders hurt. The cousin took the bundle, counted the blankets and asked why there were only three. She could have struck him.

“Because the fourth is keeping someone warm.”

He nodded and wrote three. His acceptance left her no quarrel in which to put the anger. She stood until he had recorded the owner, then went to the next errand.

At the landing, a crewman’s mother was refusing to supply the food her son had said she would prepare. She did not refuse him passage. She refused his assumption that she had nothing to do between now and dawn except bake for six men. He stood beside the cold oven with a face so like the small boy Svanhild remembered that she nearly laughed.

“What is needed first?” Svanhild asked.

“Someone to cut fuel,” the woman said.

Her son went out before anyone could tell him. Svanhild took off her cloak and asked for the meal. She could mix while the woman prepared the other food. The woman hesitated, then brought the vessel to the table.

For an hour Svanhild had no audience. She worked the dough, felt it change beneath her hands, added too much water and repaired the mistake with a little more meal. The woman beside her chopped dried fish and complained about the price of salt. Svanhild agreed. She wanted more salt in her own house and had begun calculating how little she could use without Runa noticing.

The son returned with fuel. He stayed to carry water and was rewarded by being given another task. The oven warmed. Svanhild rolled the first piece thin, pricked it and passed it over.

“Were you there?” the woman asked.

“At the hall?”

“Yes.”

Svanhild’s hands stopped.

“I was.”

The woman watched the bread. “Heard you put the words back.”

“Yes.”

“My man liked Torve’s father. He was short with people. Always knew when a favour would take longer than they said.”

Svanhild resumed rolling. She remembered that too. She remembered laughing at it with people whose favours cost the boatwright time and timber.

The woman lifted the bread before it scorched. She set it aside and took the next piece. Neither spoke for a while.

When the first batch was ready, they broke one between them to test it. It was good. Svanhild ate her portion standing, too hungry to wait for a bowl. The crewman watched until his mother told him he could have one as well. He reached for the largest, reconsidered and took a smaller piece.

Svanhild laughed then. The woman asked why, and she said he had looked exactly like her daughter trying to choose the proper thing while wanting the other one. It was an affectionate memory she had not prepared as a defence. For a moment saying it was simply pleasant.

She left when there were enough hands for the remaining work. The woman thanked her for the dough and asked her to send another person if she saw one doing nothing. Svanhild promised to send only somebody who could distinguish meal from ash.

Outside, the light had begun to go. She had flour on the cuff of the cloak she could still call her own.

Near dusk she went home to fetch Eydis’s warmer mittens. The room upstairs was empty. The girl had left a book on the bed and put her copied pages out of sight. Svanhild opened the chest, found the mittens, and stood holding them a moment longer than necessary.

Downstairs the lamp oil jar was nearly empty. She lifted it, judged what remained, and put it back without filling the lamp.

Chapter 27 The work before water

The first test load sat too high.

Petros had calculated the weight correctly. Torve objected to where it was placed. Men stood on the temporary raft, shifting sacks that contained stones rather than grain, while the engineer watched the freeboard and the boatwright watched their feet.

“If he steps there when she’s pulling sideways, he’ll have less boat under him than he thinks,” Torve said.

Petros asked the man to stand still. He went onto the raft himself, carefully, and looked along the line of the load.

“We lower that stack.”

“And leave this clear.” Torve marked a passage with his boot.

Runa wrote neither instruction until the test was finished. She had learned how easily a page could become crowded with directions that the next movement changed.

The ferry used sound small hulls lashed and braced to carry a platform. It was not a bridge, and no one was to treat it as one. Loads would wait on shore until the boat crew called them. Animals would be excluded from the relief trips. People would ride only where the crew placed them. A line helped control the crossing, but it did not excuse a steersman from watching the current.

At the landing stage, fresh timber met older posts. Petros wanted another fastening at one joint. Torve rejected the line offered for it because it would chafe against an edge under repeated movement. Vigdis brought a different length and a piece of leather to protect it. Her father examined both and nodded without improving her suggestion.

The crews practised unloading. One man jumped ashore with a sack before the raft was steady. Torve sent him back and made him do it again. The man complained that everyone was watching.

“Yes,” Torve said.

Runa saw Svanhild turn away to hide a smile. For a moment the day felt almost ordinary.

For the second test Torve asked the men ashore to stop helping.

They let go of the platform reluctantly. One kept a hand on the rail until Vigdis knocked his wrist. The lashings creaked as the weight settled entirely on the hulls. From the bank the change looked slight. On board, one of the men widened his stance.

“Now the other side,” Torve said.

Two workers moved. The platform dipped towards them, checked, and rose a little as they stopped. Runa had to remind herself that it was meant to move. She had seen enough timber put together on land that her body kept expecting a floor.

Petros watched the lower edge of a mark he had made. He asked them to move back and do it again with the load shifted. This time the dip stopped sooner. Torve nodded, but insisted on trying the first arrangement once more so that the relief crew could feel the difference.

The man who had jumped ashore before the call said he understood. Torve answered that understanding would not hold the boat down under his feet.

“Let him try,” Petros said.

Torve looked at him. The engineer stood beside the fastening with his hands in his pockets, apparently unconcerned by the rebuke. After a moment Torve stepped back.

The man moved carefully through the clear strip they had left. He put the weight down where he had been shown, waited, and returned with his hands empty. He was almost ostentatious in his obedience. Runa expected Torve to find fault with that too. Instead he gave the man the next task without comment.

Gerd called her away to witness the borrowed line. Its owner wanted it measured before use. Another worker had already told him that the load mattered more than a few lengths of rope, and now he would accept nobody’s assurance about its condition.

Runa walked it with him. Where an old splice thickened it, he made her stop and look. He explained which wear was already present. She wrote it down. He read what she had written and objected to frayed, which made it sound unsound. They settled on a description of the strands. It took longer than the measurement.

When they had finished, he helped carry it himself. Runa asked where he wanted the receipt sent.

“To the house.”

“Which person there?”

“My wife will keep it.”

He paused, then told her that his last line had come back shorter and he had been laughed at for counting. It had taken a season’s trading to replace. He was not accusing anybody here, he said. She told him she understood. He glanced at the paper again before going down to the stage.

The trial crossing took place while she was away. She came back to find the platform on the far bank and everyone looking at a man who had sat down abruptly on a sack of stones. Nobody was hurt. He had mistaken the movement of the shore for the movement of the boat and reached for a post too early. The post had not obliged him.

“Sit until she is held,” Torve called.

“I am sitting.”

On the near bank someone laughed. The man on the sack lifted a finger without turning his head.

The return was easier. The crew took longer to begin it. Runa waited with the measured receipt fluttering in her hand, listening to instructions travel across the water. Some were repeated because they had not been heard; some because the person hearing them wanted to be certain. Petros stopped a worker from shouting another order while Torve was speaking. They waited until the raft had settled at the landing before discussing what to change.

Runa had prepared herself to find the trial impressive. It was mostly people standing still while other people made small adjustments. Her feet grew cold. She shifted the paper into her case and rubbed her hands, ashamed of her impatience only when the crew began again without showing any.

At last Torve called her down.

“You need to ride it.”

“I am not on the ferry watch.”

“You’ll be telling people where they can put things.”

Petros offered his hand as she stepped across. She took it. The first sensation was of the stage withdrawing beneath her, though she knew the platform had moved. She bent her knees too far and heard somebody suppress a laugh. Torve told her to stand normally and hold the rail only if she needed it.

She needed it.

Halfway across she looked back at the shore and understood why the other man had sat down. The fixed post seemed to swing, then steady, then swing again. Her stomach followed it. Petros pointed towards the far bank and asked her to look there instead. She did, and after a few moments could speak without feeling that her words would come up with her breakfast.

“Do you enjoy this?” she asked.

“Today? No.”

“Other days?”

“Other boats.”

Torve told them to stop talking at the landing. Runa obeyed with gratitude. She waited for the call, stepped where she was told, and climbed to the dry ground. When she turned she saw the narrow passage between the stacks from below rather than from a drawing. She went back to her list and reduced the number of waiting carriers allowed on the stage.

The cold returned after midday, hardening some of the mud and making the timber slippery. Women brought food to a sheltered place above the works. Gerd laid out the dry meal portions by watch rather than by household. Several men immediately took their ordinary places, then discovered they were eating with people they did not usually sit beside. One of the cousins was there, preparing to leave for the southern store, while the other had been assigned to the far-bank reception. The distance had made cooperation possible without reconciling them.

The first cousin asked whether his father’s knife might turn up in the grain sacks. Svanhild, passing with a kettle, told him it would be useful if it did and to bring it to her for cutting bread. A few men laughed. He let the matter fall.

Solli sat near the edge of the group. His work was ashore, helping with lashings and repairs, and he was trying not to look envious of the men who would take the boats. Vigdis arrived carrying a bundle of spare pegs and dropped it beside him.

“Count those. The broad ones separate.”

“I’ve counted them.”

“Then put the broad ones where we can find them.”

He began sorting. Runa sat on the other side of the bundle and helped. One peg was cracked along its length. Solli threw it aside, and she put it on the firewood heap before someone could take it back for an emergency.

At the end of the meal Orri turned up with his glove newly patched and a piece of smoked meat his wife had insisted he carry. He offered it round so thoroughly that he nearly had none left. Gerd broke his own share in half and returned part of it without comment.

Runa checked the watches while they ate. Names that had belonged to entries now belonged to men who chewed with their mouths open, men who asked to warm their socks, men who had slept badly because someone at home was afraid. She could not put all that into the schedule. She could give one man a later start when he admitted he had been awake since the previous morning.

The break ended reluctantly. People finished cups, stretched their legs and asked where to leave things that might be needed later. Svanhild tied a label to a bundle of spare mittens in a hand Runa knew well. Her mother checked the knot, tugged it and looked up to find Runa watching.

“They’ll lose the paper,” she said.

“Then tell them what’s in it.”

“I have.”

Runa smiled. Svanhild went on to tell someone else.

Runa then checked the replacement shifts and discovered that one rested crew included two men who had been up all night repairing a boat. They were replaced by workers intended for the afternoon haul. This left another gap farther along the route. She sent a message to Gerd and waited for his answer before pretending the gap had closed.

Tova came from the clinic to inspect the first-aid shelter. She brought dressings, a kettle and a list of things she wanted placed within reach. Runa asked whether she could spare anyone for the elevated watch.

“No.”

“For part of the day?”

“Which part will nobody be hurt?”

Runa looked at the busy stage. Tova’s answer was sharp, but exhaustion had earned some of it. The clinic sister had two patients who could not be moved and a child whose fever had risen overnight. Tova would stay at the treatment shelter nearest the chapel, between the works and the clinic. She had not accepted the old keeper’s post as well.

Runa arranged ground watchers at the channel bends and an attendant at the chapel bell. The stage could be seen from the bank. The backwater behind the reed tongue could not be seen fully from either ground post, but the works were expected to end before the dangerous wind shift. Hervi had given the expected turn with a warning that it might arrive early.

Petros asked for the bell attendant to look from the upper opening regularly. Osvin agreed. Runa put that task beside the tally work Eydis would help carry, then looked at the page until she was satisfied she had not assigned the same person to two places at once.

Before the pilots left for the southern store, their crews gathered by the boat shed. There was no grand repetition of the departure rite. The undertaking was named and witnessed. One boatman spoke of his fear of ice closing behind the return route. Another said nothing until Hervi asked him directly whether he understood the passage.

“I understand it,” he said. His voice was rough. “I don’t like it.”

“Good. Watch it closely.”

Svanhild helped pass the food aboard. She knew who would eat before rowing and who would be sick if pressed. Runa checked the destination lists. Petros inspected the last repair with Vigdis.

Runa walked down to Hervi before he pushed off.

“If the window closes—”

“We stay at the store.”

“Even if the loads here are ready.”

“Runa.”

She stopped.

He looked at her, tired and kindly impatient. “I am fond of you. I am not rowing through a closed sea to spare you a spoiled timetable.”

She nodded. He grinned and turned to his crew.

The boats moved out one after another. Runa watched until she could no longer distinguish the men. Then Torve called for someone to witness a damaged plank he would not accept as sound, and she went back to the stage.

Chapter 28 The first loads

They heard the oars before the boats appeared.

Runa stood above the lee cove in rain so fine it seemed suspended rather than falling. She had slept for three hours, perhaps four, and could not remember where she had put the bread Svanhild gave her. Beside her, Gerd kept turning his head towards the path, judging whether the waiting sledges would jam at the bend.

A boat came through the narrow opening under short strokes. Hervi stood low in the stern, pointing with one hand. Behind him the grain lay covered. A floe turned near the mouth, struck a rock and broke into pieces that moved faster than the larger ice had.

Nobody cheered. They were busy getting ready to receive the boat.

The first sacks were dry beneath the outer cloth. The next had taken water along one side. Runa had them set apart and marked for early use. The men carrying them wanted to know which destination should receive the wet grain. She refused to make the farthest household pay for the damage simply because its load would leave last. They divided the sound and damp sacks with the cooks’ advice, keeping enough near Varnvik to be opened and dried promptly.

Hervi came ashore stiffly. He had eaten, slept a little at the store, and spent the return concentrating so hard that ordinary questions seemed to reach him from far away.

“More coming?” Runa asked.

“One more group. Then we stop.”

“The full amount?”

“What we could bring without making the boats fools.”

She accepted the answer and asked for the tally. He gave her a wet strip of paper protected inside his coat. The store clerk’s hand had run at one corner. They compared the marks with the loads before any boat left again.

Before the second group came in, the first boat had to be pushed clear so another could land. The men who had just unloaded wanted to rest. Hervi let two go and kept the others, explaining why in a voice stripped of patience by the return passage.

Runa took one of the freed men’s places carrying the wet cover uphill. It was heavier than she expected. Water collected in a fold and spilled over her sleeve when Gerd lifted the other end. She made a sound he mistook for a request to stop.

“Keep going,” she said.

They spread it over a temporary rack. A cook came to examine the damp grain and told Runa how much space it would need near a fire. The room first proposed was full of waiting people. Another shed had a sound floor but no heat. They divided the wet sacks between two places and marked them for use before any of the dry grain stored deeper inside.

A man from the outer settlement objected when one damp sack was assigned to his house’s load.

“You keep the good for the hall.”

Runa showed him the distribution. The hall was taking damp sacks too. He studied the marks, then pointed out that the hall had more fires and more hands to spread the grain.

She had not counted that. They changed the assignment, leaving a smaller share of damp grain for the outer houses and sending more of the sound cargo on. The cooks near Varnvik accepted the extra work with visible reluctance.

The second group arrived while they were still rearranging the sacks. One pilot had a bleeding split over his knuckle where ice had knocked an oar against the gunwale. He wrapped it himself and asked for food. Runa tried to get the vessel’s tally from him before he sat; he held up his sound hand.

“After.”

She waited. By the time he finished the bowl, she had matched most of the load by the sack marks. He corrected two entries, took the empty bowl back to the cook and lay down beneath a spare cloth. Within moments he was asleep.

The third boat came in light, carrying the men who would help return the borrowed hulls when the weather allowed. Runa had hoped for more grain. Hervi saw the hope on her face.

“She was riding badly with the last load. We took it out.”

“How much remains at the store?”

He told her. She wrote it beside the receipt and marked it undelivered, resisting the impulse to make the note smaller.

The haul began slowly. The raised track held where it had been repaired and sucked at runners where the surface softened. Men walked beside the sledges to stop them slipping towards the lower edge. Runa carried the list and a spare pole, using the pole more often than the list for the first stretch.

At the narrow bend a runner cracked.

The load did not overturn. It settled at an angle while the men swore and held it. Runa’s first impulse was to send for another sledge. Gerd told her how long that would take. They unloaded half onto the following vehicle, wedged the damaged runner and brought the reduced load forward while someone returned with replacement timber.

The delay was tedious, cold and necessary. A man sat on a sack while waiting and fell asleep almost immediately. Another woke him angrily, then apologized when he saw his face. They shared food and waited for the repair. Runa found her bread in the inner pocket and ate it without tasting anything until she reached the piece of cheese folded inside.

The repaired sledge could move, but the following one had sunk while waiting.

Gerd sent four people to it. Runa went because her name was one of those he called. The man at the rear had already begun lifting, wasting his strength against the weight of the load. Gerd made him stop. They cleared mud from beside the runner with flat pieces of wood, slid brush into the hollow and waited while the driver turned the animal’s head towards the sound strip.

“On the pull,” Gerd said. “Not before.”

Runa crouched with her fingers under a crosspiece. Mud pressed through the seam of her boot. She could smell the animal, the wet leather, the sour wool of the man beside her. The driver clicked his tongue. The traces tightened. Gerd called, and they lifted.

For an instant she believed they had done nothing. Then the runner rose a finger’s width, dropped onto the brush and moved forward. She let go too late. The timber dragged across her palm and left a burning line. She stood nursing it against her coat while Gerd called them clear.

The man beside her was laughing without pleasure.

“First good thing this morning.”

“The boats came.”

“I wasn’t pulling those.”

She laughed too. He offered to look at her hand. There was nothing to see but reddened skin. He told her not to grip so far beneath the crosspiece next time, then went ahead to move a branch from the track.

The loads spread out after that. At the bend, one could no longer see the cove. Sounds came from behind the rise before the people making them appeared: a shouted warning, the scrape of a runner, a cough which repeated at intervals so regular Runa began waiting for it. She asked the coughing man whether he should go back. He said he had coughed all winter and could carry without coughing on the grain if that was her concern.

“It wasn’t.”

He looked away. She did not ask again. When they stopped next, she made room for him near the kettle and he took it without discussing why.

A woman from the nearest house had brought hot water wrapped in layers of cloth. By the time she reached them it was merely warm. People drank it eagerly. She had only three cups, so Runa held one while its first user swallowed, took it from him and passed it onward. The woman asked whether they had seen her brother at the cove. Runa did not know which boat he had been on. The coughing man named it, told her where it had landed and said he had helped unload.

“Did he look well?”

“He looked wet.”

“He always does.”

She smiled, but kept looking back down the track. When the next sledge appeared, she left the kettle with Runa and walked towards it.

Runa wanted to sit. There was no dry place near enough to reach before somebody would ask her to stand again. She leaned against a bank of old snow, discovered too late that water ran over its surface, and felt it soak the back of her coat. Gerd watched her move away from it.

“Not your morning.”

“It was mine before daylight.”

“You should have kept it there.”

He gave her a little of the hot water from his cup. She took it and watched two men argue over which of them had seen a bird first. The bird had gone. They agreed it had been an ugly one and resumed walking.

Beyond the bend the track passed a house with smoke coming from both roof openings. A child stood outside with a basket of small stones, dropping them one by one into a puddle. He stopped to count the sledges. At the fifth he lost his place and shouted for his mother. She came to the door wiping her hands, asked whether there was news of the southern boats and then told him to leave the road clear.

He waved to every sledge. Runa waved back. A man behind her said the child was probably counting sacks. Another said he was counting horses. The boy shouted that he was counting men who had fallen over. They asked how many. He held up both hands.

Runa was still smiling when they reached the first houses of Varnvik.

People came from houses to help shift the loads. Some asked after particular boats. A woman began crying when she saw her husband and was angry with him for making her do it in front of others.

Svanhild stood by the distribution table with Eydis. The girl had a clean tally and was checking marks against the incoming loads. Runa went to her first.

“Have you eaten?”

“Yes.”

“Warm food?”

“Grandmother made me.”

“Good.”

Eydis looked at her mother’s wet sleeves. “Have you?”

“Bread.”

Svanhild heard and handed her a bowl before she could refuse. Runa ate standing up while the next sledge came in.

Loads for the outer houses went on to the gut. The first ferry passage was uneventful. That made everyone easier. The second took longer because the receiving crew had not cleared enough space.

Runa travelled to the crossing and stayed there. Petros watched the stage. Torve directed the raft crew. Vigdis inspected the lashings after each passage, feeling rather than merely looking. Gerd kept the waiting sledges back on firmer ground.

The third crossing brought an argument over the order of destinations. A boatman had promised a household he knew that its grain would come with him. Runa’s tally placed it after a house with less food. Neither promise had been made maliciously. Both had been heard as definite.

The boatman said the household had lent blankets and deserved consideration. The receiver on the far bank shouted that everyone had lent something. Torve told them to stop arguing across a loaded raft.

Runa went down to the stage and asked the boatman which promise he had given. He repeated it, word for word, with the stubbornness of a man afraid that surrendering a small undertaking would make him unreliable in larger ones. She could not alter the order without delaying the house whose children had less to eat.

“Tell them I changed it,” she said.

“I told them I would bring it.”

“You can bring it on the next trip.”

“And if there isn’t one?”

The question remained between them. Petros was watching the current at the far post. Runa looked at the waiting load and could feel time passing through the quarrel.

“Then they will ask me why,” she said. “Put my name with the change.”

The boatman did not like it. He accepted it because it gave him a truthful account to take home. Runa wrote the changed order and sent the message ahead with the first load, rather than letting the household discover it by waiting.

She carried one end of a sack to help make up the lost time. The weight surprised her; she had handled too many of them in figures since the first cove. At the stage she waited for the crew’s call, resisting the body’s wish to set it down anywhere and straighten. The man beside her grunted. Together they passed it aboard.

When she stood upright again, the view tilted. She gripped a post until it steadied. Gerd saw and told her to drink. She said she would. He brought a cup instead of trusting the answer.

For a few minutes she sat above the path and watched other people manage. The raft crossed. Men at the far bank unloaded. A cart moved into place and was sent back because it would block the return. The work continued while she drank, and the relief she felt at that was mixed with an irritation she had no intention of explaining to anyone.

By the fourth crossing, a workable rhythm had emerged: load, hold, cross, unload, return. On each bank someone kept the next movement from crowding the present one.

A man whose own house had received its stores wanted to leave. Runa began asking him for one more shift, then learned that his wife was alone with a sick child. She let him go and changed the roster. The replacement was slower and careful about every footing. She had to make herself stop comparing him with the man who had left.

Rain thickened. Water began running down the road in narrow streams. The ground watchers reported the channel rising within the expected range. Hervi, back from his last approach, said the wind might turn sooner than he liked.

“We can finish these loads,” someone said.

Torve looked at the stack. “We can try these loads.”

Runa gave the hauling crew a rest because their hands had begun slipping on the ropes. The pause cost time. Refusing it would cost something less predictable. She watched them drink and stretch their fingers, wanting them to recover faster than bodies could.

Across the water the chapel tower stood grey against a paler sky. She could see movement in the upper opening but not who it was. She thought of Eydis warm indoors with the tally, and was glad of it.

Chapter 29 The ninth stroke

Osvin had written the destination names too close together.

Eydis discovered this when Jost arrived with two marks she could not place. He had come running from the table below and wanted to leave running. She made him wait while she compared his slip with the larger tally.

“Which house?”

“He said the farther one.”

“They’re both farther.”

“The one after the turn.”

She found the mark and copied it while he shifted from foot to foot. He had been carrying messages since morning and wanted to be useful in the way that involved being sent, not standing while someone checked whether he had heard correctly.

“Ask for the house name next time.”

“He didn’t give it.”

“Then ask.”

He made a face which she would have disliked from anyone except another child being ordered about by her. She sent him down with the corrected slip.

Osvin stood by the upper window, looking towards the gut. The bell rope hung beside him. He had rung no alarm. Ordinary hours had been suspended during the works so that no one would mistake the first strokes. He had told Eydis this twice, and she had written it on the edge of the tally because it seemed important enough not to rely on remembering.

Below, someone called for hot water.

Osvin leaned out to ask what was needed. A worker had cut his leg near the loading table. The clinic helper was on the other side of the yard and two men were trying to carry him without enough hands to keep the dressing in place.

“I’ll be back,” Osvin said. “Stay with the papers.”

Eydis nodded. He went down the stair quickly, ducking beneath the low beam. She heard his boots, then his voice below.

She checked the tally again. The next group of sacks had not been marked through. Perhaps they had not reached the stage. Perhaps the slip was late. She went to the window to look for Jost and saw him waiting beside the injured man, holding the kettle he had been given. He was no longer running.

Beyond him, the channel moved in a dark strip between the banks. People at the stage looked small. Eydis could not pick out her mother. She knew the red-brown coat near the chapel yard was Svanhild’s, but the distance and rain made everyone else into movements.

She looked towards the white rock.

At first she thought the light had changed. The lower part seemed darker than it had during practice. The crack was still visible. Beneath it, where the brown line should have been, there was water.

She leaned farther, bracing a hand on the frame.

The mark had not disappeared completely. A small dark piece remained at one end. Water touched it, withdrew, and touched it again. She watched long enough for the movement to stop seeming like a wave. Behind the nearer bank, water spread into a place that had been grey mud when Osvin last looked.

She called down.

“Osvin!”

Someone answered, but it was not him.

“The mark,” she shouted. “The water mark!”

The person below lifted a hand as if acknowledging a message they had not understood. Osvin knelt beside the injured worker. His head was bent. Eydis could see one of his hands pressing cloth against the leg.

She turned back to the window.

The second landmark was harder to find through the rain. Svanhild had made her look from different places so she would not depend on a single angle. Eydis moved to the other side of the opening. There: the slanting seam, the little notch above it. Water lay farther up the seam than it should while the outer channel appeared to be falling.

She wanted Osvin to come back.

She shouted again, louder. Below, someone looked up and then towards the stage. Eydis could not tell whether they had seen the same thing. She thought of running down, taking Osvin’s sleeve, making him look. By the time she reached him she would have to explain the marks from ground level, where neither could be seen clearly.

Eydis moved to the stair and looked down. If she ran she could reach the yard quickly. The turn beneath the low beam would slow her, and she would have to leave the window long enough for the mark to disappear from her sight. She put one foot on the first step, then drew it back.

Below, Osvin was still pressing the cloth. Tova had reached him and was saying something she could not hear. The injured man’s boot lay on its side. Jost held a bowl, waiting to be told where it belonged.

Eydis called to him by name.

He looked up. She pointed across the water and shouted for Osvin. Jost turned towards the attendant, but another adult asked him to move, and for an instant the whole little group shifted without anyone coming upstairs.

Eydis returned to the window. The surviving end of the dark mark was gone. A line of floating debris had formed behind the reeds, bending inward where she had expected it to drift out. She remembered her grandmother making her find the second point, then the same point from farther down the path. She had been impatient that day. Now she was glad enough of the repetition to feel sick.

There were people on the stage. From this height she could see the waiting loads as pale shapes and the heads of men moving between them. One figure in a lighter coat was probably Petros. She tried to find her mother again and failed. Looking for her used time the water did not pause to give.

She left the window.

The rope hung beside her.

She had practised. Osvin had shown her where to stand. The men had practised withdrawing, returning, doing it again when they went to the wrong ground. She knew the groups and the pause. It was not difficult to remember.

She went to the rope and put her hands where Osvin had placed them.

The first pull moved less than she expected. She had been afraid of being lifted and had held herself too stiffly. She let her knees bend, drew down and felt the counterweight answer. The bell sounded.

The noise startled her even though she had made it. It filled the upper room, leaving no space for the thought that she might have been wrong. She waited for the returning rope and pulled again. The second stroke followed too slowly. She corrected for the third, using the movement rather than fighting it.

One group.

Below, faces turned up. That was worse than nobody hearing. Now she had to finish what they would act on.

She made the next three quicker. Her palms burned. The rope was rough in a place she had not noticed during practice. She shifted one hand and almost lost the return. Her shoulder struck the frame. She kept her feet.

Six.

She looked towards the stair. No one had come.

The seventh sounded. The eighth.

For a moment she heard the space after it as if everything else had gone away. Her grandmother counting at the boat. The young man staring at the horn. Hands held out to witnesses. The ordinary morning bell with Astri sweeping beneath it.

She wanted someone to tell her to do it.

The rope came back. She pulled.

The ninth struck so loudly that she felt her teeth meet.

She held the rope against the returning movement, trying to make the pause clear. Tears had come without her noticing. She blinked, angry with them because she needed to see. People below were moving now. A man ran towards the channel, waving both arms.

She began the repeat.

At the second stroke Osvin reached the top of the stair. He came beside her and took the rope above her hands.

“Keep the rhythm,” he said.

She did. He supplied the strength she had begun to lose. Together they completed the groups. He did not ask why until the pause after the last one.

“The mark,” she said. “And the seam. The water is behind.”

He went to the window. His face changed. He returned to the rope and rang again while she stood with her hands open, unable to decide where to put them.

“Go down,” he told her. “Tell them to keep the upper path clear.”

She took the stair carefully. Her knees felt strange, as if the floor were moving beneath her. At the bottom Jost stood holding a bloody cloth. He looked at her with his mouth open.

“You rang it.”

“The path,” she said. “Osvin says keep it clear.”

Jost ran to tell someone. Eydis stepped outside.

Rain struck her face. The yard had become a place of hurried orders. Men moved benches back from the path. Tova came from the treatment shelter and asked where the injured worker had been taken. Someone said inside. Eydis wanted to ask where her mother was, but Tova had already gone.

Svanhild came towards her from the lower path.

Eydis stood still. She had expected her grandmother to shout, or embrace her, or look past her towards the stage. Instead Svanhild took both her hands and turned them palm upward.

There were red ridges across the skin. One place near her thumb had broken.

Her grandmother held them carefully. Her own hands were cold and strong. She looked from one palm to the other, then closed her fingers round them.

Eydis began to say she had seen the mark. The words would not come evenly.

Svanhild kept holding her hands.

Chapter 30 Clear of the stage

At the first group Runa looked towards the tower. At the second she turned to the waiting loads. By the third, Torve was shouting for everyone to stop.

A sack was already passing from stage to raft. The man carrying it tried to complete the movement. The raft shifted, leaving him with one foot where the boat had been a moment earlier. Vigdis caught his sleeve and hauled him back hard enough to split the cloth. The sack fell between them and struck the edge of the stage.

“Leave it,” Torve said.

The man reached for it anyway. Runa caught his arm.

“Up the path.”

He looked at her as if she had asked him to abandon a person. Then the repeated signal began and he went.

The channel watcher was running along the bank, pointing behind the works. Water came through the reeds in a broad, dark sheet. Runa saw it reach the base of a stack they had believed safely above the channel. A loose piece of wood turned once and moved towards the stage from the wrong direction.

Petros was already at the higher end. He ordered the waiting sledges back. Gerd shouted the order along the line, naming men when the general call did not move them. The path filled too quickly. Runa stopped two people from trying to drag the same sledge round opposite sides of a post.

“One at a time. You first. Leave that load.”

The nearest crew obeyed. The next could not hear. She climbed onto a low stone and repeated it, using names she knew from the tally. This was a use for the names she had not imagined while copying them in warmth.

On the raft Torve and two men kept the loaded platform from striking the failing edge. The control line tightened. One fastening began to sing under the strain. Vigdis reached for it and her father shouted her back.

“Knife,” he said to the man beside him.

The man hesitated. Cutting it would release the raft into a current they had meant to control. Keeping it could turn the loaded platform sideways against the stage.

Torve pointed to the higher landing position. Petros saw and went towards it, splashing through water already above his boots.

“Here!” the engineer called. “Bring her nose here.”

The line was cut. The raft moved suddenly, too far for Runa’s liking. Men aboard used poles to check the swing. One pole jammed, bent and snapped. The broken end struck the man holding it in the face. He dropped to one knee, then crawled back to his place when Torve told him to keep low.

The platform cleared the worst position. It carried the grain and all three men, still aboard as they swung clear of the stage. Petros and Gerd took the new line at the higher point. For several seconds nothing seemed to happen except men leaning against forces larger than their bodies.

Then the raft turned enough.

Runa sent two more workers to the line and cleared the path behind them. Her skirt was soaked to the knees. She had lost the folder somewhere. She could not remember putting it down and could not spare attention to search.

The withdrawal was orderly only in pieces.

Above Runa, the waiting sledge had turned enough to block the men coming from the far end of the stage. Its driver was trying to save the harness from the rising water. The animal stood trembling, unwilling to move towards the crowded path. Someone struck its flank with an open hand. It flinched sideways and made the obstruction worse.

Runa climbed back to the higher edge and caught the driver’s sleeve.

“Let the others through. Then back him.”

“He won’t back with them behind him.”

She looked. The man was right. She sent the nearest people above the sledge, where a strip of rough ground gave room for single passage, and asked the driver to hold the animal still rather than make it move. Gerd took the bridle and spoke to it in a low, monotonous voice unlike his shouted orders to men.

One worker tried to use the opened space to fetch his tool bag. Runa called his name sharply. He stopped, furious, and held up empty hands as though to show what obedience cost him. She could not replace the tools from where she stood. She pointed to the safe ground. He went, swearing.

At the water the line crew had moved. A man slipping on the bank lost his grip and slid until another caught his collar. The rope ran a short distance across wet earth. Petros called for slack to be controlled, not snatched. Torve’s shout from the raft came almost at the same time. For a moment Runa could not tell whether the two orders agreed. Then she saw the higher line take the strain and understood enough to keep people out of its sweep.

She grabbed a boy carrying a basket of pegs and pulled him behind her. It was Solli, looking older with his face grey from fatigue. He had been sent to the upper fastening and was trying to reach it by the shortest way.

“Round,” she said.

He nodded and went uphill. She watched until he was clear, then turned back in time to see the lower joint move.

A timber broke at the lower stage.

The sound was sharp, almost small. The movement after it was not. One corner dropped. Water came over the planks, taking loose sacks with it. A man still trying to retrieve a tool jumped back and fell. Another, wedged between the moving brace and a post, cried out once.

Gerd released his place on the line to another worker and went down towards him. Runa followed until he told her to stay above and bring help. She obeyed, though every part of her wanted to continue doing something with her own hands.

“Two here,” she called. “With a pole. Not that one, a sound one.”

They levered the brace. The trapped man came free with his hand held against his chest. Blood ran between his fingers. Runa recognized Orri, who had asked her to record that the failed alignment was not the workers’ fault. She called his name, trying to keep him looking at her.

“Orri. Up here. Tova is at the chapel.”

He said he could walk. He took two steps and nearly fell. They put an arm beneath each shoulder. Runa went ahead to clear the path.

At the steep part of the path Orri stopped helping them.

His legs had been moving, not well but under his own command. Then his knees folded and both men carrying him lurched forward. Runa caught one by the coat before he fell. Orri made a choking sound and tried to straighten without putting his injured hand out.

“Down,” she said. “Put him down.”

They lowered him on the firm edge of the path. People coming behind crowded close until she sent them round. One man wanted to lift him beneath the arms again. The other said they would tear the shoulder from him. Orri said nothing. His lips had gone pale under the dirt.

Runa knelt where he could see her.

“The shelter is up there.”

“I know where it is.”

“Can you stand?”

He tried to draw his feet underneath him. The effort made him close his eyes. She put a hand against his good shoulder, keeping him seated.

“Wait.”

Someone brought the cover from a load. It was wet along one edge but sound. They folded it twice and put it beneath him while he swore at the movement. Two men took each end. Runa expected to carry, but Gerd had sent a replacement ahead of her, a broad woman who seized the nearer corner and told her to clear the way.

They carried him with short steps. The improvised sling sagged enough that his heels nearly caught the ground. At the next turn they stopped and changed their grip. Orri asked them to let him walk. Nobody answered because they were busy getting enough cloth into their hands.

Runa went ahead to the shelter. The doorway was blocked by a basket and a bench placed for waiting patients. She moved the basket inside and dragged the bench against the wall. Tova met them with her sleeves already tied back. She did not ask what had happened until Orri was lying where she wanted him.

“Which hand?”

He raised it a little, then stopped.

“Keep it there.”

She looked towards the doorway. “Who saw the timber?”

Runa began answering, then realized she had seen only the release. Gerd came up behind her. He described where the brace had held the hand. Tova listened, asked one more question about the direction it had moved, and sent him for hot water.

Orri had begun saying something repeatedly. Runa bent to hear.

“My wife.”

“I’ll send.”

“Not the boy.”

She understood after a moment. He did not want the child sent running into the shelter to see him. She found an older woman among the people outside and gave her the message quietly. The woman asked which house. Runa pointed, but the woman already knew it once she heard his name.

“Tell her to come by the upper path,” Runa said.

“I know the path.”

Runa stepped aside. Her hands were sticky. She looked down and found blood on the cuff she had put against his shoulder, though the hurt hand had seemed nowhere near it. She wiped the cuff on her skirt before noticing she was only spreading it.

Behind her the raft reached the higher landing. Torve got the people off before the grain. The load shifted as the last man stepped away, and two sacks went into the water. No one tried to follow them. Vigdis stood on firm ground with both hands pressed against her mouth, then dropped them and began helping unload.

Runa wanted a count. People were moving too quickly for one. She began asking each crew who was missing, then stopped a man from saying everyone was there merely because he could see his brother. They named the crew. They named the people last seen at the lower stage. Gerd went back along the safe edge with another worker to look.

As Runa turned back towards the shelter, someone told her Eydis had rung.

Runa turned so fast that the speaker stepped back.

“Where is she?”

“At the chapel. Your mother has her.”

“Is she hurt?”

“I don’t know. She was standing.”

Runa started up the path.

At the shelter door a man asked her to hold Orri’s arm while Tova cut away the sleeve. She stopped. His hand had begun to swell. He was shivering so violently that the injured fingers knocked against his chest despite his efforts to keep them still.

She sat beside him and held the arm where Tova showed her.

“Eydis is with Svanhild,” Tova said, without looking up.

“Did you see her?”

“Yes.”

“Her hands?”

“Rope burn. I’ll look when I can.”

Runa nodded. She kept holding Orri while he groaned and apologized for groaning. Tova told him to stop apologizing. The clinic sister arrived with a splint and hot water.

Outside, the bell had fallen silent. Men were moving the saved grain under cover. Runa could hear Torve arguing with someone who wanted another crossing before the water fell. The argument ended with a refusal so loud that nobody needed it repeated.

At last another woman took Runa’s place beside the injured man. She stood slowly because her legs had stiffened. Tova caught her sleeve before she left.

“Drink something. Then go.”

Runa drank water from a cup she did not remember being handed. She put it down and went to find her daughter.

Chapter 31 The house beyond the turn

She found Eydis asleep against Svanhild’s side, still wearing her damp boots.

The sight stopped her in the doorway. Her mother lifted a hand, warning her to be quiet. Eydis’s palms were loosely wrapped. Her face was blotched from crying, and her mouth had fallen open in the careless way it did when she slept deeply.

Runa knelt beside her. The girl stirred when she touched her hair.

“Mother?”

“I’m here.”

“Did they get off?”

“Yes.”

“All of them?”

“The people. Some were hurt.”

Eydis tried to sit upright. Runa helped her and then held her too hard. The girl made a little sound against her shoulder.

“Sorry.” Runa loosened her arms, but did not let go.

Svanhild moved aside to give them room. Runa felt her mother’s hand briefly on her back, then it was gone.

There was still work beyond the turn.

The saved stores on the far bank had to reach the two outer communities before darkness made the path worse. The crossing would remain closed until the water fell and the stage could be inspected. Runa had no business going back onto it. She did need to confirm the loads already across, send a clear account ahead, and make sure the people who had been working longest were fed before being asked to haul again.

She left Eydis with Svanhild after the girl insisted she could sleep. Leaving was difficult enough that she became irritable about finding her gloves. Svanhild found them on the bench and handed them over without comment.

By late afternoon the distribution beyond the gut had become a series of slow journeys between houses. Runa went to the upper footbridge, which had been inspected and cleared for people without loads. She took only her dry copy of the tally. The wet original had been found under a sledge, legible in places and useless in others.

The upper footbridge was narrower than she remembered from the survey. She had crossed it then with daylight behind her and someone talking about the spring repairs. Now the rail was wet and the water below crowded with broken things she could not identify.

A worker stood at the near end to admit people one at a time. He asked what she carried. She showed him the tally inside her coat. He nodded and made her wait for the man coming towards them to step off.

The man carried nothing. Halfway across he stopped, gripping both rails. Runa waited. The worker called to him, not loudly, and the man took another step. When he reached them he stood with his back to the bridge and breathed through his open mouth.

“Are you hurt?” Runa asked.

“No.”

The worker let him catch his breath before asking for room. Runa stepped onto the first board. It was sound beneath her foot. So was the next. She kept her eyes on the far end, but the moving water intruded at the edges of her sight. A loose branch struck something underneath and turned. She stopped before she meant to.

The worker did not call at once. She was grateful for the silence. She took the next step, then another, and reached the far side with her mouth dry.

Petros had marked the upper path where the edge had softened. A man waited there to guide the next pair of haulers back. He told Runa which house had the grain and which had only received the notice. The promised destinations had become confused when people saw the first sacks coming up the bank and assumed the rest would follow by the same route.

She walked with him to the junction. Meltwater ran down the middle of the path. They kept to the higher side, sometimes single file, sometimes with room to walk beside one another. He asked whether the crossing would be repaired by morning. She said it had not yet been examined safely. He kicked a lump of slush off the path.

At the junction two women were waiting with an empty handcart. They had heard that everything was ashore. Runa explained which bank, which loads, which route could still be used. One woman began to cry, quietly and angrily, turning her face away from the others.

“We brought the cart,” the other said.

“I can see.”

“My father got it out for us. He shouldn’t have been lifting.”

Runa looked at the empty bed. The wheel rims had been packed with mud during the climb. They had tied a cloth inside to keep the sacks clean. She asked which house they belonged to, compared it with the surviving tally, and found that part of their grain was already on this side. It had gone to the receiving house with the other loads.

They could use the cart after all. The crying woman wiped her face impatiently. She did not thank Runa. Together they turned the handles and went down towards the stores.

Runa followed more slowly. She wanted every question answered before anyone asked it, wanted dry grain standing inside every door. The path made her attend to each step. By the time she reached the house she had stopped rehearsing what to say.

At the first outer house, people had been waiting beside a cold hearth because the person who usually brought fuel was still helping at the store. Runa sent a boy to fetch dry sticks from the covered stack and was told he needed permission. She went to the owner, obtained it, and came back feeling the absurd effort required to make a pot boil.

Once the fire caught, the room changed. Wet clothes steamed. Someone cut bread. A woman opened the first sack of grain and put aside the damp portion to spread near the warmth. The waiting children became noisy, which was an improvement on their silence.

Petros arrived carrying a damaged measure. He had spent the last hour checking the upper path. He sat on a bench and bent forward with his elbows on his knees. The host put food in his hand because he seemed unlikely to ask for it.

The child beside the hearth could not eat until someone found a smaller spoon. Her father tried to feed her with his own, became impatient when she turned her head, and said she had been complaining of hunger all afternoon. The mother took the bowl from him and told him to eat his own food while it was hot.

Runa found a little wooden spoon in the bottom of the clean basket. It had a chipped edge. She smoothed the rough part with the back of a knife, washed it and brought it over. The child accepted it without looking at her and began eating with intense concentration.

Petros watched from the bench. He had put his bowl down unfinished. His hands were shaking now that he was no longer carrying anything. Runa sat beside him and asked whether he was cold.

“Not more than anyone.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

He smiled faintly. She brought another cloth and put it round his shoulders, leaving him free to reject it. He did not.

“I should have had someone permanently at the upper view,” he said.

“We had Osvin checking.”

“He had other work.”

“So did everyone.”

Petros looked at her. She could hear herself preparing reasons before he had finished naming the failure.

“We’ll record it,” she said.

“Yes.”

Neither said the record would mend Orri’s hand.

After a while he picked up the bowl again. Runa sat until he had eaten most of it, then went to help carry water. The host gave her an ordinary bucket rather than the heavy one used by the men. She took it without proving that she could manage more.

A frightened man said the ninth had broken the timber.

Another answered that water had broken it, and that he had seen the joint move before the bell.

The first man asked whether the water had heard the bell. The second told him to eat. Runa kept measuring portions. She had neither the patience nor the knowledge to settle what they meant by the question.

At the farther house a container had cracked, leaking meal into the sledge. They salvaged what could be cleanly gathered and refused to mix the dirty sweepings with it. This meant less for the household than the tally had promised. Runa recorded the loss in front of them. The woman receiving the grain watched the pen as though it might produce the missing food if held firmly enough.

“Will it be replaced?”

“When we can bring more.”

“That is not a day.”

“No.”

Runa stayed to help move the stores inside. She was tired of having only accurate answers.

The last sack was too heavy for the shelf the household had cleared. Its owner noticed before they lifted it, stopped them and fetched two low blocks. They set the sack across those with space beneath it for the air. Runa helped pull the cloth straight. Her back hurt when she bent and hurt differently when she stood.

The woman asked whether she was going back tonight.

“My daughter is there.”

“There are beds here.”

“Thank you.”

She did not add another reason. The woman gave her a cup and asked her to sit until the others were ready. There was no advantage in going into the dark alone.

Children had begun arguing in the next room. One wanted the place nearest the hearth; another said it had been promised to him before the grain came. Their mother called that she had promised him warmth, not the whole hearth. An older girl came out carrying a blanket with a torn edge and asked for a needle.

“Ask your father.”

“He’s asleep.”

“Then wake him.”

“He’ll say ask you.”

Runa found her little sewing packet in the inner pocket. The girl thanked her and sat on the floor, arranging the tear across her knees. She made broad, practical stitches. Runa watched without offering to improve them. The blanket would hold. It was not going anywhere grand.

From outside came the scrape of the cart being dragged under cover. The house owner and Petros came in together. The engineer’s boots left dark marks on the floor. He noticed and looked for something to wipe them on; the woman told him that if he wanted a dry house he could mend the roof while he was there.

“Tomorrow,” he said.

“Then eat tonight.”

He sat. Runa had thought there could be no food left after everyone they had fed, but another bowl appeared, thinner broth with bits of fish in it. Petros ate. The girl on the floor asked whether he built bridges everywhere.

“Some places.”

“Have any fallen down before?”

The room went quiet. The girl’s needle stopped.

“Yes,” he said.

“While you were there?”

“Once.”

Her mother began to speak, but Petros shook his head slightly. The girl drew the thread through the blanket.

“Did you build it again?”

“Another one. Farther up.”

“Why didn’t you build it there first?”

“We thought the lower place would serve.”

She looked at him, waiting. He did not add anything. After a moment she bent over the tear again. The adults resumed their quiet conversation with the care people take not to appear to be resuming it.

Runa drank the rest of her cup. She had heard the same questions at meetings, sharpened for use against someone. The girl seemed occupied by the physical bridge, the first and the second place. She asked whether the next one had lasted. Petros said it was still there when he left.

“That is good,” she said.

He put down his spoon for a moment.

The workers were ready at last. They had found oil for the lantern and a dry strip to bind one man’s heel. The house owner came to the door to tell them where the path had washed out. He named a leaning tree and the stone beyond it. Petros repeated both. Runa buttoned her coat, checked the tally, and remembered the needle only when the girl came running to return it.

“Keep it until the blanket is done.”

“I am done.”

The seam lay puckered but closed across her lap. Runa took the needle. The girl had wiped it carefully on a clean corner before giving it back.

The return walk took longer in darkness. She travelled with two workers, one carrying the lantern and the other talking continually to keep himself awake. He told them about a pig he had once tried to lead across a bridge. By the third telling the pig had acquired better judgment than any person involved. Runa laughed at the same place each time because she was too tired to remember it until it arrived.

At home Eydis was awake, eating slowly. Svanhild had made a place for Runa and left the pot near enough to reach. They ate together. The girl wanted to know whether Orri would keep his hand. Runa said the sister was treating him and they would know more later.

“That’s what everyone says.”

“It is what we know tonight.”

Eydis pushed a piece of bread round her bowl.

“I thought I was wrong at first.”

Runa put down her spoon.

“I wanted Osvin.”

“Of course.”

“I wanted him to do it.”

Runa nearly said that she had done it anyway, which was true and not what the girl had offered. She waited.

Eydis looked at the bandages on her palms. “I don’t want to show anyone again.”

“You don’t have to.”

“Grandmother said they would ask.”

“They can ask.”

Svanhild stood and took her bowl to the washing shelf. After a moment she said she was going to check whether Oddrun had enough dry blankets for the men staying with her. Runa nodded and drew Eydis’s bowl nearer the hearth.

Eydis leaned against her while they sat by the fire. Runa could feel the girl’s breathing become slower. She stayed in the chair long after her own back began to ache.

Chapter 32 The brave girl

By morning Eydis had never hesitated.

She heard this from a woman bringing clean cloth to the clinic. The woman had not been in the tower and had not seen the water. She knew, however, that Eydis had rung straight through while grown men stood uncertain below. She told it in front of Eydis as if the girl would be pleased to recognize herself.

Eydis said Osvin had helped repeat it.

“Yes, after you began.”

“And Jost took the message.”

“A good little runner.”

Jost, standing nearby, looked at his boots.

Eydis wanted to say he was not little. She had called him little herself the day before in her head, when he failed to bring the house name. Now the description sounded dismissive in someone else’s mouth.

The woman asked to see her hands. Eydis put them behind her back.

“They’re wrapped.”

“Poor brave thing.”

Her mother intervened then, saying Tova did not want the dressings disturbed. This was only partly true. Tova had wanted them kept clean. Eydis was grateful and annoyed that protection required another version of events.

People kept asking. Could she show where she stood? Had she counted aloud? Had she been afraid of the ninth? The last question was usually asked by someone who already had an answer ready. Some wanted her to say no, so that the bell had proved harmless. Others wanted her fear, kept intact for a telling in which she knowingly faced whatever might follow.

She had been afraid of being wrong. Of her mother still on the stage. Of the rope taking her hands upward. Of Svanhild hearing the ninth. She could not fit all of them into an answer before the next person began speaking.

At midday she snapped at Jost for watching her.

“I’m not,” he said.

“You are.”

“I wanted the tally board.”

It lay beside her. She handed it over. He took it and went without another word. Eydis felt miserable, then angry at him for leaving her with the misery instead of allowing a quick apology.

Runa found her sitting on the stair.

“You don’t have to stay here all day.”

“I wanted to help.”

“You have helped.”

Eydis looked away.

Her mother sat below her, leaving enough room not to crowd. “What is it?”

“I wasn’t like they say.”

“You were frightened. People can be frightened and still—”

“I know.”

Runa stopped. Eydis could see her trying again.

“What do they say that you don’t want?”

“That I knew. That I just knew and did it.”

“Tell me.”

So she told her, badly at first, with the tally mistake and Osvin going downstairs mixed into the water mark. Runa listened. When Eydis said she had waited after eight, her mother’s face moved in a way the girl could not interpret. She was glad Runa did not name it for her.

Later Svanhild asked what she had seen from the window. Eydis expected to be asked about the count again. Instead her grandmother wanted the shape of the water behind the bank, whether the second mark had vanished entirely, what the people on the ground were doing.

Eydis showed her with objects on the table. A cup became the rock, a folded cloth the reed bank. She moved a spoon to represent Osvin below. Then she stopped and said, “I hoped he would come back before I had to.”

Svanhild left the spoon where she had put it.

“I heard you call.”

“Why didn’t you come?”

“I was at the lower path. By the time I understood, you had begun.”

“Did you want me to stop?”

Her grandmother took a long time before answering.

“I wanted you downstairs with me.”

Eydis left the cup and spoon where they were.

In the afternoon Eydis went outside alone.

She told her mother she was going to look at the road, which was true in the least useful sense. She wanted to walk somewhere without a person approaching her hands before speaking to her face. At the corner she found two children playing at the bell with a stick and an upturned pot.

One was her. She knew it from the other child’s cry of Eydis, quick. The girl with the stick struck the pot nine times so fast that the boy complained he had not yet got his boat into danger. They began again.

Eydis stood behind the wall and watched. The pretend water was a strip of dirty snow. Three pebbles were men; a broken cup was the raft. The boy moved it, called the warning and knocked it over before the ninth stroke. He objected to his own disaster and reset the cup.

She ought to have found it funny. Part of her did. Another part wanted to seize the stick and make them stop hitting the wrong rhythm. She took a step forward, then saw that the girl had wrapped cloth round both her hands. The cloth was clean and white. The girl’s face was fierce with pleasure.

Eydis went the other way.

At the net shed she found Oddrun sorting weights. The woman asked her to hold a cord while she tied a bundle. Eydis showed her palms. Oddrun said she could hold it between two fingers if it did not hurt, and if it did she should put it down. There was no praise in the request. Eydis held it.

They made four bundles. Each had to be lifted a little to settle the stones, and Eydis used her forearms rather than her hands. Oddrun watched the first time, then let her discover the easiest way. Outside, somebody called the woman’s name. She answered without leaving her work.

“Has everyone asked you?” Eydis said.

“About what?”

“Me.”

“Some have.”

“What did you say?”

“That I was at the upper store. I heard the signal.”

“And?”

“That I left the store.”

Eydis turned the next weight in her fingers. It had a smooth groove worn by the cord. She wanted more from Oddrun and was glad not to be given it.

“I didn’t want the ninth to sound different.”

Oddrun tightened the knot. “Did it?”

“I don’t know.”

The woman put the bundle aside. “The last one I heard sounded different because I was listening for it.”

“Which one?”

“At my brother’s departure.”

Eydis knew better than to ask whether he had returned. She asked when it had been instead. Oddrun named a year in which Runa had still lived north.

“He took my best knife,” she added. “Brought back something with a bone handle and thought I should be pleased.”

Eydis laughed, relieved before she had time to notice it. Oddrun pushed the next cord towards her.

By the time they finished, the children had abandoned the game outside. The pot lay upside down in the mud. Eydis picked it up on her way past and set it beside the door. There was no reason to leave somebody’s pot where a cart could crush it.

She had walked farther than she intended. Her palms throbbed when she let her arms hang, so she carried them against her chest on the way home. She passed the chapel without looking up at the opening. Then she turned back and looked deliberately. It was a window, small from below. Someone had closed the shutter against the wind.

Inside, her mother was speaking with the sister. Eydis almost went upstairs without interrupting. Runa saw her and moved a stool with her foot.

“Sit. She wants to change those.”

The sister unwound the outer cloth gently. One part had stuck. Eydis bit her lip while it was soaked free and hated the woman for noticing.

“You can make a noise,” the sister said.

“I know.”

She did not make one. The sister cleaned the rubbed places, laid on the fresh dressing and asked what she had been carrying. Eydis said stones. Runa closed her eyes.

“Small stones.”

“How small?”

“Net weights.”

The sister asked how she had held them. Eydis showed her. After a moment the woman nodded and said she would rather the girl left the next bundle to someone else until the skin had closed.

Runa began to speak. The sister continued winding the cloth, and whatever her mother had intended remained unsaid. Eydis looked at her. Runa gave a small, rueful smile, then held the end of the bandage when asked.

That evening Jost came to return the board. Eydis met him at the door and apologized for snapping. He shrugged, then said he had carried a message all the way to the wrong house because the adults kept saying the farther one to him too.

She laughed. He grinned, relieved, and they spent several minutes naming all the places adults believed could be identified by pointing vaguely towards them.

Svanhild told them to come away from the open door before they froze the room. They obeyed and continued the discussion beside the fire.

Part Five The people who remain

Chapter 33 Timber and fingers

Orri wanted to know whether he could hold a shovel.

The sister asked him to wait until the swelling had fallen. He waited through the examination, then asked again. Runa sat beside the bed with the relief undertaking on her knees. Orri asked her how many days it covered.

His wife, Leifa, had brought clean clothes. She folded the old ones while the sister spoke, avoiding the bloodstained sleeve until the end. Orri watched her fold it inward.

“The fingers may not close as they did,” the sister said. “I cannot promise you that they will.”

“All of them?”

“I don’t know yet.”

He looked at Runa as if her papers might contain a more definite answer.

She read the provision for injury. It covered immediate treatment and wages during a defined period of recovery. It did not promise support for permanent loss of work. She had helped write it quickly before the passage. Now the limit sat between them with her handwriting beneath it.

“That gets us to spring,” his wife said.

“Yes.”

“And then?”

“We need another arrangement.”

Orri turned his face towards the wall. His wife continued folding. Runa wanted to tell them she would not forget. She had made enough assurances to know how little they fed anyone.

Instead she asked what work he could do without the full grip. The question came too soon. Orri said he was not dead. She apologized, closed the paper and stayed while his wife asked about collecting the wages already due. That much Runa could act on today.

Runa returned the next day with the wages and a witness to the payment. Orri disliked the witness until the woman explained that she was there to confirm receipt, not inspect what he did with the money. His wife took the packet and counted it in front of them. Her lips moved as she separated the coins into small groups.

“That is right,” she said.

Orri asked to see the sheet. Runa held it where he could read without moving the splint. He followed the days worked, then the days covered after injury. At the end he wanted to sign, discovered he could not hold the pen properly with his other hand, and pushed the paper away.

“A mark will do,” Runa said.

“I know what will do.”

She set the pen down. His wife continued arranging the coins, giving him time. At last he took the pen awkwardly and made a short, furious stroke which looked nothing like his ordinary mark. The witness confirmed it. Runa left the copy on the shelf within his reach.

As she put away the ink, the little boy who had been playing near the door came to look at the splint. He asked his father whether the fingers were still inside it. Orri said yes. The boy asked whether they were asleep.

“I wish they were.”

His wife glanced at him, warning and tenderness mixed in the look. He shifted on the bed and asked the boy to show him the wooden boat he had been making. One side was higher than the other. Orri began to explain how to correct it, then stopped when the child offered him the tool.

“You do it,” he said.

The boy tried. The wood slipped. Orri told him to brace it against the bench. This time the cut held. Runa waited by the door until the boy had seen the small success for himself.

Outside, Leifa followed her a few steps.

“He is not always angry.”

“He can be angry with me.”

“That is easy to say when you go home.”

The woman rubbed her hands together, cold though the day was milder.

“I need him paid while he learns whatever comes next. Not only while he lies there. There’ll be weeks when he can do something and not enough to earn what we need.”

“I’ll put that to the association.”

“Put me in the room when you do.”

“Yes.”

“And don’t tell them he is fortunate to have work for one hand.”

“I won’t.”

The woman returned indoors. Runa stood a moment with the empty payment wrapper, then folded it into her case.

Runa came back after the midday meal to tell Leifa when the association would hear her. She found Orri alone with his son.

Leifa had gone to finish work promised before the accident. A neighbour was meant to come shortly. Orri said he could manage until then. He stood by the hearth with a cup held against his body, trying to pour from a jug without setting either vessel where the child could knock it over.

Runa took the jug. He let her, then looked so furious that she could not tell whether the fury was for her or the cup. She put both on the table.

“There.”

“I could have done it.”

“I know.”

He looked at her. She had said it too quickly. She wrote the meeting time on a scrap and asked where he wanted it. He pointed to a shelf beside the bed. When she reached to put it there, she saw that the payment copy had been unfolded and folded until its creases were soft.

The boy wanted his father to mend a strap on the wooden boat. Orri told him to wait. He waited for as long as a small child could bear, then asked again. Runa crouched to look at the boat. The strap was decorative; it did not hold anything together. She almost said so.

“It comes off when I carry it,” the boy explained.

“Show me.”

He lifted the toy by the strap. One end sprang loose. She could see the small peg that had failed to catch. She asked whether Orri wanted her to set it. He said no, then called the boy to bring it over.

He trapped the hull between his knees. With his sound hand he could place the peg but not hold the little strap beneath it. The child tried to help and covered the hole with a finger. Orri raised his voice. The boy went still.

“Move that. No, that finger.”

The child pulled both hands away. The strap slipped. Orri shut his eyes.

Runa sat without offering. She wanted badly to complete the little repair and leave them with one useful thing. The boy looked at her, asking without words. Before she moved, Orri opened his eyes and told him to put the boat on the bench.

“I’ll show you where to press.”

This time he used the edge of the bench to hold the hull. He made the boy practise without the peg, then put the peg through himself. The strap stayed. The child lifted the toy by it with such sudden confidence that both adults reached towards him. Nothing broke.

He ran to the door to show the neighbour who had just arrived. She admired the repair, asked where the clean water was, and set about making the room easier to live in. A jug went where Orri could reach it sitting. The cups went beside it. He watched her rearrange his house and said nothing until she moved a tool from the windowsill.

“Leave that.”

“It’ll fall.”

“Leave it.”

She put it back. Runa stood to go. The neighbour asked whether she could fetch more fuel from outside before leaving, and the request was such a relief that Runa carried two loads, though one would have been enough.

Orri’s son came out with her on the second journey. He had begun dragging the boat through the mud, making a wake with its bow. He asked whether his father’s hand would grow straight again. Runa said she did not know.

“The sister knows.”

“She is helping it heal.”

“Then she’ll know.”

Runa rested the logs against her hip. The boy steered his boat round her boots. He was already occupied by a stone blocking the little channel he had chosen. She waited until he moved clear before stepping over it.

Inside, Orri thanked her for the fuel in a voice so formal it hurt. She said she would see him at the agreed meeting and did not ask whether he wanted anything else. The neighbour had pulled a chair close to his, and they were arguing about a repair made to that chair years before. Runa shut the door on the familiar irritation of people who knew each other well.
