At the yard, Torve had laid salvaged timber in separate piles. Sound pieces, damaged pieces worth cutting down, pieces fit only for burning. Water had left grit in joints that had been clean before the passage. The smell of wet wood filled the shed.

Runa recognized the broken brace. She had seen it move but not the grain opening along its length. Torve showed her where it had failed. He did not use the failure to excuse every choice. The stage had been intended for controlled conditions. The water had reached it from another direction, and the warning had arrived before the worst movement, but not before all harm.

Vigdis wanted to build smaller cargo frames from the shortened sound pieces. They could be lifted by two people and set beneath sacks to keep them off wet ground. Her father said the timber had been intended for a boat.

“It isn’t a boat now.”

“It is still good timber.”

“Then use it.”

He told her not to be sharp. She became quiet in a way Runa disliked more than the sharpness. After a while Vigdis took two offcuts and made a small demonstration frame on the bench. Solli helped hold it. They put a loaded sack on top, shifted it and lifted together.

Torve watched. He came over, tested the joint, and suggested a different peg. Vigdis made the change. Neither acknowledged that the answer had changed with it.

Vigdis wanted the first frame tried with an uneven load. Her father said they had already proved it could bear the sack. She said a sack was not the only thing the stores would put on it. Solli fetched a jar in a basket and a bundle of tools. They placed both on the frame, lifted, and discovered that the end with the jar dipped enough to make Runa step forward instinctively.

“Put it down,” Torve said.

Vigdis obeyed before the jar moved. She examined the handholds. Runa expected an argument. Instead the girl took a piece of charcoal and marked a different position for them. Her father watched and let her finish.

“Here,” he said, pointing slightly farther in.

“That makes it narrow for gloves.”

“Then widen it towards the other side.”

They worked together for a while. Runa took the jar back to its owner, who objected to its use in experiments more than to the risk of losing it. It had belonged to her mother. Runa apologized and brought an ordinary stone of similar weight for the next trial. Solli laughed at the dignity with which the stone was introduced to the frame.

This time it lifted level. Vigdis carried one end herself, walking the length of the shed and back. She was smiling when they set it down. Torve began to say something about the pegs, stopped, and told her it was good work.

She looked down at the joint. “I know.”

He laughed. The ease of it surprised Runa. She had spent so much of the winter seeing these people in danger of loss that she had begun to expect strain in every exchange.

A customer arrived with a broken oar. The interruption turned Torve back to ordinary business. He examined the break and accused the owner of having used it as a lever. The man denied it. Vigdis took the oar, found a crushed mark halfway along the shaft and held it up. The man changed the denial into an explanation.

While they argued over the cost, Runa and Solli cleared the trial weights from the frame. The boy asked whether the stores would really buy them.

“If they can use them.”

“They can. People will stand on them too, whatever you say.”

“Will they bear that?”

He considered the joint. “Some people.”

They both looked towards the customer, who was large enough to have done the oar damage without intending it. Runa put another question beside the proposed order. When Torve returned she asked him to test for a person climbing on, because she had learned to expect ordinary misuse more confidently than perfect instructions.

He approved of that calculation. Vigdis protested that people were not supposed to stand on a carrying frame. Her father told her they would be especially likely to if someone put it conveniently beside a high shelf. She sighed and went to fetch another sound piece of timber.

Runa ordered a small number for the stores, within the remaining allowance. She asked whether Orri might do some of the fitting when he was able, using a holding jig rather than gripping the work. Torve said perhaps, but not until the hand had healed enough for him to judge it himself.

Runa wrote down the cost of the frames.

Outside, the lower stage remained broken. People had begun using another path around it. The new path was already wearing into mud.

Chapter 34 The larger offer

The finance representative came north after the sea became passable to vessels larger than Hervi’s boats. He arrived with dry copies and a genuine increase in the allowance.

His name was Menas. He had a mild voice and a way of listening that encouraged people to continue until they had supplied the weakness in their own request. Runa had met him once in the south and remembered liking him. She liked him still, which made the meeting less comfortable than if he had been easy to resent.

The Temple would advance more for the permanent crossing and allow an additional reserve for repair. The grain credit would be extended under the district agreement. The larger jurisdiction remained. The household liability remained. The offer recognized the relief work as evidence of need and of a route worth completing.

Leifa spoke in favour of it.

She had been invited because the injured household’s circumstances were being used by people who had not asked her what she wanted. She sat with her hands clasped in her lap and looked at each person before speaking.

“I want the money to finish the crossing. I want the watch paid. I want a place to take a claim when work ruins a hand. If the court is farther away than the hall, then at least it isn’t the same room in which everyone decides not to answer.”

Nobody told her she had misunderstood freedom. Runa was grateful for that much.

Petros explained what the enlarged allowance could build. He did not make the lesser design sound equally good. The smaller road would require more local maintenance and leave a longer period during which the gut depended on ferries. It could work. It would be less forgiving of neglect.

Runa proposed a narrower undertaking, secured by landing receipts rather than a district levy.

Menas asked how much revenue, over what period, and who would guarantee a shortfall.

She had estimates for the first two. The third remained a gap.

“The users have discussed a contribution.”

“Discussed or pledged?”

“Discussed.”

“Then I cannot lend against it.”

Ingrith said the Temple would benefit from a reliable harbour. Menas agreed. He also pointed out that the larger offer already recognized that benefit. He could recommend a smaller secured advance. He could not turn anticipated goodwill into security merely because the rescue had been admirable.

Ragnel spoke only when asked about the court’s scope. She said limited arbitration could be provided for a defined contract. She preferred a more dependable district jurisdiction, but preference did not enlarge her commission. Her answer disappointed both sides in different ways.

They adjourned without a settlement. After the formal meeting Menas asked to see the store.

Runa took him because refusing would have made the grain allowance an argument conducted entirely on paper. They passed the damaged stage and the temporary route around it. Menas stopped to ask how the relief had crossed. She showed him the old attachment points and the position of the higher landing. He listened carefully, then asked which materials could be recovered for permanent use.

She felt a flash of anger at the question’s economy. He was looking at a place where people had been hurt and asking how much timber remained. Then he asked about the injured man, and the anger became less easy to keep.

“Does the present allowance cover his household?”

“Only the first recovery period.”

“That will need a local supplement.”

“Yes.”

“I can include it as a cost of the undertaking. That does not give me the money to pay it.”

At the store he inspected the damp sacks, the grain spread for drying and the tally of spoilage. He did not accuse anyone of carelessness. He asked the cook how much more could be dried if another room were warmed. She said she could find room if he could find fuel and a woman who did not already have three jobs. Menas wrote down the fuel requirement.

The cook laughed without amusement. “You heard half of it.”

He stopped, then added the wages.

Runa watched the pen. She had made the same omission often enough to recognize the discomfort in his pause.

On the way back he spoke of the office’s other northern advances. He did not name households whose affairs were not hers, but he described a store whose receipts had failed after two poor seasons and a harbour whose ruler died before a pledge was renewed. Those losses had made his superiors more restrictive. Money lent here would not be available somewhere else.

Runa said the district proposal answered those difficulties by making people liable who had not controlled them.

“Sometimes,” he said. “And sometimes it keeps a road repaired when the man who promised to repair it has died.”

She did not answer. They walked past the warning path. Tova was carrying a basket towards the clinic, head down against the wind. Menas asked whether the new watch could be supported by landing receipts. Runa said that was what they were trying to arrange.

“Then show me receipts I can defend,” he said. “I can argue for a smaller undertaking. I cannot argue that a good winter’s effort guarantees twenty bad years.”

His candour was useful and left her tired. At the chapel she gave him copies of the last three seasons’ dues, including the poor season. He thanked her for leaving the poor one in.

That evening Leifa came to the schoolroom with the injury undertaking folded in her apron.

“Did I harm the smaller agreement?” she asked.

Runa moved the lamp to make room for her. “You said what you wanted.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“No. I don’t think you harmed it. You made it harder to pretend the difference in funding doesn’t matter.”

Leifa sat. She had come from the clinic; one sleeve was damp, and a loose lock of hair kept falling across her face. She pushed it back with the back of her wrist.

“People tell me not to let them use Orri. Both sides. Then they tell me what to say about his hand.”

“What does he want?”

“Yesterday he wanted everyone to stop asking. Today he wants the bigger crossing built by summer. Tomorrow he may want Torve to admit the stage could have been made differently.”

“Does he think that?”

“He doesn’t know. Neither do I. It broke where he stood.”

Runa had explanations about current, timber and the early wind shift. She could hear how they would sound in the room. She asked instead whether Leifa wanted the technical account read with both of them when Orri could bear it. Leifa said yes, but not by a man hurrying to his next meeting.

They agreed a morning. Then Leifa asked Runa to look at an unrelated debt entry. A neighbour claimed she owed for cloth already paid in labour. Runa read the note and found that two different lengths had been entered together. She asked for the neighbour’s copy before judging it. Leifa sighed, took the paper back and said she had hoped for one thing to be simple.

As she rose, a needle fell from the fold of her apron. Both women bent for it and struck their heads. For a second they stared at each other in offended surprise. Then Leifa began laughing, holding one hand to her forehead, and Runa laughed too. It hurt and was ridiculous. Neither tried to rescue the moment from its silliness.

Runa found the needle beneath the stool. She put it in a little folded scrap so it would not fall out again. Leifa accepted it, still smiling, and left without another question about whether she had spoken correctly at the meeting.

On the agreed morning Runa brought the technical account to Orri’s house. She asked Petros to come without another appointment waiting behind it. He arrived carrying a short piece of timber and no drawing roll.

Leifa had cleared the table. Orri sat beside it with his hand supported. The boy was at a neighbour’s house. Runa noticed the empty place where he usually played and was grateful somebody had considered that before she did.

Petros set down the timber. Orri looked at its broken end.

“Mine?”

“From the same joint. Not the piece that held you.”

“Why that one?”

“So I can show how it sat. I have a drawing too if you want it.”

Orri said the wood would do. Petros placed two smaller pieces against it, one for the post, one for the brace. His first explanation was too fast. Leifa stopped him. He began again, using Runa for words he could not make plain in Linn.

The load had been taken in a direction the temporary stage could bear. The backwater came from behind and lifted where the joint had been holding down. The second force did not replace the first. Men, cargo and moving raft continued pulling while the water pressed underneath. He moved the pieces separately, then together. The broken end opened beneath his thumb.

Orri watched. “You knew the water came behind.”

“We learned it at the first alignment.”

“Then you knew.”

“Yes.”

Leifa drew a breath through her nose. Runa kept her pen still.

Petros explained the forecast turn, the watching posts and the intended time to finish. Orri listened until he reached the allowance for warning.

“I heard it. I was going.”

“I know.”

“Then don’t tell me I should have gone sooner.”

“I am not telling you that.”

“You’re telling me why it ought to have worked.”

Petros looked at the pieces. After a moment he moved them apart.

“I expected more warning.”

“From the girl?”

“From the upper view. Osvin had it with the bell duty and the tally. We did not keep one person there continuously.”

Runa said, “I wrote the arrangement. I thought the errands taken from him left enough time.”

Orri looked from one to the other. “Did you ask him?”

“Yes.”

“And he said he could?”

“Yes.”

“Of course he did.”

The room became quiet. Outside, a wheel scraped over a stone and someone called for the driver to stop. Leifa put a finger on the model brace.

“If there had been a man up there, would this have stayed whole?”

“No,” Petros said. “We would have tried to have the people clear before it broke.”

“And the grain?”

“We would have lost what we could not move safely.”

She nodded slowly. “Then you were willing to lose it.”

“Rather than men, yes.”

“Who was meant to tell the men that?”

“We rehearsed leaving loads,” Runa said.

Orri shifted in his chair. “We did. Torve shouted at him for trying to save a stone sack. I heard.”

Runa remembered the man and his empty hands on the rising path. She had expected the rehearsal to return as evidence they had prepared properly. In Orri’s room it supplied no comfort at all. He had known, obeyed and been hurt.

Leifa asked why his place had been beside the moving brace. Petros showed the ordinary task there, then the retreat route, then the way the dropped corner had closed it. He did not move the little piece back until she said she had seen. Orri reached with his good hand and changed the position slightly.

“I was here.”

Petros looked at him. “That is where we found you.”

“It is where I was before. I had turned.”

The engineer took out the drawing. He asked Orri to mark it. Orri used the blunt end of a stick while Runa entered the correction. The new position made the distance to the open path shorter and the turn around the post sharper. Petros followed it with his finger.

“I should have had a second way clear there.”

Leifa asked whether he would write that. Runa already had. She read the sentence back. Petros assented, then asked her to add the limitation of the temporary construction and the inadequate continuity of the upper watch. Orri listened while each was read. He corrected withdrawing to trying to withdraw. Runa made the change.

There was still the question of payment. Leifa asked whether admitting these things altered what Orri would receive. Runa explained what could be submitted under the undertaking and what remained for the association and estate to agree. She could promise that the account would accompany the injury claim. She could not name a new amount that morning.

“So this is another paper,” Leifa said.

“It is the account they will have to answer.”

“They can take a long time answering.”

“Yes.”

Orri looked down at his hand. He had been sitting very straight throughout the visit. Now his shoulders sagged. Runa began gathering the little pieces, but he put a finger on the broken timber.

“Leave that.”

Petros said he could. He also offered the drawing. Orri wanted both, so Runa arranged to make a copy for the engineer rather than remove the marked one. Leifa took the account and laid it beside the earlier payment sheets.

Petros asked whether there was anything they had not answered. Orri gave a small, exhausted laugh.

“Most of it.”

The engineer nodded. He did not try to convert the answer into another question.

At the door Leifa stopped Runa while Petros went ahead.

“He thought he’d have to shout.”

“He could have.”

“He was saving himself for it.”

Runa looked back. Orri had taken the model apart and was putting it together again one-handed. His face held no satisfaction. The movement seemed to have occupied the place where the shouting might have been.

“I’ll come back this afternoon,” Runa said.

Leifa told her not to wake him if he slept.

Runa found her mother at home holding a letter. It invited her to a spring gathering farther along the coast. She had already written a refusal.

“Why?” Runa asked.

“The journey.”

“Is someone ill?”

“No.”

Runa looked at the letter again. The payment offered would not cover travel and the days away from other work. Once, the household whose patronage Svanhild had lost would have provided the passage.

“I could—”

“No.”

“I haven’t said.”

“I know what you haven’t said.”

Svanhild folded the refusal. Her pride was familiar; the small economy behind it was new to Runa only because she had not needed to inspect it before.

Later her mother cut lamp wicks from old cloth while Runa worked beside the fire. They stayed close to the same light without discussing why.

Chapter 35 The shore everyone uses

Oddrun spread her net where Torve had driven a new stake.

She did it early, before the yard grew busy, and by the time he came out half the net lay weighted along the shore. The stake remained visible through it like a finger raised in objection.

Runa arrived carrying the draft description of Torve’s holding. She stopped when she saw the net.

“You’ve heard,” Oddrun said.

“I’ve come to discuss it.”

“So have I.”

Torve came down from the shed. He looked from the net to the paper and said he had hoped to finish one thing before beginning another.

“This is the same thing,” Runa said.

The proposed boundary enclosed a strip used for drying and mending. Torve said he needed to keep the launching way clear. Oddrun agreed, then asked why the strip extended beyond it. He said people left stones in places where boats struck them. She said his men left timber where nets tore. Both examples were true. Runa had seen the timber and had helped move one of the stones.

The argument continued while Vigdis worked on a cargo frame. People had boats to mend whether the boundary was settled or not. Solli carried pegs down, was asked to move the stake, and looked at Runa before obeying.

“Leave it for now,” she said.

Torve’s face tightened. “It’s my yard.”

“The extent is what we are settling.”

“You helped me keep it.”

“Yes.”

“Now you tell me how much of it belongs to everyone else.”

Runa put the paper on a dry bench. She had expected his resentment. It still hurt, especially when Oddrun looked pleased to have her authority invoked on this side.

“I won’t write the shared strip as if no one else uses it.”

“Then don’t write it.”

He went back to the hull.

Oddrun kept working on the net. Runa sat on an overturned tub beside her and waited until the older woman asked what she was doing.

“Giving him time.”

“He’ll use it to be right.”

“He may use it to mend a boat.”

Oddrun snorted, then pointed to a torn section. Runa took the shuttle and began. She had learned enough in the fish shed to be useful, though her joins were slower. The work gave her something to look at while other users of the shore came past and offered opinions.

A man carrying bait wanted no stakes anywhere. Another wanted marked places for particular nets. A woman who repaired lines for three boats said permanent places would leave her walking from one end to the other and make everyone accuse her of neglecting their gear. Oddrun agreed with her until she proposed moving the largest weights farther from the workshop. Then she discovered objections of her own.

Runa let them argue until a customer trying to reach Torve had to step across the conversation and the net. He nearly tripped. Oddrun caught his sleeve and apologized, then blamed the temporary obstruction on the disputed stake.

“It was your foot,” he said.

“It was a very obvious foot.”

He went into the yard laughing. Runa bent over the torn mesh to hide her own smile.

A practical difficulty emerged from the complaints. In good weather the shore could accommodate them if the boat launch was announced and gear shifted promptly. In prolonged wet, the higher strip was needed by everyone. A fixed division would work least well when the space mattered most.

Runa drew a rough plan on the back of an old tally. Oddrun wanted the drying area larger. Runa asked where the boat would pass. The older woman showed her, then realized she had drawn it across the place she wished to reserve.

“Don’t look pleased,” she said.

“I wasn’t.”

“You nearly were.”

Runa admitted it with a smile. They altered the plan and took it to Vigdis when she came out for more pegs. The girl pointed to the place where timber was delivered and asked them to leave enough room for a man to turn while carrying something longer than himself. Her addition made the drawing untidy again.

By the time Torve returned, the plan had become less convenient for everyone. Oddrun handed it to him without announcing that she had conceded anything. He studied it, asked which marks were temporary, and fetched a piece of cord so they could see the distances on the ground.

Runa spent the next hour taking measurements with Vigdis while Oddrun worked on the net. The girl’s proposed cargo frames occupied less space than the old stack but needed access to buyers at the landing. She pointed out that customers bringing nets also brought repair work.

“If we fence them out, they’ll leave the broken things somewhere else,” she told her father.

“They can ask.”

“They do ask. You aren’t always here.”

Torve examined the measured line. Runa showed him a boundary preserving the workshop and inherited occupation, with public use of the drying strip and a duty to keep the launching passage clear during notified work. It would require someone to give notice and someone to remove the stones. Oddrun could not keep the net across a boat’s path merely because she arrived first.

She objected to that until Vigdis asked whether she intended to pay for a hull dragged over her weights. Then she said notice had to mean more than a man shouting while the boat was already moving.

The first proposed marker was too high for a child to set and too low for a loaded cart to pass beneath. Solli demonstrated the difficulty by carrying a bundle through and knocking it aside with his shoulder. Torve blamed the carelessness. Vigdis blamed the height. Oddrun said children ought not to be responsible for such things in the first place, then remembered how often she sent Jost ahead to prepare a space.

They moved it to a post beside the slip. A red board could be turned outward when a launch was intended, with a bell rung once nearby for people already working below. The notice had to precede the movement; it could not be used to demand a cleared shore while a hull was sliding.

They tried it. Vigdis turned the board. Oddrun gathered a portion of net, discovered that two weights took longer to shift than expected, and made them extend the notice. Torve agreed after watching her move them himself. Then he objected to gear being left overnight beneath the boat’s path. The woman repairing three boats’ lines offered a raised rack which would keep her work accessible and clear of the slip.

Runa’s hand grew cramped as she recorded the uses. At one point she put down the pen and helped lift the rack into the proposed position. It fitted only after an old broken cask was moved. Inside the cask they found a nest of dried grass and no animal. Solli inspected it gravely, declared the previous tenant departed, and tipped out the rotten bottom.

The laughter made the next objection easier to hear. Torve wanted the cost of the rack shared. Oddrun wanted his timber contribution counted against the extra shore space his yard occupied. They could have quarrelled all afternoon over that. Instead Vigdis named the pieces from the salvage pile which were too short for her frames and long enough for the rack. Her father agreed to use them. The users would supply the work.

It was an untidy settlement, requiring people to remember to turn a board and move wet things before they became an emergency. Runa had no shorter wording that would do those jobs for them.

They agreed who would turn the board before a launch. By midday everyone wanted food more than victory.

Torve accepted the narrower boundary after checking that the estate could not use public access as a pretext to install another boatwright in his workshop. Runa included that distinction and had the draft read aloud.

Oddrun moved the net before the afternoon launch. Torve helped lift its wet edge without being asked. Neither apologized.

The boat had been waiting on the slip long enough to leave a pale shape beneath it where the rest of the ground had weathered. Its owner came down with two men who had helped pay for the repairs. They all wanted to inspect the same fastening and gave three different accounts of what had damaged it. Torve stood beside them, letting the argument use itself up.

Runa ought to have taken the amended description to the clerk. She stayed because Vigdis had asked her to see the launch, and because for once she wanted to watch a boat enter the water without a list of grain depending on it.

Solli brought the grease. It had thickened in the cold. He warmed the vessel between his hands, then gave up and carried it nearer the shed fire. When he returned, the owner was asking whether that much was necessary. Torve told him he could save grease or the bottom of his boat.

Vigdis turned the red board. A man below it continued laying out a line until Oddrun called to him. He looked up, saw the board and began gathering his work with bad grace. His basket had tipped, spilling the hooks. Nobody moved the boat while he collected them. One of the waiting men complained. Oddrun asked whether he intended to find the last hook with his bare foot.

The line maker counted twice. Then he moved clear and lifted his hand.

Vigdis went down the slip, checking the path as she walked. She picked up a stone no larger than Runa’s thumb and threw it aside. At the bottom she tested the water with her boot, retreated from the wash and called up to her father.

The men took their places. There was a brief argument about whether the owner should stand aboard. Torve said he could climb in when the boat floated. The owner asked whether he thought him unable to keep his footing. Torve looked at the fine boots and said nothing. One of the other men took the owner by the elbow and led him towards the water.

Runa smiled down at her papers.

The first push produced a wooden groan and no movement. The second moved the hull a little, enough that everyone stopped together to check it. Then the boat began sliding more readily. The men walked with it, shoulders lowered, one calling the pace. Runa had seen ships launched ceremonially with flowers and witnesses, but this repaired boat went down with a curse when somebody’s foot slipped and a shout to keep a pole clear.

At the water it lifted. The change was small and astonishing: the weight the men had pushed became something the tide carried. The owner stopped discussing the boots and stared. Vigdis held the line while he climbed aboard. He put his hand on the new repair, then sat and reached for an oar.

Torve told him not to try the whole coast before paying.

“I’d have to take you with me to settle what I owe.”

“Then stay near shore.”

The owner pulled a little way out and turned. The boat answered. He tried another turn, more sharply, and came back grinning in spite of himself. One of the men who had contributed to the work asked to try. They changed places clumsily. The second man rowed worse and enjoyed it just as much.

Runa stood until the cold reached through her soles. Beside her, Oddrun had begun returning the net to its drying place. She left the slip clear without being asked. Solli righted the board and carried the grease back to the shed.

Vigdis came up from the water with wet boots. Her father glanced at them.

“You went too far in.”

“It floated.”

“It would have floated with your feet dry.”

She rolled her eyes where he could not see. Runa did see, and Vigdis smiled at her. It was the first invitation to share a private joke the girl had offered without expecting something useful in return.

The boat came in again. Its owner wanted to speak about a second vessel needing work before summer. Torve went down to meet him. Runa gathered the amended description and left them discussing timber with their hands on the rail.

Chapter 36 What Ingrith gives up

They spent an evening assembling the pledge before taking it to Ingrith.

Runa had sat in the landing store while six members of the association argued over a table scarred by scales and knife work. Selka attended because her household carried goods for two of them. Oddrun came as a user of the shore and objected when someone described her work as incidental to trade.

The first proposed due fell equally on every landing. That pleased the men whose cargoes were valuable and light. The next followed weight, which pleased a different group and angered the fish carriers. A third exempted local food, at which Selka asked whether food ceased to be local when someone bought it on the next coast and brought it home.

Runa wrote each proposal and crossed it out only when its supporters withdrew it. She had to turn the page sideways to fit the last objection.

One trader wanted a permanent exemption for emergency relief. Leifa, invited as promised, asked how a relief system could be maintained if its busiest work never contributed anything. He said he did not propose charging starving people. She said she did not propose feeding injured workers on admiration.

The argument stopped long enough for people to hear the stove ticking.

They settled on ordinary dues funding the reserve, with emergency cargo accounted separately and the association’s guarantee limited in amount. Households receiving relief would not be charged at the point of hunger. The cost would return through later receipts and the agreed local contribution. Leifa read the limit back to them.

Leifa asked for the injury provision to extend through retraining, reducing as earnings resumed rather than ending at the first day he managed useful work. The association accepted a limited period. She asked for longer. They refused. She said she would come back if it proved insufficient, and nobody told her she could not.

By the end of the evening they had a pledge smaller than Runa hoped and signed by people who understood what they were risking. The next morning she brought it to Ingrith.

The jarl asked who would be angry first.

The trade men looked uncertain whether she was joking. Runa, who had brought their proposed dues, waited.

“Among my kin,” the jarl clarified. “I know the rest of you will take turns.”

Dagrin named the household whose exemption would end. It was not Svanhild’s former patron alone, though that household belonged to the group affected. The exemption covered certain cargoes brought under kin service. Ending it would produce usable revenue and a quarrel which no calculation could make disappear.

Runa laid the signed pledge beside Dagrin’s account of the exempted cargoes.

Menas examined the figures. Petros supplied the reduced works schedule.

Ingrith listened to the association’s spokesman, then asked whether he would still pay during a poor season.

“Within the stated limit.”

“And after the limit?”

“The advance must be sized to it.”

She nodded. “A sensible thing to say when someone else needs the larger road.”

The man flushed but did not withdraw it.

Runa showed the portion of harbour income still needed. Ingrith tapped the figure with a nail.

“That pays other promises.”

“Yes.”

“Which ones do you recommend I fail?”

Runa did not answer. She was glad she had not prepared a noble speech about shared sacrifice; it would have sounded ridiculous in this room.

The jarl sent for food before they went further.

One trader said he could wait. Ingrith told him she could not, and a servant came in with bread, fish and a pot which had plainly been intended for fewer people. Bowls were fetched from another room. The discussion paused while everybody pretended not to notice who received the best one.

Runa found herself beside the cousin whose exemption they proposed to end. He moved his cup to give her room. The courtesy made her more uncomfortable than an insult would have done.

“My ships carried timber when this hall was repaired,” he said.

“I know there was service attached to the exemption.”

“You know a line in an account.”

Runa looked at him. He had thick hands and a scar near the base of his thumb. He was not the harmlessly idle kinsman she had found convenient to imagine while adding his cargoes to the estimate.

“I was aboard one,” he said. “The first attempt lost the rudder. We came in round the point with a steering oar borrowed from a fishing boat.”

“Which boat?”

He named it. Hervi had spoken of that vessel during the survey. Runa asked whether the replacement oar had been paid for. The cousin smiled a little.

“Twice. The owner kept finding further damage.”

She smiled back before remembering the table. He broke his bread and began eating. He asked whether Hervi still claimed to have steered her home alone. Runa had heard that account too.

Across from them Leifa was trying to divide a hard piece of bread without disturbing the papers. Menas moved the ink away and gave her the clear corner. Ingrith ate quickly, listening while Dagrin spoke in a low voice about a household due to supply summer work. Runa could not hear the details. She saw the jarl shake her head, then ask something which sent the steward for another list.

The cousin watched too.

“You will be gone when the crews are asked,” he said.

“Perhaps.”

“We will still have to answer.”

Runa put down her bread. “There will still be dues to collect if nothing is built.”

“Yes. I know.”

He sounded tired rather than defeated. She let the exchange end there. It was easier when she could think him merely protecting a comfort. Now she would have to keep the memory of his boat and still recommend ending the exemption.

A child entered the room carrying a small wooden horse. A servant caught him before he reached the table, but he had already seen Ingrith and called to her. She held out a hand. He came to show her that one wheel had fallen off.

“Who mends these?” she asked him.

He pointed to Dagrin.

“Then ask Dagrin when he has finished.”

The child wanted to know when that would be. Several people laughed. Ingrith did not. She examined the wheel, put it in the child’s pocket so it would not be lost, and told him to eat before making further demands on anyone. He accepted a piece of bread from the servant and left.

The jarl watched the doorway a moment longer, then pushed her bowl aside. Runa gathered the figures out of reach of the crumbs. The cousin put his cup beside the exemption entry, close enough that Runa moved the paper.

Dagrin began naming expenditures that could be reduced or delayed. Some were obvious comforts. Others supported people who would have little defence against a delay. Ingrith rejected one reduction because the household had already supplied men for the relief. She accepted another and told Dagrin to speak to them before the decision travelled by rumour.

One member of the association asked whether the harbour share could be reduced in years when receipts fell below the estimate. Ingrith said that would leave the guarantee answering precisely when her share ceased to do so. The man looked at Menas. The representative nodded.

“We can set a reserve before the first surplus is paid,” Menas said. “We cannot promise every contributor he will be the last person exposed.”

They worked through a poor season using actual figures from the books. Runa moved counters across the table while Dagrin read the receipts. Without the kin exemption, the amount was better but still uncomfortable. With the exemption preserved, the maintenance reserve disappeared too quickly.

Ingrith watched the counters. When the last small pile was divided, she asked how much grain credit remained.

Menas gave the lower figure. A trade man began to say that families would manage as they always had. Leifa, who had come to hear what provision might be secured, made a small sound of disgust. The man stopped and looked at the table.

Runa wanted him to hear her, but she also wanted him to keep his pledge. She disliked discovering how quickly those wants could become a wish that Leifa would speak more tactfully. She left the counters in place and asked Menas to repeat the figure so no one could later say the loss had been obscured.

Dagrin fetched another account. This one showed outstanding dues which might be collected, though not soon enough to count as cash. Ingrith struck out two debts she knew would never be paid. The association’s spokesman protested that the total was being diminished. She told him he was welcome to collect from a dead boat and a burned store if he found them.

Runa removed the two debts from her copy.

Ingrith’s cousin objected to the loss of the exemption. He argued from service, from precedent, then from the danger of allowing Temple clerks to define local custom. Ingrith let him finish.

“The Temple clerk has not ended your exemption,” she said. “I am considering it.”

“Because she has made it necessary.”

“Because you want a harbour without paying the same as people who use it less.”

The cousin pushed his chair back and left. Ingrith sat for a while without speaking. Dagrin waited near the door, ready to follow when instructed.

“They’ll withhold two crews next season,” she said at last.

“Possibly,” Dagrin answered.

“Certainly. Find out which before I promise them elsewhere.”

She accepted the revenue assignment and the end of the exemption, subject to the smaller advance being approved. Menas could now recommend a secured contract rather than ask his office to trust the valley’s intentions.

Runa gathered the pages. She felt relief and tried not to show it too broadly in front of the people who had just lost something.

Ingrith stopped her at the door.

“Your mother’s winter payment is not in this.”

“I know.”

“I will not have that confusion later.”

“There is no confusion.”

The jarl studied her, then dismissed her with a nod.

Dagrin caught up with her at the outer door.

“I’ll send the amended receipts to the chapel.”

“Thank you.”

“Read the third page before you copy. The new share begins after the present cargo, not before.”

“I heard.”

“I know. Put it where the people collecting will hear it too.”

She promised. They stood beneath the lintel while two servants carried a bench past. Dagrin looked more tired than angry now that the meeting had ended. His work would include telling kin why an exemption had ended and telling ordinary users why the new dues were necessary, with little chance of pleasing either.

“Kolfin may go back west,” he said unexpectedly.

“I thought the winter work there had fallen behind.”

“It had. It is catching up. He came here expecting room.”

Runa thought of the man standing in Torve’s shed, careful not to touch anything. “Could he work on the permanent crossing?”

“If there is one to build soon enough. He has men to keep.”

She said she would ask Petros which contracted work might be offered without displacing local crews already promised it. Dagrin nodded. He did not thank her for considering a household on his side of the dispute. Perhaps he felt she had taken long enough.

At the path he turned back towards the hall. Runa watched him go, then put the signed pledge farther inside her coat where thaw water could not reach it.

At home that evening she told Svanhild what had been agreed. Her mother listened, asked one question about the kin exemption, and went back to cutting lamp wicks.

Chapter 37 A correction with listeners

Svanhild crossed out the word remembered.

She had written that she had remembered a fuller account. It suggested the account had come back to her of its own accord, as if it had been waiting politely beyond the edge of recall. She wrote knew above it and sat looking at the uglier sentence.

The formal correction would accompany the description of Torve’s holding. Runa had offered to take it down from speech. Svanhild had said she could write. Now she had spent most of the morning proving that writing did not prevent evasion.

She put the paper aside and went to the house where she had been invited to eat. It was a modest invitation from a woman whose winter food Svanhild had once helped obtain. The woman did not mention that. She asked whether Svanhild would mind sitting near the door because the warmer place was needed by her old father.

Svanhild said she preferred it. This was a lie told for an ordinary kindness, and she was tired enough of examining her own speech to let it pass.

They ate fish and a thick barley dish. The old man complained that people no longer knew the right tune for a certain departure account. Svanhild sang the beginning. He said that was the wrong one too. She asked him to show her, and he produced four wavering notes before running out of breath. They were not the notes she knew. She tried them back to him and he smiled.

For a little while she enjoyed herself.

The old man wanted the rest of the departure account. Svanhild knew nearly all of it, though not to his tune, and offered to put the words to the notes he had given her. He watched her lips as she began. At the second turn his hand lifted from the table. She waited for a correction, but he was only following the departure of the boat.

His daughter sat down with the empty pot still in her hands.

It was a poor place to sing. The door did not close properly; someone outside was chopping wood; the old man interrupted twice to ask whether a name had been said. Svanhild had sung in halls where everyone waited through the silence before she chose to begin. Here she raised her voice over the axe, repeated the name without annoyance, and found herself leaning towards the table as though the boat might leave without her.

The old man knew what happened at the last headland. He said the first word before she did, almost spoiling it. She gave him the second. They reached the turn together, his thin voice trailing hers.

When it was finished he asked for a little more barley.

His daughter fetched it. Svanhild felt foolishly disappointed. She had wanted him to say she had brought something back to him, perhaps wanted it more than she wanted him to remember. He was occupied with getting the spoon between his lips without spilling. She drank from her cup and let him eat.

The daughter accompanied her to the door.

“He will ask you for that again.”

“I can come.”

“On a day you are passing. I didn’t mean to make another duty for you.”

Svanhild assured her it would not be a duty. The woman opened the door, then kept her hand on it.

“I was in the hall,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I did not clap. Someone behind me started. I couldn’t.”

Svanhild had not heard any applause. She remembered only the sound of the people moving and Torve looking at the floor.

“You needn’t explain.”

“I thought you might have seen.”

“No.”

The woman nodded, but did not release the door. Svanhild understood that she had not finished.

“My father remembers your master. He thinks the two of you sang together last winter. I have told him how long it has been.”

“It comes back unevenly.”

“He’ll ask you things.”

“I know.”

“I’m not asking you to agree with him.”

Svanhild looked back. The old man had set down his spoon and was licking a finger where the barley had spilled. Beyond him hung the little shelf on which the household kept its cups, all turned handles outward. She had known the room for years and could not remember whether she had noticed that before.

“I can tell him when I don’t remember.”

His daughter let go of the door.

On the way home Svanhild met the woman who had taken back the travelling wrap. They stepped to the same side of the narrow path, corrected in the same direction, and laughed awkwardly. The woman asked about the weather. Svanhild answered. They might have got past one another on that little exchange had a boy not come running down the path with a hoop, forcing them both against the wall.

“You still have my pin,” the woman said.

Svanhild had forgotten the pin. It had been in the wrap when it was lent, and she had removed it to fasten her own cloak at the feast.

“I’ll bring it.”

“There is no hurry.”

“I’ll bring it this afternoon.”

The woman looked embarrassed by the promise’s precision. “My sister asked after it. That was all.”

Svanhild almost said that she had things of her own. Instead she said she would look for it as soon as she reached home. They parted. A few steps later she turned, but the woman was walking away too quickly to call back without making everyone on the path attend.

She found the pin where it should not have been, among Eydis’s ribbons. The girl had borrowed it to hold a folded strip while sewing. There was no theft, no intended insult, only a thing passed from hand to hand because everybody had assumed there would be time to return it. Svanhild wrapped it in cloth and laid it by the door. Then she went upstairs for the correction.

The witnessed correction took place in the schoolroom. Fewer people came than had attended the dramatic admission in the hall. This made it easier to speak and harder to hide inside the occasion. Torve sat with Vigdis. Oddrun came because the shore description concerned her. Dagrin represented the estate. Two people who remembered the older account attended separately, each determined not to let the other improve his recollection.

Runa sat with the record before her.

Svanhild read what she had written. When she reached the crossed-out place she used the new word without explaining the alteration. She named the account she had carried, the older testimony she had known, and the part she had left out. She said she had believed the hall’s continuing provision depended on retaining authority over the service. She did not ask the listeners to agree that this had entitled her to decide for Torve’s house.

The witnesses added their evidence. One corrected a boat name. Another could not swear to a boundary mentioned in the copied fragment. The record preserved both limits.

Torve waited until they had finished.

“What about the years before this?” he asked.

Svanhild looked at him.

“My father paid for repairs he thought he had to accept to keep the place. He let timber stand on the shore until it spoiled because the steward said the landing wasn’t ours to refuse. What happens to those years?”

Dagrin began to explain that compensation was outside the present record. Torve held up a hand.

“I asked her.”

Svanhild put her paper down.

“I can’t return them.”

“I know.”

He sat back. The answer had not satisfied him because the question had not been seeking information. She would have to live with being the person to whom it could still be asked.

Runa read the final record aloud. Svanhild made her mark. Torve acknowledged receipt without offering his hand. The other witnesses signed or marked in their turn.

After the witnesses left, Svanhild remained while Runa sanded the wet lines. The room smelled of ink and cooling wax. Outside, someone was asking Torve whether the statement meant the old jarl’s promise had been lawful from the beginning. He replied that he was going back to work.

“Will you keep that copy here?” Svanhild asked.

“One here. One with the holding record. One for you, if you want it.”

“I ought to.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Her daughter’s voice was gentle enough to make the familiar words painful. Svanhild looked at the page. Her admission occupied fewer lines than she expected. Years of withholding could be read aloud in minutes.

“Yes,” she said. “I want it.”

Runa set aside a clean sheet. They waited for the ink to dry before gathering anything. Svanhild could have offered to help with the copying. She did not know whether the offer would be welcome, and for once left the uncertainty unfilled.

Eydis came to the door with a question about supper. She looked at the empty chairs and asked whether they had finished. Runa said yes.

“Was it all right?”

Svanhild almost answered that it was. Then she saw her daughter watching her.

“It is written,” she said.

Eydis accepted this because she had a more urgent question about whether the fish in the covered bowl was meant to be cooked again. Svanhild said certainly not. They went home together, arguing mildly over whether the girl had been told that before.

At home the loose hook by the door finally pulled out of the wall under Runa’s coat.

The coat fell into a wet place. Runa swore, picked it up and stood looking at the hook as if it had chosen the day deliberately. Svanhild fetched a peg and the small hammer. They had repaired this hook badly more than once. This time they removed the softened piece beneath it and cut a new seat farther along the sound wood.

Runa held the peg while Svanhild started it. Then they changed places because Runa could strike more cleanly at that angle.

“I shouldn’t have spoken of the school that way,” Svanhild said.

The hammer stopped.

“I wanted you to go.”

“I know.”

“I did not give it so you would owe me this.”

Runa lowered the hammer. Her face was turned partly away.

“You made it feel like that.”

“Yes.”

Outside, a cart went past slowly, its wheel clicking at each turn. Svanhild watched her daughter’s hand around the hammer.

“I was frightened,” she began, then stopped. She could hear how the sentence might ask to be forgiven before it had finished.

Runa set the hook against the new peg.

“Hold it there.”

Svanhild held it. Her daughter struck twice, tested the fastening, and hung the coat. It stayed. They stood side by side looking at the small successful repair.

Chapter 38 The smaller road

The office accepted less than the valley wanted and more than Menas had first been authorized to offer.

The smaller advance would be secured against the pledged landing receipts and the assigned portion of Ingrith’s income. The trade association’s guarantee covered a defined shortfall, not every bad season. Maintenance had to be funded before any surplus was distributed. The warning watch would have paid shifts, named replacements and a repair allowance of its own.

The requested grain-credit ceiling was refused.

Runa read that sentence three times, hoping to find a qualification she could use. There was none. The office considered the exposure too large under the narrower security. The district offer would have provided more.

At the final meeting Leifa said so.

“We could have had the reserve.”

“Yes,” Runa said.

“And the permanent crossing sooner.”

“Yes.”

The woman folded her arms. “Then don’t tell me we have all got what we need.”

Nobody did. Ingrith had authority to assign her revenues; the landing users ratified their dues; the households required to provide specific work gave their assent through the agreed local process. Enough agreed for the undertaking to proceed. Those who preferred incorporation remained in the room while the decision went the other way.

Ragnel certified the limited arbitration clause. Contract disputes arising under the undertaking would go to the synod on the defined terms. Other matters remained with the Thing unless separately submitted. She checked the language concerning enforcement twice and removed a phrase that could have enlarged it by implication. Runa watched her do it with gratitude complicated by the knowledge that Ragnel still preferred the wider court.

Torve’s occupation and inheritance record was attached separately. Public access to the working shore remained. The papers did not merge every local right into the road simply because they were signed on the same day.

When it was finished, people wanted to leave. They had spent enough hours discussing the work to resent another minute in which it was not being done. Menas packed his copies. Petros rolled the revised drawings. Runa remained to check the witness list, then went outside into a wind that smelled of open water.

She walked the road with Gerd the next morning.

It already needed attention. A wheel had cut a rut in a softened place. Water stood where the last clearing had left too shallow an outlet. They marked repairs and spoke to the workers who would do them. The paid watch had not yet begun its full schedule; Tova and another keeper were learning how to divide the shifts around clinic work. A third person would cover absences. No one was expected to be permanently available merely because a parent had been.

Gerd asked her to return in two days to witness the first paid repair under the new arrangement. Runa put the day in her book, pleased by how ordinary the appointment sounded.

It rained both days.

When she arrived, the men were waiting under a sheet held between two poles. There was work they could do and work the weather would spoil if they began it. One had brought his own barrow from a considerable distance. He wanted to know whether waiting would be paid. Gerd said the agreed hours included the time after he had reported, but that he would be sent home if the ground did not improve. The man asked whether he would be paid for bringing the barrow back tomorrow. That had not been agreed.

Runa opened the book. They could pay for the barrow’s use. They could not reasonably expect its owner to make a daily journey because the forecast kept changing. Gerd proposed leaving it under cover near the work. The man needed it at home. By the time they settled on hiring a different barrow from a nearer house, the first owner was irritated enough to say his was better made.

“It is,” Gerd said. “Take it home before we spoil it.”

He laughed reluctantly and accepted the payment for the journey already made. Runa entered it against preparation, then went with Gerd to the softened outlet.

The ditch held water all along its upper edge. The bank itself was sound. They needed to remove the material washed down during the thaw, give the flow room at the turn and secure the places where feet had cut it. None of this required a new drawing from Petros. Gerd had the approved line and the authority to keep it working.

He explained the tasks to the remaining crew. Runa recognized two men from the failed alignment and a woman who had carried blankets during the relief. They took up their tools without waiting for an engineer to appear. She moved out of the way.

At the first obstruction a worker found a child’s wooden wheel. It had lodged against a stone and caught a tangle of grass behind it. He held it up, asking whose it was. Nobody knew. He put it on the bank rather than throw it into the spoil. Runa thought of the jarl’s little visitor and almost claimed it for him, then remembered there must be dozens of such toys along the coast.

The water began moving when the grass came free. It carried more dirt than she expected. Gerd watched the colour, told the men where to stop and let it clear before deepening the outlet. Runa stood with her hood drawn close, discovering that properly paid maintenance could still require a great deal of waiting in rain.

A woman came along the road carrying eggs in a basket. She stopped before the narrowest place, waiting for a worker to move. He reached to help her without asking, and she stepped back so sharply he nearly put his hand through the handle.

“I can carry it. Give me the path.”

He moved the shovel. She passed. Farther along, she turned and asked whether the new surface would reach her house before the end of summer. Gerd told her where the present contract ended. It was short of her door. She looked at the comfortable stretch beneath her feet and sighed.

“It would be a shame to stop there.”

“Yes,” he said.

She went on. Runa had learned to hear requests in every observation and had to resist searching the allowance for an extension she could not fund. Instead she marked the unfunded stretch on the next report, where it would be read as a proposed need rather than a promise.

The rain eased towards midday. The crew ate under the sheet. One man had brought sour curds wrapped so badly that they had spread inside his bag. He accused his daughter of doing it deliberately. The woman beside him said she had seen him pack it himself. He examined the damage and announced that the girl had distracted him. They gave him no sympathy, but somebody supplied a clean bowl.

Runa ate with them. The food was plain, the conversation occupied by households she was beginning to know without needing their relationships explained. She understood a complaint about a missing hen before the owner was named and felt a small, foolish pleasure at belonging to the joke.

A cart came along the completed stretch while they ate. It was not carrying relief, only household goods and a stool tied awkwardly on top. The driver kept one hand on the stool until a child beside him took over. They passed the old muddy turn without unloading. Nobody cheered. One worker lifted his bowl in greeting, and the driver lifted a finger from the reins.

After the break Gerd asked Runa to see the cleared outlet before it was covered at the crossing point. She knelt, looked, and asked a question which required him to show the depth with his hand. She could now distinguish the useful answer from the comforting nod she would have given at the beginning of winter.

They signed the completed tasks. The men left with the next day’s arrangement spoken aloud. Runa remained to help fold the shelter sheet. Water ran down her sleeve as they lifted it, exactly as it had at the lee cove. This time she had dry cuffs at home and no need to hurry beyond the gut.

Gerd picked up the little wheel before leaving.

“Somebody will come looking.”

He put it on the fence post where it could be seen from the road.

At Orri’s house, the immediate wages had been paid. The association and the estate had agreed a further provision, limited but sufficient to give the household time to reorganize its work. Torve was preparing a bench jig for the injured man to try when the sister allowed it. Orri had not promised to be grateful for a new craft. He still wanted the old use of his hand.

Runa left after hearing him complain about the jig’s height. The complaint was practical enough that she carried it to Torve without interpretation.

The first trial of the jig took place on a morning when Orri had already washed, dressed and exhausted himself getting to the yard.

Runa arrived with the work allowance and found Leifa standing outside the shed. She was holding his coat. The day had warmed enough that neither of them needed one while moving, but there was no place to hang it clear of the wet timber.

“Will you take that?” she asked.

Runa took it. Leifa went in.

Torve had made the device lower than his own bench and left the fastening loose enough to change. A piece of scrap lay between two wooden jaws. Orri could hold it still by pressing a lever with his uninjured hand, but that left him no hand with which to work. Torve explained how to set the peg so the pressure would remain when he let go.

“I heard you.”

He tried it. The peg caught too high. The scrap moved under the tool.

“Again,” Torve said.

“I can see that.”

Vigdis carried a board past them without looking. Solli, less practised at seeming occupied, studied a perfectly ordinary knot in the wall. Runa wished she had brought the payment an hour later. She put the coat on the back of an unused chair and stayed by it, unable to leave without appearing to have come only to inspect him.

On the fourth attempt the scrap held. Orri drew the tool towards him. The cut came away long and clean, curling over his thumb. He stared at it.

Torve said nothing.

Orri reset the wood. This time he found the peg easily. He made three more cuts, then put the tool down with an expression Runa could not read.

“Too slow.”

“For what?” Torve asked.

“For earning.”

“You are learning where the peg is.”

“I knew where things were.”

The shed was quiet except for Vigdis driving a fastening at the far end. Leifa stood behind the chair. Runa kept her eyes on the loose shaving so he would not find all of them watching his face.

He picked up the tool again. His hurt hand shifted towards the work before he stopped it, and the sudden check made him swear. A little blood had come through the binding near the thumb. Leifa went forward.

“Sit.”

“I am sitting.”

“Then stay there.”

She fetched a clean strip from her bag. He let her lift the hand. While she examined it he looked at Torve.

“Could it be nearer the edge?”

“The whole thing?”

“The lever. I’m reaching across myself.”

Torve moved to his side, leaned over and tried the movement. He had to keep one hand against his chest to imitate the limitation. After a moment he nodded.

“I can move it.”

“Before you charge for it.”

“I haven’t charged.”

“Then before you start.”

Vigdis laughed from the other end of the shed. Orri glanced at her. She did not look away quickly enough, and for a moment Runa feared he would take offence. Then he asked whether she was always so slow with a fastening or only when listening. Vigdis showed him the bent nail somebody else had left in the wood. He accused her of keeping it ready for excuses.

They stayed until the sister came looking for him. She had said a short visit. He maintained that this was one. She asked him to stand. His face changed as he rose, and the argument ended.

Outside, Runa helped him into the coat. She put the injured arm in first, as Leifa directed, feeling the awkward weight of another person’s clothing and the intimacy of doing it badly. He endured her until the sleeve caught.

“Pull the cloth, woman. Not me.”

She loosened it at once. “Sorry.”

He stood still while she finished.

On the walk home he stopped twice. The second time he pretended to inspect a drain. Runa stopped with him and examined it too. It was blocked with leaves. He said someone ought to clear that before another rain. She said she would tell Gerd. He asked why she could not do it herself.

So she put down the payment case, fetched a stick and cleared the mouth while Orri leaned against the wall with his eyes closed. Leifa held his good hand behind the shelter of their coats.

Three days later the lower watch began keeping its new book.

Runa went up with Tova to deliver the spare paper and found the first relief already waiting, embarrassed at being early. The woman had brought a meal large enough to share, a blanket, and a stool nobody had asked her to bring. Tova told her where it could stand without hiding the lowest mark on the shore. They moved it twice before she was satisfied.

The book listed more than water. A broken rung. Oil used. A fastening beginning to loosen. The time the next keeper was expected and the name of the person to fetch if no one came. Runa read the last provision while Tova tested the lamp.

“What if the person to fetch is away?” Runa asked.

“Then the keeper doesn’t leave.”

“For how long?”

Tova put down the lamp. “Until somebody comes.”

“That is how it was before.”

The waiting woman looked from one to the other.

Tova took the book and sat. They worked out a second name, then a place at which the absence would be reported if neither replacement could be found. The waiting woman suggested the store, where somebody would notice a missed return before the clinic did. Tova wrote it in herself.

Runa had expected to sign and go. By the time they had finished she was hungry enough to accept the offered meal. They ate with the door open to the spring air. The new keeper asked whether every quiet hour had to be entered. Tova said a mark at the change of watch would do if nothing had changed.

“And if I hear something?”

“What?”

“I don’t know yet.”

Tova looked out over the water. “Write what you hear.”

The woman asked whether she should ring for a seal on the lower step. Tova said only if it had learned to move the landing. They laughed, and Runa was glad of it. Then Tova showed her the places a swimming seal could resemble floating timber, and neither of them laughed while she explained where to look next.

The southern offer came with the next packet.

It was for a better-paid post supervising records across several harbour works. Her report on the failed alignment had impressed the office more than she expected; she had documented the error, paid for the correction and kept the revised costs legible. The letter praised reliability rather than the outcome of the district negotiation. It did not require her to recommend incorporation elsewhere.

She read it in the little room above the kitchen while Eydis slept. The pay would allow better lodgings, regular schooling, and fewer nights worrying about a winter payment delayed by someone else’s account. The work would be demanding. She wanted it immediately. She read the names of the harbours again, imagining the packet from each arriving on her desk, the different hands she would learn to recognize. There would be clerks to train. She began considering whom she might ask for before remembering that she had not accepted.

Then she looked at her daughter’s sleeping face and the warm wall beside the bed.

She folded the letter. Downstairs her mother was laughing with a visitor. Runa sat listening until the laughter ended, then read the offer again.

Chapter 39 A saga has to be heard

Eydis had promised Jost that the winter account would contain a dog.

She could not remember making the promise quite so firmly, but he remembered hearing it. He had come to the house with three versions of how the dog might have helped at the landing. In one it warned the men. In another it stole a glove which somebody then had to pursue to higher ground. In the third it simply ate the meat intended for a worker and was chased away before anything happened.

“The third is true,” he said.

“Whose meat?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then how do you know it’s true?”

“Because it was eating some.”

Eydis looked at him. He grinned, conceding that this was not enough. They went out to find the animal, which had no interest in supplying evidence and was asleep in a sunny place behind the shed.

The day was warm enough for both children to leave their cloaks open. Water dripped from an eave into a broken pan. Every few breaths the dog opened one eye to see whether they still expected something of it. Eydis sat on a low wall and spread her pages on her knees.

Jost said the account had too many people who stood still talking. She said that was what people had done. He asked why she had not put in the man who fell in the ditch. She had, but in a note. He wanted to know why the entertaining things were always in the notes.

She had no answer she liked.

They tried speaking the beginning to the dog. It went back to sleep. Jost maintained that the animal was a poor listener because it already knew where all the food had been. Eydis threw a little twig at him. He caught it, broke it in two and handed back the shorter piece as her share.

Vigdis found them there on her way from the yard. She read the first page upside down while Eydis was still speaking and said she could not hear half the words when the girl looked at her knees.

“I need to see it.”

“Then hold it up.”

“The wind takes it.”

“Put a board behind it.”

Eydis had been making the same objection for days without considering a board. She went inside for one. When she returned, Vigdis had sat beside Jost and the dog was awake enough to accept a scratch behind the ear.

For a little while they talked about the summer instead of the winter. Jost knew a place beyond the racks where flat stones warmed in the sun. Vigdis said the water there stayed cold whatever the stones did. He asked whether she was afraid of cold water. She said she was afraid of wasting a warm afternoon proving water cold.

Eydis laughed. She wanted to see the place. She wanted to be included in plans made because people expected her to be nearby when the weather changed. It had happened slowly enough that she had not noticed the expectation forming.

Vigdis could not stay. There was a new order for frames, and her father had agreed to let her set out the joints. She said it as casually as possible. Jost immediately spoiled the attempt by congratulating her too loudly. She told him to keep his voice down unless he wanted the whole village to arrive with suggestions.

“Bring the account up tonight,” she said to Eydis. “I’ll listen after.”

“With Grandmother?”

“If she’ll listen to me.”

Eydis said she would. After Vigdis left, Jost admitted he was not certain about the meat. The dog might have been chewing a strip of old leather.

“We can leave it out,” Eydis said.

He looked relieved and disappointed. She understood both feelings. They sat with the dog until the sun moved off the stones, then went inside before Svanhild could find them and give them something to carry.

That evening Eydis set her board at the proper height and began.

The first version was accurate and dull.

Eydis discovered this halfway through, when Jost began picking at a loose thread on his cuff. Vigdis listened more politely, which was worse. Svanhild sat with her chin on her hand and waited for Eydis to reach the place where three witnesses disagreed about the order of the sledges.

“That is where I put their names,” Eydis said.

“I can hear that,” her grandmother answered.

“So people know.”

“Yes.”

Eydis looked down at the page. She had worked hard on it. Every time someone corrected a detail she had added a note. The notes now interrupted the movement more often than the movement interrupted them.

Vigdis said she liked the part about the broken runner.

“That is because you’re in it.”

“No. It is because something happens.”

Jost laughed, then stopped when Eydis glared at him.

Svanhild took the page, asking first. She read a section silently, then said it aloud with a different rhythm. She left out two explanations and moved the runner’s crack to the beginning. Suddenly the waiting made sense as something the listeners had to endure with the crew.

“But you left out who repaired it,” Eydis said.

“Then put him where he repairs it.”

“He is named before.”

“I have forgotten him before.”

They worked on that one passage until the lamp needed oil. Jost contributed the fact that the man fetching the replacement timber had fallen into a shallow ditch and arrived furious because everyone thought the mud meant he had taken the wrong path. Vigdis said her father had blamed the sledge before seeing that an old split had been hidden beneath a repair. Svanhild asked which detail belonged to this moment and which could wait.

Eydis argued for a woman who had cooked through the whole operation. Her grandmother said the name alone would not keep her in the listeners’ minds.

“She fed them.”

“How?”

“With soup.”

“When?”

“While they waited for the runner.”

“Then let us smell it while we are waiting.”

Eydis tried. The line was clumsy, and everyone helped make it worse before they found something that sounded like her own mouth. They laughed so much at one version that Runa came upstairs to ask what was happening.

“Soup,” Jost said.

“That explains everything.”

Her mother stayed to listen for a while. Eydis read the revised passage. Runa did not correct the distance or ask for a qualification. She smiled at the ditch and winced at a detail she remembered too well.

For the next attempt they moved downstairs. Eydis said the stair gave her more room for the ship passage; her grandmother said it gave her an excuse to keep the paper above her listeners’ eyes. Jost volunteered to be the sledge. Vigdis said that was the first suitable part he had offered to play.

He lay across two stools and refused to move until somebody brought soup.

Eydis tried to begin over their laughter. Nothing she said could survive the sight of Jost’s boots sticking into the room. At last she threw a rolled cloth at him. He caught it, tucked it under his head and said the delay was getting worse.

“Get up.”

“I have broken a runner.”

“You have broken the account.”

“Then repair me where I happen.”

Even Svanhild laughed at that. Eydis hated them for three breaths and then laughed too, because he had used the words exactly as her grandmother had said them. When they had quieted she asked him to stay still and tried the passage with the sledge visibly stuck. The waiting was easier to hear now. She could say where the people stood because she had to walk around him.

Vigdis interrupted at the repair.

“My father didn’t do that.”

“He said he did.”

“He said he had it done. I held the brace. Solli fetched the other piece.”

Eydis looked at her page. She had written Torve’s name across the whole repair because he had been the person telling her. There was no room to add both names without squeezing them above the line.

“Will you show me?”

Vigdis borrowed the hearth tongs for the brace. Svanhild objected before the demonstration began, so they used a stick. Jost complained when Vigdis pressed it against his shin. She told him the sledge had not made that noise.

They tried it once with Eydis speaking and the others moving, and again with Eydis looking away. The second version failed because she had forgotten the man waiting with the pin. He could not do anything until both ends of the brace were held. Vigdis waited in an uncomfortable position until Eydis remembered him.

“That is why he was swearing,” she said.

“I thought he was angry about the delay.”

“He was angry at his back.”

Eydis put that in.

When her grandmother went to fetch more light, Jost got up from the stools and came to the table. He had stopped smiling.

“You’ve got me calling Osvin.”

“You did.”

“Only after.”

“After what?”

“After you told me twice.”

Eydis remembered him below the window, caught between her calls and the adults around the injured man. In the telling she had made the message run straight from her mouth to Osvin because she wanted to reach the bell. She had also liked giving Jost something useful. They had not quarrelled properly since the alarm, and she did not want to begin now.

“It doesn’t matter.”

“It did then.”

She looked at him. He pulled the loose thread from his cuff and broke it between his fingers.

“You needn’t say I was stupid.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“But don’t make me quick.”

She crossed out a word. Jost looked at the change, then returned to the stools and sat on one instead of lying across both.

Svanhild came back with the lamp. Nobody explained the silence. Eydis started again from the top of the stair, and this time the message took long enough that she felt the old impatience before she reached the rope. She stopped there.

“That’s enough for tonight.”

Her grandmother nodded.

Vigdis helped put away the stools. Jost wanted the cloth he had used as a pillow because he had dropped a shaving from his cuff inside it and knew Svanhild would blame him if she found it later. Eydis shook it over the hearth. Three shavings fell out.

“Only one of those was mine.”

“Then find the other owners.”

He grinned at her, and she let him.

After the others left, Eydis carried her pages upstairs. Runa followed and sat on the edge of the bed.

“I’ve accepted the southern post.”

Eydis stared at her.

“When?”

“Today. The letter will go with Menas’s party.”

“You already said yes?”

“Yes.”

“What about here?”

“The work will continue. Petros stays through the next stage. The clerk can keep the records.”

“I didn’t mean your records.”

Runa looked down at her hands.

“I know.”

Eydis felt the whole room become something she would have to pack. The page on the bed, the marks by the window, the place where she knew how to duck beneath the roof. She had begun to belong to routes and errands nobody explained anymore.

“I want to stay.”

“You can stay through summer, if you and your grandmother want that. Then you come south to me.”

“So you’ve decided that too.”

“We’ve discussed whether it would be possible.”

“Without me.”

“To find out whether it was something we could offer you.”

Runa’s answer was reasonable. Eydis hated it. She wanted the adults to have made no arrangements and every arrangement at once, so that her choice could be free without requiring her to solve the journey.

“Grandmother will be alone.”

“Your grandmother has people here.”

“Not us.”

Her mother’s face tightened. Eydis knew she had struck something and was immediately sorry, but not enough to take it back.

They did not settle the summer that night. Runa kissed her and went downstairs. Eydis lay awake listening to the two women speak quietly below. She could not make out the words and did not go to the stair to hear them.

Chapter 40 The road south

They settled it over several meals, with interruptions for ordinary work and one quarrel about boots.

Eydis would stay until the late-summer passage with a household both women knew. She would continue lessons with Astri. Letters would go by the coastal boats when possible, and she would not be punished for describing unhappiness as well as enjoyment. Runa said this awkwardly enough that Svanhild looked away to spare her. Eydis asked whether she had to write every week. Her mother said no, then named a schedule that amounted to nearly every week when boats were available.

The boots caused trouble because Svanhild believed the pair being made locally would last through autumn, while Runa wanted another allowance kept aside in case the girl grew again. Eydis said she could not promise not to. Both women laughed, and the quarrel lost some of its dignity.

On the morning they finally agreed, Runa took Eydis to the shoemaker herself.

The new boots stood on a shelf beside a much larger pair. Eydis put her feet into them and immediately said they pinched. Runa crouched to feel the place. There was room enough at the toe. The shoemaker asked the girl to walk. She walked as though she had never owned feet before.

“Ordinary steps,” Runa said.

“These are ordinary.”

“They are the steps of a crane stealing eggs.”
