Eydis looked at the shoemaker to see whether he was laughing. He was wisely busy with the old boots. She took three less exaggerated steps and admitted that only one pinched, at the side.

He marked the place. Runa examined the sole while Eydis stood in her stockings. A boatman came in asking for a repair he needed before noon. The shoemaker pointed to the work already on his bench. The boatman looked at Eydis’s boots and said a child could wait until tomorrow.

“I could,” Eydis said.

Runa opened her mouth, then closed it. The girl had answered without looking at her.

The shoemaker told the boatman to leave his repair and come back after eating. He did not put aside the marked boot until he had finished what he was doing to it. Eydis watched him, rocking on her heels.

Outside she said, “I don’t have to ask you everything.”

“No.”

“Even if I could have waited.”

“No.”

Her daughter seemed dissatisfied with two agreements in succession. She kicked a loose piece of grit down the path, followed it, and kicked it again.

“Will Grandmother let me go to the shore with Jost?”

“That depends on the shore, the weather, and what you mean by go.”

“She’ll say that.”

“Then you have practice answering.”

Eydis caught the grit beneath her boot and ground it into the mud. They walked a little way without speaking. At the turn she took Runa’s hand as if she needed help over the uneven ground. She held it after the ground had flattened.

“I don’t know if I’ll like it when you’re gone.”

“I don’t either.”

“What if I don’t?”

“We’ll write. If it is more than missing one another for a bad afternoon, we’ll arrange an earlier passage.”

“How will you know?”

“You’ll have to tell me.”

Eydis considered that. “You ask whether I’m eating.”

“You can tell me before I ask.”

“That’s what you’ll ask first.”

Runa squeezed her hand. “Probably.”

They went home by the longer path. Runa let the work wait through that walk, though twice she began calculating what would have to be done before evening. Eydis pointed out the place where somebody had moved a stone she used to step over. It had been a perfectly ordinary stone. The girl described its old position with enough indignation that Runa had to stop herself laughing.

At home Svanhild was laying out linen. Eydis told her she had decided to stay, then added the conditions about letters and an earlier passage if necessary. Her grandmother listened without finishing the girl’s sentences. When Eydis had gone upstairs, Runa remained by the table.

“You can say if this is too much.”

“I have said what I can manage.”

“She will be difficult sometimes.”

“So will I.”

Runa folded a corner of the linen, unfolded it, and smoothed the crease with her palm.

“I don’t want her to feel she is being left because the work matters more.”

“Does she want to go with you?”

“Not now.”

“Then let her stay.”

Her mother lifted the linen away from Runa’s fingers and folded it properly. Runa disliked being so transparent to her. She also wanted to sit down and let her mother decide the whole summer, which was an equally unwelcome discovery.

“Don’t tell her she is lucky to have the choice.”

“I wasn’t planning to.”

“I know. I only—”

“I heard you.”

They put away the linen. Svanhild made room for Eydis’s things on a lower shelf, shifting objects which had occupied the same places since Runa was a child. Runa watched a little painted bowl move towards the edge and reached to steady it. Her mother thanked her. They finished without quarrelling.

Runa packed gradually. The work papers went first, copied and assigned so nothing necessary travelled south only in her bag. She left a clear record of the remaining allowance and the commitments already made against it. Petros checked the figures, found one transposition, and corrected it without making an occasion of her fallibility.

The circuit clerk came to the chapel for the final handover with a new pen and the expression of someone determined not to need help.

Runa had prepared a list of places in which he would probably need it. She looked at the list, remembered arriving with her own ruled forms, and laid it facedown beneath the accounts.

They began with money already assigned. He checked the figures efficiently, catching a duplicated receipt before she pointed to it. He wanted to combine two small headings. She explained why one held household loans and the other purchased stores. He nodded, kept them separate and moved on. It was going well enough that she became anxious for a different reason: he might manage without knowing half the things she had spent winter learning.

She took him to the cupboard with the restricted interview notes. He knew they were restricted. He asked who could authorize a copy. She gave the agreed process, then said Ivara’s niece must not be discussed through the house’s service affiliation again without speaking to Ivara and the girl.

He asked what had happened. Runa told him, including that she had promised to wait and had not waited. The clerk looked at the cupboard rather than at her while she spoke.

“Is the claim settled?”

“No.”

“Then I’ll need to know if somebody comes.”

“Yes.”

She had wanted him to understand her error. He was already considering the next person through the door. She showed him where Dagrin’s clarification lay and which copy had gone to the uncle. He made a note in his own hand.

Astri came in while they were sorting the remaining paper. She said the school needed the table clear that afternoon. Runa began to offer another room and stopped. The rods had already been moved. The table could be cleared. She and the clerk put the accounts away and wiped the boards while Astri fetched the children’s work.

One of the slates had a drawing of the temporary ferry. Runa recognized it by the lopsided platform and the large bell floating above the boats. She asked who had drawn it. Astri named a younger child, then turned the slate over to show a row of sums.

“He is better at boats.”

“Most boats don’t add correctly either.”

Astri laughed. She set out the counters and moved the little stool Runa had used all winter to the place where a short child could reach the light. Runa found herself watching the stool as though it were a possession somebody had taken without asking.

The clerk went to carry the amended dues to the store. Runa stayed to help with the bench. One fastening remained loose. Astri held it while Runa tightened it with a borrowed tool, and for a moment the two women were kneeling beside one another as they had done over schoolwork years earlier.

“Will you come back?” Astri asked.

“For Eydis, if the passage needs arranging.”

“I meant after.”

“I hope so.”

Astri nodded. “I thought you might.”

Runa wanted to ask whether that had been praise or a forecast. She tightened the fastening again instead. The bench stopped shifting.

“Your daughter listens differently from you,” Astri said.

“She argues sooner.”

“She waits longer.”

Runa looked up. Astri was testing the bench with her weight. The observation had been offered without invitation to an argument, and Runa could not decide whether she had mistaken her child or simply known another part of her. She put the tool back on the table.

Outside, the clerk was already halfway to the store. He stopped a man carrying a bundle, asked a question and was directed along the lower path. Runa almost called to tell him the path was wet. He had eyes. She watched him choose the higher edge and went home to finish packing.

At the yard Torve gave her a small wooden case for her pens. Vigdis had fitted the lid. The hinge moved neatly, and the clasp held without forcing.

“For the papers that don’t get wet,” he said.

“Some of them managed.”

“Use better boats.”

She thanked them. Neither spoke of the grant. She stepped aside while they carried a plank through the doorway.

Oddrun brought a parcel of food and instructions concerning its order of eating. Selka asked Runa to take a copy of her residence entry south, so that it would exist somewhere beyond the fjord. Runa explained what the regional office would and would not do with it. Selka listened, then asked again. This time Runa took it without trying to protect her from wanting the record.

Ivara said goodbye courteously. Her niece’s service dispute remained unresolved, though the girl was back at the house for the present. Runa gave them the name of the clerk keeping the restricted notes and made sure he knew to consult Ivara before releasing another copy.

On the last evening Svanhild cooked too much. Everyone pretended not to notice. Eydis ate little, then became hungry after the bowls were cleared and had to admit it. Her grandmother brought the food back without comment.

Runa went upstairs to finish packing. The small room looked larger with the papers gone and poorer without the objects she had complained there was nowhere to put. She sat on the bed, holding the pen case, until Eydis came in.

“Will I have my own place at the new house?”

“Yes.”

“A room?”

“If the lodging is as described.”

“With a window?”

“Yes.”

Eydis nodded. “Don’t put my books near the jug.”

Runa laughed, then began to cry with so little warning that she was embarrassed before she could stop. Eydis came to sit beside her. The girl put an arm round her, awkward at first, then firmly.

“I am coming,” she said.

“I know.”

“After summer.”

“I know.”

They sat until Runa could speak normally. She did not apologize for crying. Eydis seemed relieved that she did not have to reassure her again.

The morning departure was untidy. One bundle had been tied too large for its allotted place on the cart. Runa could not find the gloves everyone had watched her put down. Svanhild insisted she eat while looking for them. The driver said there was no hurry in a voice indicating there was some.

At last the bundles were settled. Eydis stood beside the wheel, trying to look as though summer were an ordinary interval. Runa embraced her, stepped back to see her face, and embraced her again.

“Write what you want to write,” she said.

“You’ll ask questions.”

“Yes.”

Eydis smiled. “I’ll answer some.”

Svanhild walked beside the cart when it began moving. She had meant to go only to the bend. At the bend she continued. The road carried them past the repaired drain, the drying nets, the place where the old path had required people to lift their loads. It was uneven, unfinished in stretches, and easier than what had been there before.

Runa sat facing sideways so she could speak to her mother. For a while they discussed food. Then the conversation thinned, not because they had settled everything but because the cart was reaching the place where walking back would take longer than Svanhild intended.

Her mother stopped.

“Your coat is caught.”

Runa freed it from the edge of a bundle.

“Thank you.”

Svanhild put a hand on the cart rail. Runa covered it with her own. They stayed like that while the driver tactfully examined a strap.

“Go,” her mother said at last. “You’ll have him waiting all day.”

Runa let go.

From farther along the road she looked back. Svanhild had turned towards home. A worker called to her from the drain, asking something about a name. She stopped to answer, one hand raised against the wind. Runa watched until the turn hid them both.

Coda The summer kept

By late summer Eydis could find the warning mark without being asked, and was tired of being asked.

Her grandmother continued asking anyway. Some days she made the question a game, looking from a place where the white rock was partly hidden or pretending to have chosen the wrong seam. Other days she simply stopped on the path and waited until Eydis pointed. The paid watcher knew them by then. He waved from the little shelter, where a new board kept the worst rain off the place in which a person stood.

Tova took some shifts there and others at the clinic. She complained about both with equal familiarity. When Eydis visited, there was usually smoke from the house. The too-large boots had gone from beside the hearth. She did not ask where.

The summer had contained good days which did not belong in the winter account. She and Jost found a place to swim where the stones warmed quickly in the sun. Vigdis let her help fit a cargo frame, then made her take out a peg driven badly because wanting to help did not make the joint sound. Svanhild taught her a song too improper to repeat at school and was offended when she repeated it at exactly the wrong family supper.

They quarrelled about chores. Eydis said her mother would not make her fetch water after dark. Svanhild wrote to ask whether this was true. Runa’s reply contained an account of several errands her daughter had apparently forgotten. Eydis was furious with both of them, then laughed when her grandmother attempted the dignified retreat of Mistress Venna caught by her sleeve.

The swimming place was less secret than Jost had claimed.

On the first warm afternoon they found three other children there, a woman rinsing cloth and Solli asleep on his back with his boots beside him. Jost said nobody came this early. The woman told him she had come early to avoid boys saying that.

Eydis put her things on a flat stone. The water was clear enough to see where the pale bottom dropped away. She stepped in and stopped, breath caught high in her chest. Jost walked farther with a heroic expression which lasted until a little wave reached his waist.

“It gets better,” he said.

“When?”

“When you stop standing.”

She splashed him. He splashed back. Within moments both were wet enough that the remaining plunge seemed less of a decision. Eydis pushed off, reached the farther stone sooner than she expected, and clung to its warm edge laughing from cold and surprise.

Solli woke to their noise and told them the whole coast could hear. They invited him in. He said he had work later. The woman rinsing cloth said the water had never prevented work yet. He rolled over, hiding his face in one arm.

They swam only a little. Most of the afternoon was spent drying, then deciding they were warm enough to go in again, then finding the water just as cold. Eydis lay on the stone with her hair spread behind her and listened to Jost describe a boat he meant to own. Each time he described it, it gained a piece of equipment. She asked whether there would be room for him among all the things. He said he would stand at the stern, where people could see who was in charge.

“You would fall off.”

“Only if you were steering.”

She kicked water at his feet. The woman lifted her clean cloth out of reach with a warning. They apologized and became quiet for several minutes, long enough to hear a boat passing somewhere beyond the point without seeing it.

When they went home, Eydis was hungry and sleepy and had no important thing to say about the day. Svanhild asked whether she had enjoyed it. She said yes. Her grandmother gave her bread and told her to comb the tangles out before they dried harder. Eydis complained, fetched the comb and sat in the doorway, drawing it slowly through the salty ends.

She wrote about the cold water to Runa. She did not put it in the winter account.

Letters came irregularly. Runa described the new lodging, the window, the woman downstairs who disliked boots on the stair and sang while preparing breakfast. She asked questions about Eydis’s lessons and wrote about mistakes at work without turning them into lessons too. Eydis kept the letters in the pen case Torve had made a smaller copy of for her.

Once she and Svanhild visited Selka, who had taken her residence copy to a dispute over a delivery and returned pleased that no one had asked her husband’s brother to confirm who she was. The paper had not decided the entire matter. It had shortened one part of the conversation. Selka considered that worth a cup of sweetened water and a second telling.

A week before leaving, Eydis went with Tova on the evening watch.

The light lasted so late that she forgot to be tired. They brought food in a covered pot and set it on the shelf beside the window. Tova checked the rope, the mark and the little board on which the relief keeper left anything the next shift needed to know. Most entries concerned ordinary things: a loose hinge, a boat expected after dark, someone borrowing the spare lantern and promising to return it.

Eydis read the board while Tova made a small fire. There was an entry about cattle near the lower path. Another hand had added that they had been fetched.

“Do you get bored?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“What do you do?”

“Watch.”

Eydis grinned. Tova smiled back, then gave her vegetables to cut for the pot. They worked beside the window, looking up often enough that the view became part of the task. From here the main village seemed peaceful even when people there were quarrelling. Eydis could see the roof beneath which she and Svanhild had argued that morning about a shirt packed before it was properly dry.

Tova asked whether she wanted to go south.

“Yes.” Eydis stopped cutting. “And no.”

“That sounds inconvenient.”

“Everyone says I’ll be glad when I get there.”

“You may.”

“Will Grandmother be all right?”

Tova stirred the pot. She did not answer quickly enough for Eydis to believe the question had been dismissed.

“She has been all right and not all right for longer than you’ve been here. You’ll write. She’ll show the letters to people who haven’t asked to see them.”

Eydis laughed. “She won’t show all of them.”

“No. Probably not.”

They ate while a fishing boat moved along the far shore. Tova checked its course, waited until it cleared a difficult point and returned to her bowl. Nothing called for the bell. Later the relief keeper arrived with a repaired lantern and news of an argument about its wick. Tova listened, handed over the watch and took Eydis home.

The girl slept soundly that night. In the morning she unpacked the damp shirt and hung it where Svanhild had told her to put it.

At the final gathering before Eydis’s departure, they performed the winter passage together.

The girl began with the lee cove. She had not been there for the first loads, so she used the details Runa and Hervi had given her, choosing what could be heard without pretending to have stood in every place. Svanhild took the crews and the pledged boats. Eydis returned at the broken runner, where the smell of soup made people laugh because they remembered wanting it.

Vigdis’s fastening appeared when it was needed. Tova’s hands were busy with the injured worker while Osvin came downstairs. Jost ran with a kettle instead of a message and later carried the message too. Orri was named before the timber moved, as a man working beside others, not introduced only as a hand to be hurt.

His wife sat beside him. He listened with his splinted fingers resting on his knee. When the account reached the treatment shelter, she looked down and remained looking down for several lines. Eydis did not make that a reason to stop. She did not seek her approval afterward.

The bell passage was short. Eydis had argued for keeping the hesitation. Svanhild had helped her place it where it could be heard without describing every thought she had endured upstairs. When she reached the eighth, the room waited. She did not hold it longer than it had lasted merely because she could.

Near the end, a man interrupted to correct which boat had carried the last sound sacks.

Svanhild looked towards him. Eydis felt the old tightening in her own stomach.

“Which trip?” her grandmother asked.

He explained. Hervi disagreed about the order but accepted the boat. Svanhild asked one more question, then repeated the changed line and went on. The interruption did not destroy the performance. It did leave a rough place, which Eydis heard each time she thought of it afterward.

People stayed late. There was food and argument, a request for the comic bridegroom, and someone who thought the older version had moved faster. Eydis helped wash bowls. Her grandmother’s voice grew hoarse towards the end of the evening. She drank warm water and let another singer take the room.

Eydis went to the back door for cooler air while the other singer held the room. Her face felt hot. She had been waiting for somebody to tell her which parts had worked and was ashamed of how much she still wanted it after spending months deciding whose account should be heard.

Vigdis found her there.

“The pin was right.”

“Only the pin?”

“The rest too. I noticed the pin.”

Eydis leaned against the doorpost. Inside, the new singer had reached something the audience knew; several voices joined the next line. She wanted to go back in and wanted a few minutes in which nobody expected her to listen well.

“Are you coming in the morning?” she asked.

“To the road?”

“Yes.”

“I have to open the shed. I’ll come if Father is there early.”

“You don’t have to.”

“I know. I’d like to.”

The answer pleased her enough that she stopped trying to make the leave-taking casual. She told Vigdis where they expected to meet the cart. Vigdis repeated the place, then took a little polished wooden toggle from her pocket.

“For your bag. The old fastening is splitting.”

Eydis took it. It was shaped to turn easily through a loop and broad enough not to slip back without being turned. She rubbed it with her thumb.

“Did you make it?”

“Yes.”

“When?”

“While Father was discussing timber with a man who intended to buy none.”

Eydis laughed. Vigdis showed her which end should go through first, though the operation scarcely required instruction. They stood together while the song inside reached its noisy return. The door shook slightly under the force of somebody’s foot keeping time.

Jost came out carrying two bowls and asked why they were hiding. Eydis said they were waiting for him to wash those. He announced that he had washed more bowls than anyone and began counting them until Vigdis took one from him and carried it inside. Eydis followed with the other.

By the time they had finished, Svanhild was sitting near the hearth, too hoarse to compete with the people talking around her. A man asked her to repeat the bride’s line from the goat account. She gestured that she could not. Eydis supplied it before thinking.

The man laughed. Her grandmother smiled at her, then drank another mouthful of warm water. She did not make Eydis continue.

On the way home they walked slowly. Svanhild had a stone in her shoe and would not stop until they reached somewhere dry enough to take it off. Eydis held the parcels while she stood on one foot, leaning against the wall. The stone was very small for the trouble it had caused. Svanhild showed it to her in offended silence and threw it away.

Eydis wanted to ask whether she had done well. Instead she said the correction about the boat had been awkward. Her grandmother nodded.

“Will we change it before next time?”

Another nod. Then, with an effort, Svanhild said, “Before you forget which trip he meant.”

At home they wrote the correction together. Eydis tied the new toggle onto her bag while her grandmother watched. The old fastening went into the box with the other things that might still be useful.

Next morning the travelling household called at the door earlier than expected. Eydis was dressed but had not tied her bag. Svanhild found the missing cord and knelt to help with the final knot. Her hands moved more slowly than they had when the girl arrived, or perhaps Eydis was watching them more carefully.

At the road they said goodbye without agreeing on everything that ought to have been said. Svanhild reminded her to give Runa the parcel and not eat the best part on the journey. Eydis promised with insufficient seriousness and was made to promise again.

She took her place beside the other travellers. The cart began to move.

The chapel bell rang. Its strokes came evenly across the roofs. Eydis counted before she meant to, her hand resting on the side of the cart. At the ninth she looked back.

Svanhild stood on the path. She heard it and said nothing.

Across the fjord the warning bell hung silent, with an attendant beneath it.
